Preface
This book examines the persistent lack of alignment between Kingdom-centered unity and prevailing patterns of church leadership, and how this misalignment has shaped division within the Church throughout biblical history. Particular attention is given to leadership-driven divisions in contemporary churches, with a focused case study on the Togolese Assemblies of God Church (TAGC).
The central proposition of this work is that Jesus’ teaching on the Kingdom of God provides a foundational and unifying framework for ecclesial leadership. This Kingdom-centered vision stands in critical dialogue with Old Testament models of kingship and Pauline perspectives on authority, offering a distinctive paradigm for understanding power, leadership, and unity within the Church.
To establish a strong biblical foundation, the book explores leadership structures in the Old Testament, especially the concept of kingship and its theological implications. These models are then examined alongside the teachings of Christ and the apostolic tradition to assess their influence on contemporary church leadership practices.
Ultimately, this book offers both theological insight and practical guidance for church leaders, particularly within the TAGC, who seek to address leadership conflicts and overcome division. By realigning leadership practices with the Kingdom-centered example demonstrated by Christ, the work presents a pathway toward restoring unity, integrity, and faithfulness within the Church.

Introduction
 
Why This Book Matters
Church leadership has always played a key role in shaping the identity, mission, and unity of God's people. From the Old Testament monarchy to the New Testament apostolic era, leadership structures have greatly influenced the spiritual and social lives of temples, synagogues, houses of worship, and churches. First, in the Old Testament, kings served as God's vice-regents, appointed by Him to lead His people. Edward Lipiński  notes that Israel's leadership shifted from a tribal confederacy to a monarchy. After the monarchy, leadership evolved into a prophetic role. Specifically, during times of crisis, prophets emerged to challenge the established authorities that had failed to provide alternative visions for community-building and the future (see Jer. 21:1–23:8).
Thus, leadership formulations establish the way authority, duties, and decision-making flow within a society. In biblical contexts, these structures outline methods for governing God's people, the delegation of authority, and expectations for leaders to lead by following divine guidance. Although the Old Testament leadership was gradually developed into a kingship, prophets, and a covenantal form of authority, the New Testament proposes a new model that focuses on the teachings and practices of Jesus. New Testament proposes a new model that focuses on the teachings and practices of Jesus.
The Old Testament leadership forms indicate the existence of a monarchy that spans from 1 Samuel to 2 Kings, from Saul to the Babylonian exile (c. 1050–586 BCE).  The same era is characterized by several key elements, including divine appointments (Saul by Samuel in 1 Samuel 10; David in 1 Samuel 16; Solomon in 2 Samuel 7). Leaders served as both political and religious figures, defining this period with centralized authority and a hierarchical structure. The monarchic period further illustrates that kings held the highest positions, serving as supreme leaders and responsible for military leadership, justice, and religious regulations. During this time, kings were charged with upholding the covenant, administering justice, leading in warfare, and overseeing worship.
The same era is characterized by several key elements, including divine appointments (Saul by Samuel in 1 Samuel 10; David in 1 Samuel 16; Solomon in 2 Samuel 7). Leaders served as both political and religious figures, defining this period with centralized authority and a hierarchical structure. The monarchic period further illustrates that kings held the highest positions, serving as supreme leaders and responsible for military leadership, justice, and religious regulations. During this time, kings were charged with upholding the covenant, administering justice, leading in warfare, and overseeing worship.
The monarchic period extends from Saul's anointing to the fall of Judah, marked by varying degrees of success, faithfulness, and failure. This monarchy also included prophetic oversight, in which prophets—unlike kings who held political authority—served as God's messengers and assessed the king's moral authority. For example, Nathan rebuked David (2 Sam. 12), while Samuel (1 Sam. 8:6–9; 10:17–25; 12:1–25; 13:8–14; 15:10–23) and Elijah (1 Kings 17:1; 18:17-18; 21:17-24; 2 Kings 1:1-17) delivered God's will and held kings accountable. Prophets were divinely appointed and had spiritual authority that was not granted or controlled by the monarchy.
Alongside this, the monarchic period is characterized by both successful and unsuccessful kings, such as Jeroboam I (1 Kings 11–14), whose failures led to the creation of golden calves at Dan and Bethel, ultimately causing worship to cease in Jerusalem. Accordingly, today's leaders must avoid Jeroboam's legacy of sin, as he became a symbol of what an evil king represents. They would not want God to say of them that they have done more evil than all who came before (1 Kings 14:9). Current leaders must also never replace God's glory and His presence with anything that could lead to spiritual corruption.
Similarly, Manasseh (2 Kings 21), whose failures also reversed Hezekiah's reforms and led Judah into severe idolatry, including child sacrifice, serves as a strong warning to today's leaders. Manasseh's sin, which resulted in Judah's exile (2 Kings 24:3-4), is a warning to current leaders to avoid sins that could have lasting consequences and anything that could undo progress or bring divine judgment upon entire communities. It is also crucial for modern leaders to realize that their moral and ethical decisions are long-term investments, with consequences that persist after they are made and cannot be undone.
However, Hezekiah (2 Kings 1820; 2 Chron. 29–32) presents a king who willingly expressed his spiritual power in the elimination of idolatry, the restoration of a centralized temple worship in Jerusalem, the rebuilding of the Passover, and his faith in God in the face of the Assyrian menace (2 Kings 19). The Hezekiah example of courage, leadership focused on reform in times of spiritual decay, and the necessity of restoring society to the Word of God and His presence can be studied by modern leaders. They also have an opportunity to learn to value God's commendation (2 Kings 18:5) more than human praise.
In line with this, 2 Kings 22-23 and 2 Chronicles 34-35 depict that Josiah exercises spiritual power by repenting and reinstating covenant faithfulness through reforms founded on scripture and penitence. Leaders today can also emulate Josiah by understanding that revival spiritually is through scripture and seeking the approval of God because the Scripture indicates that there was no king before him or after him who displayed such covenantal devotion (2 Kings 23:25). Like Josiah, the leaders today are supposed to be concerned with purity, biblical teachings, as well as spearhead reforms whenever necessary despite the circumstances being against their favor.
Thus, it was the responsibility of kings to make covenants and submit themselves to God, and it was according to God's standards and how faithfully kings kept the covenant (Deut. 17:14-20). Successful church leaders are constantly to bear in mind that, just as Samuel and Elijah, they have to submit their attentions to godly reality rather than ethnic, racial, local, and institutional allegiance. Moreover, as the biblical leadership model holds leaders responsible for God's values rather than their own self-interest, prophetic leadership is imperative to integrity and cohesiveness. Finally, similarly to Elijah, who scolded Ahab, leaders in the modern world cannot forget that prophetic leadership is motivated by justice and inspired by spiritual discernment.
Furthermore, Scripture evaluates the kings according to their faithfulness to covenants, their obedience to God, and their administration of justice, worship, and treatment of vulnerable people (Deut. 17:14-20). In addition, successful kings, such as David (2 Sam. 5-24; 1 Kings 1-2), have these qualities: profound devotion to God (Ps. 51), a heart of covenant, repentance, and those of a shepherd-king. Nevertheless, David was also weak in leadership, as evidenced by his moral failings toward Bathsheba, Uriah, and his family dysfunction, which revealed both his strengths and his weaknesses in covenant leadership. Modern leadership can find inspiration in David: greatness does not spare them of imperfection; however, when they are swift to repent, as David was, they can restore themselves to the cause of God. The current generation of leaders is also aware that humility, repentance, and confession are great qualities of leadership, not mere images as people often perceive them.
In modern leadership teachings, Ahab is viewed as an evil king (1 Kings 16-22) whose moral decline led Israel into idolatry and injustice. His marriage to Jezebel encouraged Baal worship, persecuted prophets, and resulted in Naboth's murder. Therefore, Ahab stands as a warning about the dangers of external influence, similar to in-grouping and out-grouping, which warn against forming ungodly alliances. Thus, contemporary leaders have the responsibility to ensure they are not compromised spiritually by pride or unhealthy ambitions.
The monarchic era was also characterized by a priesthood centered in the Temple. Specifically under Solomon, priests oversaw worship, sacrifices, and religious instruction; however, over time, the high priest gained influence, and priests often served as subordinates to the king. Along with this, though the kings were the most authoritative, tribal and local leaders still exercised governance and authority at the community level in judicial practices (Ruth 4:1-12; 1 Kings 21). So, during the monarchic era, the hierarchy was clear, with divine authority granted to the king, who exercised it through his appointed officials. To clearly understand the key aspects of biblical leadership development, one must examine the New Testament rewrites and extensions of the Old Testament norms.
In contrast, leadership structures during the New Testament apostolic era, roughly from 30 to 100 CE, marked the early period of the Church and were characterized by a charismatic and collegial leadership style. The New Testament consistently presents Church leadership as a shared responsibility rather than a domain controlled by a single leader. Overall, the New Testament leadership model is shown through elements such as the apostles (the primary leaders, including the Twelve, who are considered eyewitnesses of Christ). The apostles, along with Paul, serve as authoritative teachers (Acts 1:21-22; Gal. 1:1). According to Acts 1:21-22, apostolic power is gained through the experience of Christ, not through theological education. Jesus's earthly ministry also provides historical continuity, affirming apostolic claims, which Paul used to deny human origin or mediation and to challenge his authority in Galatia (Gal. 1:1).
Similarly, when Paul writes phrases such as "not through man" and "but through Jesus Christ and God the Father," he paints the picture of the apostolic calling as anti-human. In contrast, the process of choosing the Twelve is presented as an alternative, and the direct divine calling is confirmed. As such, when Paul asserts that direct revelation is superior to the institution's succession method, it is akin to the divine prerogative of the king in the Old Testament. Consequently, Acts 1 not only likens historical commissioning to the appointment process but also establishes a pattern of the forerunner authority of the apostles through historical contingency and societal selection, as is found in Acts 1:21-22. It provides historical principles of the apostolic succession of the Twelve. By contrast, Galatians 1 reveals that God can call apostles who do not meet those conditions, indicating divine prerogative and the justification of apostolic authority through direct divine revelation and personal vocation.
Resting on these leadership systems, the contemporary institution must be flexible enough to follow the movement of the Spirit, creating a balance between historical continuity and immediate divine sanction, episcopal succession, congregational calling, and institutional ordination, and charismatic gifts. In his view, continuity and witness in Acts 1 and divine power and self-sufficiency in Galatians 1 lend credence to the notion that the resurrection of Christ and the initiative of God, not man-made invention, are the basis of apostleship. They present a complementary apostolic leadership theology for the early Church that offers the contemporary Church perspectives on leadership, authority, authenticity, and mission.
The existing leadership should be based on the apostolic tradition, and their leadership practice is faithful to the life, death, and resurrection of Christ. The contemporary Church needs to have its biblical foundations, the presence of the Spirit, and be mission-oriented to support and openly share the apostolic message. Authentic leadership is not inherited or selected; it is identified by the faithful tradition and the fresh experience with God that led the Church to challenge and embrace charismatic vocations, such as Paul's, without compromising the apostolic doctrine. Consequently, authority is founded on Christ, obedience to the Gospel, personal integrity, and the marks of the calling of God, and not on position, title, degrees, credentials, or ordination. The faithful adherence to the Gospel is the final sense of continuity of the apostles, and therefore, the contemporary Church is supposed to focus on the fidelity of doctrine and Christ-oriented proclamation as principal signs of a successful and effective ministry.
Despite the biblical leadership models that emphasize homogeneity in churches, the current situation of the church, particularly the Pentecostal and Charismatic movements in Africa, is rife with tensions and concerns related to divisions arising from leadership crises. Like most fast-growing denominations, the TAGC is also severely threatened by ethnic and leadership struggles, which undermine ecclesial unity, credibility, spiritual energy, and the efficacy of the mission, terms that need to be defined, but which indicate the necessity of ensuring unity in the church.
Ecclesial unity encompasses both the theological concept and the practical reality of harmony or connection among diverse Christian communities, both locally and globally, that share a common faith in Jesus Christ. It forms a visible body that reflects the unity of the Trinity. Richard Lints describes it as "the union of the people of God, in all their various distinctives and expressions, bound to God and one another. The unity of the Church is a function of the unity of God, which the Church is called to worship."  It differs from humanly fostered unity, which can be based on favoritism, ethnic or cultural loyalties, and fleshly motives such as selfish ambitions, the desire to gain control in relationships, or the quest for self-satisfaction and everyday needs.William H. Radner points out how the anti-slavery movement interpreted Ephesians 2:11-22 to mean equality of all individuals before God, regardless of their backgrounds, rather than the reconciliation of differing groups in Christ.  The abolitionists fought for equality in earthly affairs—equality before men. Their opponents in the pro-slavery era agreed that men are equal before God but argued that this equality pertained to a man's soul rather than his earthly relationships. Without doubt, ecclesial unity is expressed through doctrinal agreement, fellowship, and a shared mission. In other words, the unity of the Christian family is spiritual, similar to the unity of the Godhead. This ecclesial unity involves participation in the divine nature of the triune God, which includes mutual exchange among all members in God's forgiveness, reflected in mutual forgiveness and love for one another.
To further clarify ecclesial unity, it is important to recognize that credibility is confined to an ecclesial or institutional context, reflecting the authenticity, believability, and trustworthiness of the Church as both an organization and a community. This base is pinned on its adherence to divine revelation, instructions, activities, and leaders, its moral uprightness, and robust testimony of the Gospel, all of which contribute to spiritual health. Spiritual vitality refers to the dynamic, life-giving spirit of the Holy Spirit in the Church, manifested in the spiritual well-being of individuals and the whole community. This vitality comes in the form of active faith, an authentic relationship with God and people, personal development, and practical ministry.
Nonetheless, Carlyle Fielding Stewart III is concerned about the dwindling number of mainstream Protestant churches, particularly African American ones. He points out problems such as loss of spiritual passion, disconnection from culture, scriptural illiteracy, lack of holiness, and absence of true worship  This is because, according to him, what is really needed is a revival of strong spiritual passion. He then presents the solutions, which include personal transformation, thorough Bible reading, cultural worship, new evangelism, and empowered leadership. Stewart III calls mainline churches to go back to their spiritual origins in pursuit of true faith and robust leadership that can see them through the trials of the modern world. The transformation of both the individual and the community is brought by spiritual vitality, which can be described as being vibrant and authentic in their rapport with Christ, the Word, and the Church of God. 
Besides that, the Church also has the duty of fulfilling its missional mandate through the pursuit of ecclesial unity. In this study, the term "missional mandate" refers to the effectiveness of churches or Christian communities in fulfilling their calling to make disciples and participate in the mission of God (Missio Dei). This mission involves proclaiming and sharing the Gospel, making disciples, and serving the poor and marginalized. It includes engaging culture in a redemptive way in line with the Great Commission (Matt. 28:18-20) and the Great Commandment (Matt. 22:37-40). Alan Hirsch, Christopher J. H. Wright, Darrell L. Guder, Lesslie Newbigin, and Mark Dever encourage churches to be mission-minded, emphasizing that the true purpose and identity of the Church go beyond merely having a missions department; they involve actively participating in the mission.  Missional mandate effectiveness refers to the extent to which the Church integrates worship, discipleship, outreach, social justice, and community engagement in a coherent, transformational manner that reflects the Kingdom of God.
Based on this background, it is essential to note that internal tensions and divisions in the contemporary Church are often fueled by in-group versus out-group exclusions and interests.  	 Therefore, He established service, not domination, as the ethical framework for leadership. To quote John Bright,  Jesus's teachings challenged Greco-Roman imperial ideas of leadership and greatness, where power was celebrated through domination and authority was expressed through supremacy. Israel progressed from a weak tribal alliance (during the time of the Judges) into a centralized monarchy under Saul, despite prophetic warnings, following a request for a king (1 Sam. 8). Monarchy was both a response to Israel's demand for a king and part of God's plan that reflects the Deuteronomic tension.
Furthermore, Jesus's example serves as a radical challenge to His ministry (Matt. 19:30; 20:16; Mark 10:31; Luke 13:30). All these passages include variations of the proverbial saying: "But many who are first will be last, and many who are last first will be last," (Πολλοὶ δὲ ἔσονται πρῶτοι ἔσχατοι, καὶ ἔσχατοι πρῶτοι). The saying echoes Luke 14:11 and 18:14. Joachim Jeremias indicates that it represents one of Jesus's most distinctive and characteristic elements, along with challenging teachings about the nature of God's Kingdom.  I. Marshall Howard  highlights that Luke's formulation is unique, using present-tense verbs (εἰσὶν) alongside future forms (ἔσονται), suggesting both a present reality and eschatological fulfillment. Each text underlines and converges around the exact reversal of human expectations in the coming Kingdom, as Jesus subverts hierarchical concepts of societal and spiritual status, seniority, and merit-based worldviews. Craig Keener, however, points out that the parable does not advocate idleness. It dismantles presumptuous self-righteousness, representing the pitfalls of ecclesial leadership.  In Matthew 19:30 and 20:16, the rich young ruler and Jesus's disciples stand astounded at the difficulty of entering the Kingdom. At the same time, Peter questions Him about rewards (Matt. 19:27), prompting Jesus to assure the disciples of the eschatological blessings (v. 30), which are further elaborated in the parable of the vineyard workers (Matt. 20:1-15). Specifically, in Matthew 20, the reversal theme underscores God's freedom to distribute grace and reward. The analysis of the parable of the workers in the vineyard (Matt. 20:1-16) reveals that laborers are hired at different times but receive equal pay. Jesus introduces the concept of the Kingdom, a grace-based idea that challenges conventional notions of merit, reward, and human fairness, warning that divine grace transcends human hierarchical expectations and the concept of merit.
In Matthew 20:25-28, James and John seek positions of honor through their mother. However, in Mark 10:42-45, they pursue them directly, prompting Jesus's correction by contrasting worldly power structures with the values of the Kingdom. The former "lord over" or "lord against" (κατακυριεύουσιν) emphasizes overpowering, subjugation, control, and the exercise of authority. In contrast, the latter emphasizes practical, humble service and directly links servanthood to Jesus's redemptive mission, portraying service as more than just an ethical act, but a soteriological one. Leadership in the Kingdom reverses worldly expectations.
Additionally, Jesus's many paradoxical statements demonstrate how His Kingdom challenges traditional social hierarchies, and underscores their reversal. He criticized religious leaders who often sought status and recognition, using a child (Luke 9:46-48), one with little social standing in the ancient world, to illustrate the dramatic reversal of status that defines His Kingdom's vision. The social reversals depicted in the teachings of Jesus are also evident in his ethical teachings, and when he uplifted the women, the poor, and the ill as vital parts of God's Kingdom.  Jesus prayed for unity (John 17:20-23), reflecting a theological ethos that challenges power-driven or status-based approaches to leadership. When the Church lives and works together, it shows the world, divided as it is, the truth of Christ's mission and love, and strengthens the Church’s witness to the world.  
Jesus also consistently emphasized servant leadership, sacrificial love, humility, mutual respect, and submission as the foundation of Church harmony (Matt. 7:12; 20:25-28; 23:11-12; 
Mark 10:45; Luke 22:42; John 13:12-17; 13:34-35; 15:12-13; 17:20-23; Phil. 2:5-8). The selected passages collectively call the Church to reflect Christ's servant leadership, humility, sacrificial love, and unity. They reveal an ongoing theological development from ethical insight (Matt. 7:12) to Christological foundation (Phil. 2:5-8), demonstrating that Christian servanthood is fundamentally participation in Christ. Furthermore, the selected passages invite the community of believers to reflect on Christ's cruciform prototype by embodying His love and unity in a fragmented world.
Matthew 7:12 affirms a relational ethic of mutuality and presents the Golden Rule as a fundamental principle for any church leadership. Craig Blomberg and Ulrich Luz assert that the maxim is a theological statement about the connection between divine and human love. In its positive form, it challenges the disciple to be proactive, not just reactive, in their actions of goodness.  Not only that, the passage establishes the hermeneutical principle that underpins all Christian leadership ethics, serving as an expression of Kingdom righteousness rooted in God's covenantal expectations (Lev. 19:18).
In Matthew 20:25-28 and Mark 10:45, Jesus responds to the disciples' desire for greatness by redefining leadership, sharply contrasting with the Greco-Roman honor culture that equated greatness with supremacy (cf. Mark 10:42). Jesus explains that in the Kingdom economy, greatness is not measured by domination or the exercise of coercive authority "κατακυριεύουσιν," or oppressive domination, status, control, or titles, but by self-giving service "διάκονος" grounded in "δοῦλος" and sacrificial love "λύτρον." The phrase "λύτρον" is a theological metaphor for atonement, referring to a payment made to free slaves or captives. This concept is especially significant, as noted by James R. Edwards, who observes that Mark 10:45 serves as the theological key to Mark's Gospel, Jesus's path to the cross, and the way of true discipleship and leadership.  Therefore, Matthew 20:25-28 and Mark 10:45 reveal that Christlike leadership is fundamentally kenotic, other-centered, and sacrificial, and teaching contemporary leaders that biblical leadership calls for a radical realignment with the crucified and risen Christ's values.
Jesus has shown that service is his definition of authentic leadership through his teaching and selfless ministry. He confirmed this tendency when He taught, "The Son of Man did not come to be served but to serve, and to give His life as a ransom for many" (Mark 10:45). Consequently, Jesus exhibits a unique type of Christ-centred leadership by showing that in God's Kingdom economy, authentic and compelling leadership is not based on power, privilege, fame, honor, or control, but on sacrificial and selfless service, as well as redemptive and self-emptying love. 
Agreeing with this idea, Joel Green affirms, "Jesus's self-giving represents the pattern to be replicated by His followers in community life."  Therefore, the broader context of Mark 10:42-45, where Jesus explicitly contrasts His revolutionary servant-leadership model with the Gentile who "lords it over" their subjects, emphasizes that atonement and humility are the foundations of leadership. Consequently, the understanding of the Christian leadership held by Jesus is closely related to the theological fact of atonement and the practicality of humility. It demonstrates that the flesh work of His redemption is the fundamental foundation of all justifiable spiritual power.
Matthew 23: 11-12 denounces religious elitism and reiterates the difference between self-glorification and Godly glory, exposing that the greatness in the Kingdom lies in service. True spiritual power is ironically shown when a person exhibits humility due to the divine reversal theme (see Mat 19: 30). Keener points out that the passive voice in will be "humbled or exalted" reflects divine activity in establishing hierarchy, which is much like James 4:6, which emphasizes the biblical motif of divine reversal where God opposes the proud but rewards the lowly.   Therefore, modern leaders should never lose sight of the central message of Matthew 23:11-12: that spiritual greatness is associated with humility and service. Consequently, they have to renounce pride (1 Pet. 5:5-6) and seek humility as a prerequisite of unity, conflict resolution, and peace.
Luke 22:42 shows voluntary submission, not passive and fatalistic, but active in line with the divine mission. This is one of the significant choices all disciples encounter when they experience suffering. It is a Christological guide on servant obedience (Phil. 2:8). The same term, "let it be done," echoes Luke 1:28 and provides a thematic connection between the submissiveness to the redemptive purpose of God, and at the same time, the idea of incarnational obedience is the focus of all Christian service. 
John 13:12-17 falls within the Johannine development of love as divine participation, illustrating a progression from "example" to theological principle (v. 16). Raymond E. Brown observes, "The gesture reorients the entire structure of leadership toward mutual service."  The foot-washing, which is the symbolic subversion of hierarchical standards, is not just another illustration, but the support of the symbolic act of self-emptying service of Jesus, and promotes servant leadership as a Christian community standard. Jesus also prays for oneness among His followers, grounding their oneness in the unity between the Father and the Son. The underlying unity here is both spiritual and missional, resulting in the Church's rapid growth, health, and identity. However, Scholars have perceived various problematic realities that accompany the rapid development of human or church demographics. Undeniably, the lived experience of Christians and communities in general, or Christians in particular, has not always been easy. Working and living in heterogeneous families, institutions, congregations, etc., may lead to conflicts and feelings of incompleteness.
Nevertheless, Jesus prays, "That all of them may be one, Father, just as you are in me and I am with you. May they also be in us so that the world may believe that you have sent me" (John  17:21). The Greek word for "one" (εἷς, heis) conveys the idea of agreement, sameness, or unity. According to Donald A. Carson, Jesus' petition to His disciples is characterized as a request whose meaning is explained in the subsequent lines. It is not simply a "unity of love" but a unity predicated on adherence to the revelation the Father mediated to the first disciples through His Son, the revelation they accepted (vv. 6, 8) and then passed on ('those who will believe in Jesus through their message,' v. 20).  Jesus's prayer emphasizes ecclesial unity as central to the mission of the Church, even though ethnic and leadership-driven divisions have often stained the practical outworking of this unity.
John 17 emphasizes unity, serving as a missional strategy for the Church across all ages. More importantly, it highlights participation in the Trinitarian relational unity of the Father, Son, and Holy Spirit. The mutual indwelling, love, and cohesive redemptive work inspire believers to share in this divine unity, reflecting God's relational nature—which exceeds human understanding—and revealing the intimate relationship within the Trinity. Equally important, the Holy Spirit's presence within believers empowers them to live in harmony and demonstrate love, respect, patience, mutual acceptance, and forgiveness—traits that mirror God's nature and foster unity. 
Ecclesial divisions often lead to schisms, leadership disputes, and compromises, which weaken the effectiveness of the witness. As Johnson Asamoah Gyadu notes, "Pentecostal leadership conflicts become specifically difficult to address because they are often spiritualized through claims of divine endorsement, with opposing factions' claims of spiritual legitimation from each involved party."  Within the TAGC, the causes of misunderstandings and disunity include excessive loyalty to one's ethnicity or culture, polarization, otherness, exclusion, in-group preferences, and the forming of out-groups.
Ogbu Kalu rightly observes, "Leadership conflicts in African Pentecostalism often involve unresolved tensions between charismatic authority and institutional power, between indigenous models and imported structures, and between competing theological visions of church governance."  Along with Kalu, W. Vanderbloemen, W. Bird, and A. Homer advocate transferring authority, power, and influence from one leader to the next. At the same time, Vanderbloemen and Bird affirm that, regardless of how talented, gifted, or spiritual someone may be, "every pastor is an interim pastor."  Alongside Weese and Crabtree, B. Russel & Bucher, Vanderbloemen, Bird, A. Homer, and J. Ozier and Griffith use biblical characters as models of succession. They share the belief that leadership practice, development, and succession are usually a point of intense conflict in African secular and spiritual settings.  As Kalu points out, in the TAGC context, certain aspects of divisions, such as regionalism and ethnic in-group preferences, have manifested with notable intensity, particularly in matters of regional loyalty and ethnic preference. Such challenges have the potential to hinder both the spiritual and the numerical growth of the church. However, if contemporary leaders remember that any pursuit of a leadership position will ultimately be passing, they should seek lasting Kingdom roles rather than temporary earthly ones. 
That is, unless these issues are adequately addressed, they jeopardize the unity of the Church, its spiritual well-being, and its public testimony. Jesus not only declared this vision of a Kingdom, but also modeled it in his ministry. Other scholars uphold the countercultural idea of greatness by Jesus. As such, any attempt to achieve any unity in a multiethnic church community will be significantly impeded if Kingdom ethics are not observed. Herein, a critical reconsideration of leadership models and the theological pillars of church unity is paramount, primarily grounded in the teachings and ministry of Jesus, which serve as the theological reference for assessing and revising modern leadership models.
Overall, Matthew 20:25-28, Mark 10:45, and John 13:12-17 reaffirm servant leadership, in contrast to power and dominance, as Jesus redefines greatness through his leadership approach and challenges his disciples to serve others. Jesus and Paul also emphasize humility as the cornerstone of leadership (Matt. 23:11-12, Luke 22:42, and Phil. 2:5-8), since they valued humility over privilege. Jesus is an example of humility through His submission to the will of His Father (Luke 22:42) and His humility in the incarnation (Phil. 2), demonstrating the humility that authentic leadership must possess. John 13:34-35 and 15:12-13 emphasize practical, sacrificial, self-giving, and unconditional love as the hallmark of community, while Matthew 7:12 advocates for the Golden Rule, which captures Jesus's core ethical teaching of reciprocity and promotes relational justice and mutual respect. 
The motivation for this research stems from both theological study and the urgent practical need to address this ongoing issue within the Church. The book offers an evidence-based, scholarly, and ministry-applicable analysis of Jesus's Kingdom vision,  Church unity and leadership models, compared with the Old Testament and Pauline frameworks. The aim is to deliver a biblically grounded and contextually applicable response to ethnic and leadership-driven divisions in the TAGC. Although these have been enriched biblical backgrounds, in the modern church setting, leadership-induced tensions persist, which this paper aims to address.
Problem Statement
Even though church leadership is theologically supposed to embody the power of Christ, the TAGC has been experiencing prolonged and numerous leadership issues that take different forms, such as regionalism, ethnic disputes over church roles, factionalism, and differing visions for mission priorities and governance. Despite the Church's Pentecostal commitment to biblical authority and Spirit-led ministry, a notable gap appears to exist between professed theological beliefs and actual leadership practices. Ethnic and leadership-driven divisions present a complex challenge with theological, historical, cultural, and organizational dimensions, manifesting in different ways that include:
First, conflicts emerge between national, regional, and local leadership structures. Ethnic and leadership-driven disputes among TAGC national, regional, and local leaders constitute a broader form of tension that is also present in most other Pentecostal movements, as research on African charismatic governance reports. The overall organizational framework of the Assemblies of God follows the Presbyterian polity model, as outlined by David J. Bosch, Paul G. Hiebert, Culbert, and Stephen Neill. This model combines multiple levels of leadership, allowing local churches independence while providing oversight to regional and national councils.  Such an organizational framework can potentially create tensions.
Second, ethnic and regional factionalism is expressed through leadership contests. 
Togo has two major ethnic groups: the Ewe in the south and the Kabyiè in the north. These ethnic dynamics tend to shape expectations and decision-making regarding leadership in the TAGC.
Third, disputes on leadership choice, succession, and authority. The research examines the internal wars of the TAGC by discussing tensions over the selection of new leaders, the leadership succession mechanism, and conflicts over the limits of pastoral authority at the local, regional, and national levels. The significant issues are the debate over whether a leader should be elected by the majority ethnic group, spiritual issues, and succession, in which aging pastors who are regarded as knowledgeable and experienced would be replaced by new leadership. It will examine the role of ethnic, generational, and in-group preferences in controversial elections, factionalism, and ethnic suspicion.
This is a burning point because African Pentecostal churches, including the TAGC, are forced to walk the tightrope between Western missional structures and local spirituality and ethnic demands. The results provided insight into broader questions regarding ecclesial power, institutional control, and conflict management in the growing charismatic churches of West Africa. The book also contains some great practical theological lessons and insights into how spirituality and organizational leadership dynamics relate.
Fourth, rival definitions of leadership models and practices. This part examines the competing demands of ethnic rule and the various approaches and definitions of leadership, which often lead to internal contradictions and instability. It explores how these aspects shape perceptions of biblical leadership, cultural practices, legacies of past missionary hierarchies, and socio-political dynamics. It focuses on how these conflicting interpretations and expectations create divisions within the Church and examines national and regional church communications to determine how divergent interpretations shape leadership expectations and practices, resulting in division, mistrust, and disputed legitimacy. In this paper, the author presents the thesis that it is necessary to develop a contextualized, biblically based model of leadership integrating the servant kingship of Jesus, shared leadership of Pauline leadership, and ecclesially relevant indigenization to facilitate spiritual growth and unity within biblical, ethnic, regional, political, missional, and ecclesial systems. The inherited forms of the Church determine these tensions. The missionary-hierarchical model is also characterized by high centralized control, top-down authority, including that of the General Superintendent, and structural leadership in regions.
Conversely, the servant leadership model is rooted in Jesus's teachings and ministry style (Mark 10:42-45; John 13), and Pauline shared leadership (1 Cor. 3-4; 1 Thess. 2), emphasizing humility, mutual submission, acceptance, and spiritual maturity. Concerning ethno-national or regional governance models, socio-political dynamics influence immature leaders through in-group preferences and exclusivity. Ultimately, the charismatic leadership model emphasizes the importance of spirituality, accountability, inclusion, and maturity, addressing ethnic and leadership-driven divisions.
According to Michael Ayers, in similar church settings, past efforts to resolve these conflicts often relied on management principles or approaches grounded in cultural and ethnic resolution methods, without adequately engaging biblical teachings and theology.  Ayers further points out that leadership has been approached based on accomplishing important tasks, social skills, emotional intelligence, leadership behaviors such as individualized consideration, intellectual stimulation, inspirational motivation, and idealized influence, or the skill to embrace chaos and ambiguity.  Leadership is not about doing or completing tasks that can lead to conflicts, misunderstandings, and vision infighting, but about being, which involves one's character and leads to acceptance, love, respect, and unity. 
B. Meyer further notes, "Leadership conflicts frequently manifest as theological disputes while fundamentally involving contested cultural models of authority and legitimation."  This underscores the need for approaches that integrate biblical theology and cultural anthropology to address ethnic and leadership-driven divisions. These divisions have led to leadership resignations, church splits, anonymous letters, leadership disagreements, weakened evangelistic effectiveness, and compromised witness within Togolese society.
Central to this book is the hypothesis that leadership and ethnic-driven divisions within the TAGC context partly arise from the selective use of biblical leadership models that highlight hierarchical authority but do not adequately incorporate Jesus's kingdom-focused vision of servant leadership. This research employs a multi-method approach that combines in-depth biblical-theological analysis with other relevant methods. The leadership and ethnic crisis, which continues to destabilize the TAGC, serves as the main issue, and a biblically based framework is needed to address this challenge.
Purpose Statement
The primary objective of this study is to examine Jesus's attitude and the organization of church leadership as expressed by him through Old Testament and Pauline leadership, in comparison with church leadership in the New Testament. First, the study aims to identify the key theological principles underlying Jesus's model of leadership and unity. Second, it evaluates these principles alongside Old Testament kingship, prophetic leadership, and Pauline models of church governance. Third, it discusses the impacts of these biblical systems of leadership in the TAGC leadership structure. Fourth, it proposes a contextualized model of Christ-centered leadership that not only minimizes divisions but also encourages unity. 
To support this purpose, the study acknowledges that Jesus's radical "upside-down" Kingdom perspective offers a revolutionary paradigm based on servanthood, rejection of domination, and self-sacrifice. Pauline theology, through concepts such as the Body of Christ and shared spiritual gifts, provides a practical approach to church governance that upholds Jesus's ethos of servant leadership. Therefore, a theological reflection grounded in critical exegesis (1 Sam. 8:1, 11-17, 19-20; Matt. 20:25-28; Luke 22:25-26; 1 Cor. 12:27-28; Eph. 4:11-12) will help identify enduring principles that transcend their original contexts. 
Narrative-driven
This book primarily explores Jesus's life, ministry, teachings, and practices related to church leadership and unity as depicted in the Gospels. It also draws on the kingdom of the Old Testament and Pauline teachings about organizing the church, using Jesus as the model. The paper analytically examines the theological, ethical, and practical dimensions of these structures to assess their applicability to the present-day disunity in the Church, particularly as it pertains to the TAGC. The study seeks to provide a comprehensive set of insights that address all three biblical approaches to theology, establishing a sound Christian leadership framework that respects God, upholds the biblical mandate and Pentecostal peculiarities, and enhances the mission of the Church and promotes unity and co-leadership through an analysis of the theological methodology.
The study provides a comparative theological study of leadership and unity within three biblical systems: the Old Testament kingship, the Pauline apostolic leadership, and the servant-kingship of Jesus. Thus, it is within this scope to carry out, first, an exegesis and theological interpretation of biblical texts relevant to leadership and unity principles in the church. Second, a comparison of Old Testament model leaders like Moses, David (in his prime), and Nehemiah, with less model leaders like Saul, Solomon (in his downfall), and Rehoboam, in terms of their theological, leadership, and implications for the life of the covenantal community. 
The analysis will focus on Jesus's teaching and practice regarding leadership and unity, with specific reference to the Gospels (e.g., Matt. 20:25-28; John 17), and their application to the life and governance of the Church. The theology of Pauline leadership will also be addressed, namely in the Pastoral Epistles and the Corinthian and Ephesian letters, with special focus on unity, authority, and charismatic order. Nevertheless, the study also examines other distinguished and selected leaders who did not model themselves on Christlike leadership, such as the Pharisees, Sadducees, and Diotrephes, based on the main observations. Contextually relevant applications that bridge biblical principles with the realities of African Pentecostal life will be produced by exploring these Pauline teachings on ecclesial governance, unity, and servant leadership, in comparison with other insights. Thus, the paper will evaluate how these models address, or fail to address, ethnic and leadership-driven divisions and provide practical implications of the TAGC.
Relevance and Significance of the Study
This book suggests additions to the existing theological and ecclesiological literature by critically examining disagreements between institutional or hierarchical theories of leadership and the paradigm of leadership illustrated by Jesus. It examines the Pentecostal perspective on Church administration and compares it with the leadership principles Jesus taught. Furthermore, the significance of this work is justified by its aim to solve the pressing leadership issues the TAGC has. Additionally, the research offers practical recommendations for addressing leadership development problems and conflict resolution within the TAGC and other African churches facing leadership and ethnic challenges, as witnessed in the church. The book offers additional contributions by hinting at broader debates on ecclesial government and Pentecostal theology, and suggesting ways to foster unity in multiethnic church environments.
Research Methodology
The book uses a qualitative research design to review reported leadership disputes in the TAGC between 2010 and 2024, with a primary emphasis on Ewe in the South and Kabyiè in the North of Togo. It also takes a historical and canonical approach to this investigation. The Old Testament history, what Jesus taught, and what Paul discussed about the Kingdom of God are examined in the primary biblical texts as one means of evaluating the principles of leadership and unity. This approach examines how kingship, governance in early churches, and ecclesiastical battles developed throughout history, to learn how leadership models have been altered. The theological and ecclesiastical study will systematically test and compare the leadership paradigm of Jesus with the Old Testament and Pauline views to identify the continuities and differences. The selection of the biblical texts employed in the study, including Matthew 6:33, Luke 17:20-21, and John 17:20-23, relies on their thematic and theological significance to the core goal of the research.
The writings of note are thematically significant in that they showcase essential features of the Kingdom message of Jesus. Matthew 6:33 reaffirms the value of the Kingdom, whereby people should change their approach to leadership to the authority of God. Luke 17:20-21 explains the Kingdom not as a visible political kingdom but as an existent spiritual kingdom, in which leadership assumes a new perspective as one of relationship of service rather than dominance. In John 17:20-23, the high priestly prayer of Jesus emphasizes unity as both a divine purpose and a leadership value grounded in Trinitarian fellowship. 
The selected texts are central to the Gospels and are widely acknowledged by scholars such as Nicholas T. Wright,  E. 
P. Sanders,  and Scot McKnight  as crucial to understanding Jesus's vision of the kingdom. They therefore serve as theological points for reading more about how Jesus has redefined leadership, especially in comparison to Old Testament monarchy, as well as the issues of the Pauline community. They serve hermeneutically by their inclusion. Matthew 6: 33 criticizes the self-serving aspect of leadership institutions evident in texts such as 1 Samuel 8. Luke 17 re-arranges the hopes of exterior domination into internal change, in contrast to Deuteronomic models of the kingship. The character of John 17 is intended to counter-narrate divisions among early Christian communities, such as the one in 1 Corinthians.
Therefore, the choice of these passages is grounded in a logical, theological, and exegetical argument that aligns with the research's purpose, emphasizing the Kingdom-focused model of leadership and the unity of Jesus. Its criteria are not random but are deliberately based on their weight in the doctrines, the position of their narrations, and their ability to address the issues of leadership and unity in modern ecclesial situations. This methodological approach examines the use of biblical leadership principles in the TAGC setting. 
Assumptions
The following assumptions are foundational:
Theological Assumptions
Biblical or Scriptural Authority: The Bible, the Old and New Testaments as written in canonical Scripture, is considered the foundational, inspired, and authoritative Word of God. It offers the main guideline towards interpreting the unity and leadership of the church.
Relevance of Jesus's Teachings: Jesus's teachings on unity and leadership remain relevant and offer guidance for today's church settings, particularly within the TAGC.
Historical Reliability or Comparative Value: The study presupposes that the Gospel accounts are the most reliable sources of information about the teachings of Jesus, and that different methods of interpretation can yield different interpretations of texts. Consequently, comparing Jesus's perspectives with those of the Old Testament and Pauline doctrine will help alleviate ethnic and leadership-based divisions. 
Theological Continuity: This study argues that Jesus's teachings build upon and sometimes reinterpret Old Testament leadership models, all while maintaining consistency with God's redemptive purposes. 
Ecclesiology: This study maintains that Jesus's teachings on church unity and leadership serve as a model for contemporary Christian communities, including the TAGC. 

Cultural and Contextual Assumptions
The Dynamics of Cultural Context: This work accounts for the role of cultural undertones, as the TAGC exists in a specific cultural environment that shapes its leadership and vision of unity, even as leadership issues in the TAGC follow universal human patterns. Research also suggests that African Christianity, especially in Togolese Pentecostalism, can highlight aspects of biblical leadership in ways different from Western Christianity. It presents a contextualized theology that blends biblical values with African social and cultural ethics, such as models of kingship leadership and respect for the elderly.
Leadership Impact: Leadership practices play a substantial role in the cohesiveness or fragmentation among local, regional, and national leaders at the community and church group levels.
Denominational Framework: There is an assumption that, despite the similarities evident worldwide, the TAGC denominational structure has acquired distinctive attributes that shape perceptions of leadership and unity. 
Socioeconomic Factors: In this paper, regional and social inequalities in Togo, as well as between TAGC leaders and local churches, are assumed to influence leadership processes and help create schisms.
Language and Translation: The study presupposes that the original Hebrew, Aramaic, and Greek sources of the Bible allow for a more sophisticated interpretation of key theological, leadership, and unity ideas than most contemporary translations of the Bible. This involves translations into French and other Togolese dialects, such as Ewe, Mina, Moba, and Kabyiè, which may have better translated the key meaning of the original texts. These local languages can affect the reception and interpretation of biblical texts on leadership and unity, potentially altering the original meaning due to linguistic and idiomatic variations. Thus, there is a presumption that any translation of Scripture is inherently biased in culture and interpretation. The specific focus is on the translation and interpretation of terms related to leadership and unity, such as diakonos, koinonia, and basileia, in the Togolese and African Pentecostal context.
Methodological Assumptions
Comparative Methodology: It is based on the premise that a comparative analysis of Jesus's perspectives in the Old Testament and Pauline ecclesiology would shed light on contemporary ecclesiological issues. It also views that an awakening of Old Testament kingship of Jesus, as well as Pauline ecclesiology, a comparative approach to theology is a rigorous scholarly methodology of interpreting the biblical vision of leadership and its application to ecclesial cohesion. 
Scripture Interprets Scripture: It is a method that presupposes the internal consistency of Scripture and views the writings of the Gospels, the Old Testament, and the Pauline Epistles as capable of engaging in fruitful dialogue with one another. It also assumes the continuity and development of theological aspects throughout the Bible; thus, the interpretive scheme involves historical-critical, literary, and contextual approaches to biblical interpretation that will yield sound results.
Contextual Theology is Key to Practical Relevance: The paper presupposes that theology must be perceived and put to practice in particular cultural and church contexts; therefore, the significance of examining the issues of leadership crisis in TAGC via Scripture and culture. Consequently, indigenous hermeneutics presupposes that African Christians do not merely apply the Bible to indigenous worldviews, which tend to prioritize relational harmony, spiritual power relations, and community over individual identity, i.e., aspects that shape the perception and experience of unity and leadership.
Theological Normativity of Jesus's Teachings: It is thought that a special theological authority of Jesus is exercised in his teachings, life, ministry, and example as the incarnation of the Word of God (John 1:14; Heb. 1:1-2) and as the normative standard of Christian leadership and cohesion, as opposed both to the Old Testament and to the Pauline perspectives that need critical review.
Research Implications and Theological Foundation: In this study, Jesus's instruction on the unity of the church and its leadership is assumed to carry divine authority that prevails over human tradition, cultural life, or other institutional preferences. Thus, according to the testimony that He is both fully God and fully human, His teachings not only reflect human intelligence but also divine revelation of what the Kingdom of God should be and of God's leadership principles.
Delimitations
Cultural bias is identified as one potential limitation, acknowledged given the researcher's cultural background and training in theology. The researcher also admits that translation presents challenges, noting that translation between languages might not fully capture the nuances of the biblical texts. That is why the study warns against overgeneralization, as their results may not be generalizable to Assemblies of God churches in other African countries or other cultures.
Geographical Scope: It focuses on the TAGC in Togo, in the Northern and Southern parts, where the preferences of the leadership in-group are most evident. The research focuses on the Assemblies of God rather than other Christian churches. It particularly examines documented leadership disagreements during 2010-24, with the Ewe in the South and the Kabyiè in the North of Togo.
Comparative Denominational Analysis: This paper does not compare the leadership of the Assemblies of God with that of other Pentecostal denominations in Togo or with any other church established in Togo. Also, the study fails to explore worship styles, liturgical practices, and theological issues that do not directly relate to church leadership and cohesion.
Exclusions: This study does not encompass a thorough political inquiry, though it recognizes how broader Togolese politics influence the church's leadership. Besides, this work is not devoted to gender issues, despite an understanding of the significance of gender in leadership discussions. Moreover, despite the above evidence of the significance of gender in the leadership context, the work does not emphasize it. Moreover, despite considering the economic factors related to leadership, economic research on church finances or pastoral compensation remains underdeveloped. This research does not include quantitative surveys of church members or case studies analyzing conflicts that arise in the field of leadership within the TAGC. Moreover, a biblical survey of all passages about leadership, exegetical studies of whole books, and comparative studies with other New Testament leadership models, e.g., Petrine or Johannine teachings, are also not included exhaustively.
Definition of Terms
Several key terms require definition to establish conceptual clarity.
The Church (capitalized) refers to the universal Church, often called the "Body of Christ," which encompasses all believers in Christ throughout time and space. This differentiation adheres to typical ecclesiological usage. It follows that the term church (lowercase) represents a local congregation or specific group of believers who gather for worship, teaching, fellowship, and ministry.
Ecclesial Unity Versus Disunity: Therefore, the ideas of unity and disunity play a key role in discussions in both the secular world and the church community. In these debates, various definitions are offered to clarify the meaning of these concepts.
The presence of disagreement, dissension, dissonance, misunderstanding, and conflict within a group often characterizes disunity. The Collins COBUILD English Language 
The dictionary defines disunity as "the lack of agreement among people which prevents them from working together effectively."  That absence of agreement can occur in the physical, social, or spiritual realms, demonstrating that those who were once divided shared the same intentions.
Synonyms of the term disunity include conflict, discord, discordance, disharmony, dissension, (also dissension), dissent, dissidence, dissonance, disunion, division, friction, infighting, inharmony, schism, variance, war, warfare, and more.  Each of these synonyms has a distinct nuance or connotation related to the idea of disunity. Consequently, disunity encompasses a broad spectrum of expressions.
Genesis 3 shows how division is born in the spiritual (between the man and God) and the social (between human beings) sense. In other words, the sin of Adam and Eve caused a spiritual or vertical separation between them and God. Their sin also led to a social or horizontal disunity among humanity.  
On the other hand, unity is defined as the state of being one or in agreement. The Collins Dictionary defines unity as "the state of people being in agreement and acting together for a particular purpose."  Following Volf, unity is understood not merely as organizational uniformity, but as a "theological reality that demands institutional expression."  This is through shared identity, purpose, and mutual commitment, despite legitimate diversity.
The Three-In-One Concise Bible Reference Companion defines unity first as a "joined relationship, and second, as the unity of believers, which is believers' oneness with each other and with their shared purpose."  The first part of this definition is illustrated by scriptures such as Psalm 133:1, "brethren to dwell together in unity"; Ephesians 4:3, "keep the unity of the Spirit…"; and Ephesians 4:13, "we all come in the unity…". Wayne Grudem, Daniel J. Treier, and Walter A. Elwell also maintain that ''the unity of the church is its degree of freedom from divisions among true Christians."  Significant challenges to ecclesial unity and leadership have accompanied the remarkable growth of the church since its inception.
Furthermore, James D. Douglas and Merrill C. Tenney explain that the horizontal aspect of unity among true believers depends on their vertical unity with Christ. Scripture shows a richness and variety in its teachings on oneness and harmony. There is unity of the believer with the Lord (see union with Christ), and there is the union expressed in the Body of Christ, the Church, which ultimately rests on a more profound unity of believers in "one Lord, one faith, one baptism." Unity with Christ is illustrated in many ways, such as the relationship between a husband and wife or between stones and a building.  Notably, Douglas and Tenney emphasize the vertical and horizontal dimensions of Christian unity. Disunity is caused by sin, which creates separation between God and man at the vertical level. Sin also causes disunity among people at the social or horizontal level. Therefore, it makes sense that restoring horizontal unity should begin with restoring vertical unity. However, God's view of disunity and unity does not always match man's view. A brief examination of each perspective will help clarify this in the context of the study.
Leadership-Driven Divisions: This term describes conflicts, schisms, factionalism, disunity, or separations within church settings that mainly stem from personal ambition, abuse of power, contested leadership authority, a lack of servant leadership, competing leadership visions, practices, or preferences, or succession issues rather than doctrinal disagreements. Leighton Ford notes that "when leadership ceases to be Christ-centered and Spirit-led, it becomes self-serving and divisive."  Van Gelder also observes, "Leadership crises in churches are often a failure to embody the communal and Spirit-directed nature of the body of Christ."  This leads to fragmentation rather than unity, as Andrew D. Clarke emphasizes that early Christian communities struggled with factions due to leadership disputes (1 Cor.) and that Paul's corrective models highlight the root of many divisions as "leadership loyalty" rather than doctrinal error.  In essence, they are fractures in ecclesial unity catalyzed by leaders whose practices contradict the biblical models of humility, service, and Christ-centered authority. 
Ecclesial Leadership: For this study, ecclesial leadership refers to the exercise of influence, authority, and pastoral care within Church communities to shape vision, make decisions, allocate resources, nurture, and guide them, based on recognized authority, for the fulfillment of the Church's mission as the body of Christ. This form of leadership encompasses directing Christians in their spiritual formation, organizational development, and missional engagement. Robert Banks indicates, "Biblical leadership differs from other forms of leadership primarily in its theological rootedness, particularly in the character and calling of God."  For John Stott, "Christian leadership is not about power but about serving. It is modeled on Jesus’s ministry of giving and humility."  Some key characteristics of ecclesial leadership are grounded in theological principles rather than purely secular management theories. This theological foundation shapes both the nature of authority and the exercise of power within ecclesial settings. Based on Jesus's model in Mark 10:43-45, ecclesial leadership prioritizes service over domination, and Robert K. Greenleaf defines this approach as leadership that "Begins with the natural feeling that one wants to serve, to serve first. Then, conscious choice brings one to aspire to lead."  Thus, it is the practice and theological understanding of guiding and serving the Church (the ekklesia), primarily rooted in the servant-leadership of Jesus's (Mk. 10:42-45), shaped by the apostolic models of the early Church (e.g., Acts 6; Titus 1:5-9), and exercised under the lordship of Christ through the empowering of the Holy Spirit. Andrew D. Clarke investigates the nature of leadership roles in the first-century Christian community, focusing on service and moral integrity. At the same time, Joseph H. Hellerman argues that "the early church leadership was built upon familial and communal patterns, not hierarchical structures, and that authentic ecclesial leadership is always relationally grounded."  Besides, Craig Van Gelder defines this as a leadership "rooted in the missional nature of the Church as a spiritual community created by the Spirit to participate in the redemptive mission of God."  Besides, ecclesial leadership aligns with the divine mission, spiritual identity, and communal purpose as defined by Scripture. Furthermore, ecclesial leadership is distinctly Christocentric, integrating both functional leadership tasks (administration, oversight, teaching, pastoral care) and spiritual dimensions (discipleship, discernment, servanthood) within the Body of Christ.
Kingdom of God: The notion of the Kingdom of God (βασιλεία τοῦ θεοῦ) represents one of the most pivotal yet complex theological ideas in biblical literature. George Eldon Ladd indicates, "The Kingdom of God is primarily the dynamic reign or kingly rule of God, and derivatively, the sphere in which the rule is experienced." Moltmann expands this understanding by positioning the Kingdom of God as "not only transcendent but also immanent, not only future but also present, not only God’s action but also human responsibility." Additionally, Joachim Jeremias provides linguistic insights, noting that the Aramaic term malkuta that Jesus likely used "refers always to a reign, never to a realm… It is royal rule, not territory ruled by the king."  These definitions emphasize the active sovereignty of God rather than merely a territorial domain. This dialectical framework presents the Kingdom as both "already" inaugurated in Christ's ministry,  and "not yet" fully consummated. 
Kingship: The broader anthropological and theological category of kingship encompasses various dimensions of royal authority and governance. It refers to the office, authority, and function of a king, mainly as exercised within a covenantal and theocratic framework in Scripture, concerning divinely sanctioned leadership over people, with the king acting as a representative of God’s rule, either devotedly or insubordinately. Von Rad defines kingship in ancient Near Eastern settings as "a divinely ordained institution through which cosmic and social order is maintained."  For Leo G. Perdue, "Kingship represents the institutionalization of power and authority within political, religious, and economic structures, often legitimized through divine sanction or charismatic leadership."  In the Old Testament, kingship is a central institution through which God's covenant with Israel is mediated (Deut. 17:14-20; 2 Sam. 7), representing both a political and spiritual role that often combines judicial, military, and religious responsibilities.
Leadership, in the general sense, represents the capacity to influence, guide, or direct others toward a shared goal or vision. Theologically, however, leadership is not merely a function of authority, but is relational, purpose-driven, and often spiritually grounded in divine calling and communal responsibility. Banks and Ledbetter indicate, "Leadership in Scripture is not primarily about authority or charisma but about enabling the people of God to live faithfully within God’s purposes."  However, Bernard M. Bass argues that leadership is one of the most observed and least understood phenomena on earth.  It is worth noting that this complexity is reflected in the evolution of leadership theories over time. 
Servant Leadership: This term is a leadership philosophy and practice exemplified by the leader's primary devotion to serving others' highest priority needs and rooted in Jesus's example (Matt. 20:25-28), whereby positional authority is exercised through self-giving service rather than power or coercion, resulting in the growth, empowerment, and health of followers and the communities they serve. Joshua E. Cogswell, Chad J. Marshall, and Lonnie D. Smith echo, "Distinct from traditional forms of leadership that focus on the leader's agenda and ability to influence others toward that agenda, servant leadership focuses on the leader's ability to develop followers and to focus on helping others to become the best version of themselves."  This perspective centers on humility, enabling, mutuality, and prioritizing others' spiritual and shared well-being.
Pauline Ecclesiology: Pauline ecclesiology refers to the theological understanding of the nature, purpose, and structure of the church as the Body of Christ. Andrew Clarke, and Kevin J. Vanhoozer support the view that Pauline ecclesiology centers on the vision of the Church as a new creation in Christ, formed by the Spirit, marked by unity in diversity, oriented toward mutual edification and mission, and is characterized by a dynamic tension between charismatic authority and ordered community life, with leadership understood primarily in terms of service to the community rather than positions of status.  Paul's vision of the church and understanding of spiritual gifts are developed in his New Testament writings, shaped by Christ's lordship, the work of the Holy Spirit, the unity and diversity of spiritual gifts, leadership structures, and community practices within the early Christian congregations. 
Church Governance: Church governance refers to the systems, structures, offices, and practices by which authority, decision-making, and leadership are exercised within Christian communities to maintain order, ensure accountability, and fulfill the mission. Volf and Thomas F. O'Dea define this as the patterned distribution and exercise of power that exists along a spectrum from hierarchical to congregational models within an ecclesial community, which seeks to balance concerns for order, participation, contextual relevance, and theological integrity to maintain the church's identity and mission while adapting to changing contexts.  Synder, on his side, asserts, "All forms of church governance reflect not only theological convictions but also cultural assumptions about authority, community, and decision-making that are often unacknowledged yet powerfully shape ecclesial life."  Emphasizing the value of cultural analysis in understanding governance challenges, which is related to the TAGC setting that this research examines.
Practical Theology: Practical theology reflects theologically on the practices of the church and Christian life, serving as a bridge between doctrine and ministry, and facilitating the application of theological truths to real-life challenges. Richard Osmer outlines practical theology's core tasks as descriptive-empirical, interpretive, normative, and pragmatic, indicating, "The critical, theological reflection on the praxis of the church in the world, to ensure the fidelity of that praxis to the Gospel."  Thus, practical theology enables this research to investigate how Old Testament, Pauline, and Christocentric leadership models can be contextualized to address contemporary challenges posed by ethnic and leadership-driven divisions. It warrants theological insights that are not merely abstract but applied and reformational, examining the practices of the church and Christian life, and seeking to interpret them through theological reflection to guide them toward greater faithfulness and effectiveness in specific settings. This definition explains the theological basis for deconstructing leadership and unity in the TAGC.
 
Old Testament Leadership Perspectives and Community Formation
 
	The Old Testament leadership, which encompasses patriarchs, prophets, judges, and kings, is closely intertwined with the establishment of the covenant community and what the covenant community should be. The topic explores the theological underpinnings and historical functions of these leadership models, as well as their support for Israel's covenant identity and unity. The Old Testament outlines several forms of leadership, such as patriarchal, priestly, royal, prophetic, and judicial, all of which are fundamental to a particular covenant theology.
Prior to discussing the individual models of Old Testament leadership, it is necessary to explain that scholars generally articulate these positions in terms of leadership offices: patriarch, priest, royal, prophetic, and judicial. These offices are the preferred means by which God guides his people and carries out his salvific plan throughout the history of creation. Having different offices, authority structures, and theological meanings is understandable and makes sense within Christian theology, ultimately culminating in Christ as the culmination of the covenant. Herman Bavinck states, "Christ is the only mediator between God and humankind, the all-sufficient Savior, the highest prophet, the only priest, the true king. Christ is not merely an instance of a genotype; He is known only through mediated special revelation. Jesus is not an idea; He is the unique Son of God who became flesh and dwelt among us."  Jonathan Pratt also covers the three Old Testament offices, distinguished by a divine anointing: prophets, priests, and kings.  The universal purpose of God is reflected in the patriarchal office, which is based on covenant (Gen. 12; 15; 17), and is realized in Abraham, who was chosen by grace to be a blessing to the nations (Gen. 12:1-3). According to Treier, Vanhoozer, and other authors, the Old Testament patriarchs and matriarchs (Gen. 12–50) played a significant role in the identity of the covenant of Israel. Patriarchs were leaders of their families or tribes, and they were the continuers of the covenant promises through their blood and spiritual heritage. Patriarchs are ecclesiastical titles in both Catholic and Orthodox churches.  The identity is patriarchal since the family of Abraham would serve as the mediator through which the entire nation would be blessed (Gen. 12:3), a notion that Paul describes as the comprehensive nature of the Gospel (Gal. 3:8).
The priesthood office introduced a regimented order of worship, sacrifice, and spiritual teaching, thus keeping Israel in the covenant with its holy God. Priestly leaders had the duty of teaching divine law, offering sacrifices for sin, and preserving the sacred traditions that set Israel apart from other nations. With the Mosaic covenant, the priests acted as mediators between God and Israel through atonement of sin, teaching people about God, and keeping the covenant faithful (Exod. 19; Lev. 10:10–11; 4; 16). They also preserved the entry to the presence of God and underlined the need for cleansing and atonement. Thus, the Priesthood emphasizes purity, holiness, and sacred order. Joel B. Green, Jeannine K. Brown, Andrea Giardina, and Nicholas Perrin note that "many scholars agree that priests function as God's representatives, and this role is tied to their political position and an equally complex theology of the 'temple,' which explains important (and otherwise puzzling) aspects of the Gospel records. Priests were priests by birth (in the line of Aaron and the Levite)."  Pratt indicates that priests were in the presence of the Lord where they ministered (Deut. 17:12; 18:5). He continues to observe that God appointed the high priest to represent the people in the submission of gifts and sacrifices (Heb. 5:1; 9:3). His intervention was essential due to the fact of sin and guilt. In essence, priests acted as sacrificial agents, intercessors, instructors, and agents through whom the blessings and pronouncements of the gods flowed. They were also used to sustain the sacrificial system, preserve and teach the Torah, and to meet the needs of human reconciliation because of sin.
The Old Testament indicates God's promise of an everlasting kingship through Christ, King of kings, revealing His desire for man to reign over creation. This leadership is a divine authority exercised by human rulers who lead the people of God according to his righteousness and justice. The royal leaders maintained the law of God, judged, and provided leadership in wars and defence to bring peace to the country. The royal office was to maintain faithfulness to the covenant throughout the land, provide defense to the weak, and execute divine justice on the wicked. Moreover, Jeremiah 33:14-26 restates royal and priestly functions with a messianic and eschatological perspective, promising a future Davidic king and a priest similar to Melchizedek. The Davidic covenant forms the pattern of the Kingdom of God where Christ, the prophetic Messianic fulfilment (Matt. 20:28; Luke 1:32-33), reigns in righteousness and exercises His rule over all creation through the Church, and ultimately in the eschaton.
	The prophetic office is a covenantal office that emphasizes that God promises revelation will continue and that He will convey it by human hands (Deut. 18:15-19), along with ethical, remedial, and eschatological functions. Prophets acted as messengers of God who challenged Israel for covenant unfaithfulness and encouraged them to return to obedience. Katharina Pyschny and Sarah Schulz highlight the moral authority of prophets such as Jeremiah (Jer. 36-38) and Zechariah (Zech. 9-14) who revealed covenant breaches.  The essence of prophetic leadership is to receive visions of the future and speak the word of God with divine authority, compelling individuals to covenant faithfulness by addressing sin and calling them to repentance.
Thus, prophets acted with extraordinary divine power, summoning people and leaders, such as kings and priests, back to the Torah's faithfulness, threatening God's judgment while also promising His restoration. They dealt with short-term breaches of covenant and the wider redemptive intents of God, as the latter Christian understanding acknowledges shining toward the Messiah. Modern leaders should not be selfish, corrupt, prideful, greedy, or hypocritical, as they divide rather than unite. Contemporary leaders must avoid self-serving actions, corruption, pride, greed, and hypocrisy that foster division rather than unity.
