The prophetic office under the Deuteronomic covenant involved proclaiming the covenant and offering critique, fulfilled in Christ through prophets whom God promised would be like Moses (Acts 3:22-23). Therefore, as the incarnation of the Word Himself and as a prophet similar to Moses, Christ sends the final Word of God to mankind and gives the ultimate revelation of God.
The laws of the Mosaic covenant regarding judges depict them as charismatic leaders and legal arbiters (Deut. 16:18; Judg. 2:16-19) who adjudicate cases within the framework of divine law, without favoritism in legal affairs. In contrast to the in-group human, ethnic, and regional prejudices of TAGC, the judges of the judicial system had a divine mandate to free Israel of repression and establish justice in the righteousness of God. Modern leaders must imitate judicial leaders who enforce the law of God and ensure that peace and justice prevail in their societies.
The monarchical leadership is the fulfillment of all promises, creation of the eternal Kingdom of God (Isa. 9:6-7; Dan. 2:44), the ideal king, and the contradiction of the divine kingship and the human agency (1 Sam. 8). This leadership is rooted in the fact that Israel wants to have human kings like other neighboring countries. It brings about complicated relations between the sovereignty of God and the people. Despite its flaws, the monarchy fostered a sense of national identity when it was centrally governed under theocratic leadership, as evidenced by the cases of Saul, David, and Solomon. Thus, the monarchial leadership can be very blessed in the hands of God. However, the consequences of such leadership can be very severe, as kings have personal interests in covenant faithfulness. The eternal kingdom is not national, but universal, not temporal, but eternal; it is not divine, but is human, and is absolute, not delegated in its power, but is one supreme and sovereign power that embraces in itself all the former functions one above another.
Overall, both models are based on the former models but introduce new elements. Thus, the patriarchal paradigm creates the paradigm of the foundation covenant; the priestly one creates the paradigm of the problem of sin; the royal one of the divine rule; the prophetic one of the maintenance of communication of God; the judicial one of the justice in the covenant; and the monarchial one that fulfils all the promises. All these functions are fulfilled in Christ, prophets are the incarnation of the Word of God, priests are the ideal sacrifice and the intermediary, the king is the just ruler of the people, the judge is the impartial judge of the people, and the king is the sovereign of all creation, in eternity. These paradigms are linked, not independent bodies, within the multidimensional, complex, and layered nature of covenant theology when read through the prism of biblical narratives, historical church processes, and social settings.
The Old Testament leadership models were required not only in the rule, but also in community building, developing spiritual cohesion, and forming the identity of a nation. The Israelite leaders were not simply functional or political; they were representatives of the divine covenant and spiritual intermediaries, mediating divine authority and providing moral guidance that preserved the social order. They provide a significant outlook on how to maintain and rebuild the covenantal society in Israel. 
That is, OT leaders were called and anointed by God to have spiritual insight to mediate the covenant. They not only demonstrated administrative competence but also exhibited leadership qualities such as ethical integrity, relational loyalty, and faithfulness, so that their hearts were in line with the purposes of God for his people. These are the leadership tenets that remain vital to contemporary church leadership and cohesion. Although the leaders of the Old Testament were not flawless, they exhibited characteristics that made their hearts and actions consistent with God's purposes for his people. Thus, these Old Testament leadership models can be studied to demonstrate how these principles are relevant to leadership in the present day. Moreover, there might be a weakness in modern leaders, but there can also be tremendous blessings when they serve and follow God's standards and biblical morals. 
With the point of ethical integrity and upholding the moral standards of God, honesty, justice, and righteousness, it is not something of the past. However, it is a crucial leadership principle nowadays. The modern world requires such leaders as Moses who would be the intermediary between God and nations and promote the law of God with humility, justice and righteousness (Exod. 18:13-27; Num. 12:3). On the same note, the modern world is in crisis of leadership that will appreciate and utilize wisdom as an ethical governing system that is just and requires honesty in its application as Jethro was valued by Moses (Exod. 18).
Similarly, Joseph (Gen. 39-41), who persevered in his integrity even in such challenging moments (he could resist the overtures of the wife of Potiphar and did not succumb to her efforts to seduce him (Gen. 39:7-10) and who could interpret dreams and give sound advice thus sparing Egypt and his family potential starvation are not just a thing of the past. The world today and the TAGC do not have leaders of the caliber of Joseph. Thus, upholding moral and fair principles and making the right decision despite pressure will continue to help communities, cities, and countries today and glorify God in the lives of people who have yet to know Him.  
It is important to note that relationally loyal leaders are those who attach significance to faithfulness to God and to fellow people by developing trust and community, both of which are significant today. Examples like David (1 Sam. 16-2 Sam. 24) and Ruth (Ruth 1:16-18). Contemporary leaders can also prioritize the welfare of churches over self-interest, as David and Ruth did.
Secondly, it is important not to be deceived out of the purposes of God, and even nowadays, people can still utilize the services of leaders who are not distracted by worldly pleasures but are focused on the purposes of God for their people. Thus, perseverance combined with prayerfulness and faithfulness in achieving God's objective, as demonstrated by leaders such as Nehemiah (Neh. 1-6), who did not give up on rebuilding the walls of Jerusalem despite resistance, remains essential in the kingdom of God. Following the example of Nehemiah, the contemporary world, which is in significant decline, will also be significantly benefited by leaders such as Samuel (1 Sam. 7:3-15), who helped Israel emerge from a spiritual downturn. The world is craving prophets such as Nehemiah who will not serve for selfish gain and to advance the nation, but will serve under the direction of God, who will anoint the president or leaders. Thus, the virtue of faithfulness, the ability to match one vision and activity with the purposes of God and manifest endurance in the accomplishment of the missio Dei, is still one of the values of the present leadership.
The concept of leadership in Ancient Israel and the present day are similar, as the importance of integrity, which was viewed as ethical, is the same in both periods. Ancient Israel required its leaders to live righteous lives and obey the law of God. Ethical integrity is now considered an important construct for establishing trust, transparency, unity, and social justice. In business or religious contexts, when a leader is honest and fair, he not only exemplifies the ethical behavior of Moses and Joseph but also glorifies God. The Old Testament loyalty centered on community and covenant relations, and its lessons apply to contemporary leaders to increase trust, create team loyalty, develop organizational loyalty, and foster national loyalty. The present leaders must be concerned about the welfare of their churches or their teams, as David was concerned about his people and created a faithful and powerful following. More than that, Old Testament leaders such as Nehemiah and Samuel, who put the mission of God ahead of their personal ambition, encouraged leaders in the modern era to serve God and His mission, as they exemplify this doctrine. Consequently, national, regional, local, and community leaders must aim to serve others rather than pursue their own interests; this way, they disciple God and become people of faith, just as Nehemiah is.  
To conclude, the leadership theories of Moses, Joseph, David, Ruth, Nehemiah, and Samuel underscore the significance of integrity, moral values, relationship loyalty, and faithfulness. Leaders who stick to these values resonate with the purposes of God and are not obsolete with time. Also, modern leaders who possess these Old Testament traits can establish credibility, build unity and community, and accomplish their God-given purpose with good moral character, similar to the ancient leaders of Israel.
Nevertheless, the Old Testament provides valuable records of theological and historical leadership that shed light on the multidimensional associations among leadership, community building, identity, and cohesion in the Hebrew Bible. According to Brueggemann, biblical leadership does not focus on position or prestige, but emphasizes service, responsibility, and faithfulness to the purposes of God among the people.  In the words of Gerhard von Rad, this Old Testament leadership, beginning with the prophets and the monarchs, is founded on the relationship between divine sovereignty, covenant, and the calling of Israel as a covenant people.  The development of leadership paradigms at different stages of the patriarchate and the establishment of the monarchy was based on theological priorities and the socio-political reality. In practice, the Old Testament system of leadership is not founded on personal power but rather on common custodianship and future generation, which defined corporate identity and enabled collective actions towards divine ends. Shared stewardship, mutual respect, and acceptance must define the complicated relationships between modern leadership.
Methodologically, this section advocates the view that Old Testament leadership texts can be understood through qualitative theological-historical inquiry into the development of leadership paradigms within the broader biblical narrative and their effects on community formation. At the center of this approach is a theological hermeneutic that interprets the Old Testament as divinely inspired and incorporates insights from literature, canon, and narrative to find its theological meaning. Based on the interaction between the covenant and community, Brueggemann insists that the theological structure determines the vertical (divine-human) and horizontal (human-human) relationships necessary for the development of community.  Jon D. Levenson, in turn, concludes that the covenant pattern offers the basis of power, responsibility, and social unity.  According to Brevard S. Childs, the leadership texts serve as both historical witnesses and theological concepts within the broadest biblical narrative.  This methodology gives a more detailed insight into how leadership practices mirrored and influenced communal identity in ancient Israel. 
Old Testament Leadership Theological Foundations
Old Testament leadership is primarily focused on God, where He serves as the supreme leader, and human leaders become stewards of His plan. Forrest, Benjamin, and Chet Roden investigate the biblical authors to argue that the basis of biblical leadership rests on scriptural exegesis rather than secular models of leadership. They assert that the Pentateuch presents Godly leadership ("Moses as the humble servant," "Solomon, representing leadership discernment and wisdom," "Joseph, embodying resilience shaped by hardship," "Daniel and his companions, exhibiting courageous dependence," "Nehemiah, portraying strategic wisdom and leadership," and "Esther, revealing brave servanthood traits") rooted in divine call, as opposed to ungodly leadership (Cain who exhibits a self-centered, violent and spiritually astray demeanor) grounded in self-interest.  God calls people to lead, and some of these leaders are Moses (Exod. 3:1-12), Samuel (1 Sam. 3:1-21), and David (1 Sam. 16:1-13). In this way, modern leaders can study Forrest, Benjamin, and Chet, as Moses did, through their leadership philosophies of humility and delegation, showing how one can avoid burnout in leadership by empowering others under their supervision. Through the wisdom of Solomon, listening helped him show how to rule with wisdom and discernment because he understood people's needs. The story of Joseph and his character growth provides valuable information on crisis management. By setting an example of bravery and reliance, Daniel and his friends demonstrate how one can stay loyal and faithful in the face of persecution and pressure. Julie Headley notes that Daniel, Joseph, and David are three prominent Old Testament figures who showed elements of what we now call authentic leadership.  By reinstating Jerusalem through communal effort, Nehemiah introduces strategic planning, inclusion, and delegation. Lastly, from Esther, who showed courage and sacrificial service, current leaders can learn to leave God to work through them and to redeem His people by risking their own lives. All these leadership characteristics promote ecclesial leadership and cohesiveness.
As a person who led a multinational people of Israelites on a forty-year transformative journey from slavery to nationhood, Moses exhibits the characteristics of a conventional leader ready to face the challenges that characterize leadership in the current times. His leadership includes crisis management, organizational development, conflict resolution, and vision casting, grounded in decades of practical ministry experience. It shows how the divine calling, together with human maturity, can create effective leadership to serve God's goal and to address issues that touch real life. The Bible provides a step-by-step account of his achievements and setbacks, offering present-day leaders a model of what good leadership entails. Moses's leadership style offers timeless principles that transcend cultural, regional, ethnic, in-group, and historical boundaries, serving as a model for contemporary leaders on how to lead church communities in various contexts. His genuine humility (Exod. 3:11; 4:13), his reluctance to seek power or position (Exod. 3:11, 13; 4:1, 10, 13; 12:13), his skill in managing conflicts (Exod. 12:1-15; 16:1-35; 17:1-7; 18:13-27; 20:1-13; 32:11-14), his ability to maintain morale during difficult transitions (Exod. 14), conflict resolution (Num. 12), and his leadership of a large group (Exod. 18:13-27; Num. 11:16-30) remain relevant across cultures and centuries.
Solomon's notable success in political influence and economic prosperity (1 Kings 4:20-34; 9:26-28; 10:11-29) acts as a cautionary tale on the corruptive influence of unchecked power. On the one hand, the period of his reign demonstrates that divine gifts and blessings might be the beginning of spiritual compromise when leaders cannot trust in God and forget about their duties to Him. On the same note, the decision to choose wisdom over personal wealth, power, or self-interest when God presented him with everything he wanted (1 Kings 3:9-12) highlights one of the most important values a leader today should adhere to. The fact that he could comprehend complex situations and relationships (1 Kings 3:16-28), that he could treat others justly and equally, and that he could apply the principles to the particular cases provided valuable lessons for modern leaders. Also crucial is his recognition of individual shortcomings (1 Kings 3:7) and his unselfishness to seek assistance, as well as his readiness to be taught by others, no matter who they are, and his desire to be helpful to others instead of being served, which is, nevertheless, pertinent in the present day. 
Pat Williams and Jim Denney tell us how one of the coaches of the Cincinnati Bengals, Marvin Lewis, learned about a very well-rounded junior college linebacker whom several universities had heavily recruited. However, nobody was even bothering with it any longer. When Marvin inquired of the other coaches and scouts why they had not recruited this player, they all said he was a bad apple, regardless of his talent. They characterized him as arrogant, disrespectful, and uncoachable. Marvin disregarded all these warnings and hired the player. He could not even get along with the team when he was on it; worse still, he had negatively affected two other fine young players, along with a talented freshman, all because of his bad character and attitude.  To ensure unity, modern leaders should be careful not to believe that their leadership skills qualify them for leadership roles. Neither should they be a bad apple in the leadership team, nor should they condone bad apples in their in-groups who happen to be of ethnic or regional backgrounds. Modern leaders can also learn to avoid the errors made by Solomon in the quest to emerge successful, as this can easily lead to a compromise of their values in the name of growth or success. They need to develop rapport with individuals who will offer transparent feedback.
Joseph is a classic biblical example of a resilient, godly, and strategic leader (Gen. 37-50), who has exhibited perennial values that are still applicable in the current ministry, business, politics, family, or governance. His leadership experience shows how character-building through adversity prepares one to handle positions of responsibility and influence. Current leaders can learn from Joseph not to lose sight of the God-given, compelling vision for their lives, even when facing adversity (Gen. 37:5-10). They can also learn the importance of staying true to God and being faithful, regardless of the circumstances they face. David Sharpley observes, "Effective leadership is always context-specific. Personal conviction must be linked to a meaningful rationale that contributes to momentum and the development of a shared purpose. A clear vision creates the opportunity to engage others in constructive discussion concerning goals and the overall strategy."  Rick Warren, Gen. Norman Schwarzkopf, Warren Bennis, Oswald Chambers, Ken Blanchard, and Phil Hodges agree that vision gives pain a purpose and that leadership without strategy is better than leadership without character. Leadership is the capacity to translate vision into reality, and God does not call the already equipped. Instead, He equips the called, and developing strategies and spiritual habits help leaders stay on purpose.  Joseph's leadership style combines practical wisdom with spiritual insight, demonstrating how Christians can lead effectively in secular settings while upholding God's principles. 
By giving power to others around him (in prison as well as in the court of the Pharaoh, 39:22; 41:40), Joseph proves that good leaders never accumulate power; they empower others, distribute responsibilities, and build leadership paths. Although ambition is essential in business, integrity, resilience, and clarity remain only through vision in such trials. The model of leadership exhibited by Joseph shows that after intervals of preparation, which entail several tests, the process usually enhances one's character, a crucial ingredient of faithfulness to God. The present leaders in countries require leadership, as illustrated by Joseph.
Daniel lived in an environment of aggression (Dan. 1). The fact that he does not want to contaminate himself with the food of the king demonstrates convictional leadership founded on wisdom and moral power (Dan. 6). This type of obedient defiance still constitutes a valuable leadership lesson to the current leaders. Equally, Daniel is a practical example of what it means to be spiritually upright in the face of secular service that can be inconsistent with biblical ethical principles. As Headley points out, Daniel knew the limit of his strength and the beginning of the strength of God (Dan. 2), which is why he was very knowledgeable that his knowledge, his wisdom, and even his life were dependent on God. Along with being self-aware, Daniel exhibited relational transparency with those around him (Dan. 1:8). He stayed true to his core values, demonstrating the potential for faithful witness through excellent performance combined with an unwavering commitment to God's standards, regardless of the cost to himself. He remained committed to God alone, even when faced with imminent death, revealing his internalized moral perspective (Dan. 1-6).  In addition, Daniel's disciplined praying life (Dan. 6:10), which he continued despite the threat, contributed to his social influence and challenged the current leaders to develop the same spiritual depth in the face of adversity.
Nehemiah exemplifies burden-driven visionary leadership (Neh. 1:3-4), prayerful and strategic leadership (Neh. 2:4-8; 3-6), and teaches today's leaders to let God inspire vision through burden, while acting with urgency, careful planning, and reliance on God. His leadership approach shows how a genuine spiritual burden for God's people and purposes serves as the foundation for effective ministry and organizational transformation. Current leaders can learn from Nehemiah the importance of blending faith with administrative precision, building a team, facing criticism, and persevering with clarity of mission. Raymond Brown indicates that Nehemiah not only saw Jerusalem's broken walls, but also its broken identity, teaching other leaders to become both builders of walls and people.  Hans-Georg Wünch notes that "Nehemiah is the uncrowned hero. Everything he does is right and God-given, and one can and must learn from it."  The integrity of his character was beyond taxation, and his behavior in Jerusalem was above praise; and his foes, not able to slay him, slandered him. The model that Nehemiah provided is powerful for modern ecclesial leadership.
Another point is that Nehemiah demonstrates leadership driven by a passion for God's kingdom rather than a desire for social status. His prayer life focuses on the need for leaders to make a habit of talking to God. His approach to communicating his vision (Neh. 2:17) inspires the current leaders to communicate their visions in a way that encourages followers to join the organization toward a shared objective. Moreover, he asked the governors to send him letters (Neh. 2:7-8), indicating that he sought to preempt potential challenges. Current leaders will be able to anticipate challenges, develop contingency plans, and procure the required resources before initiating major projects. They, too, may learn the importance of maintaining spiritual sensitivity and being practical, wise, or passionate, but planning well, as Nehemiah demonstrates, prayer ought to inform strategy rather than substitute for it.
Adding to this, Nehemiah, as a governor of Jerusalem, could have enjoyed his salary and used his position to amass wealth solely for himself; instead, he chose to share with many poor people around him and to rescue the covenant community (Neh. 5). He sacrificed what was rightly owed to him for the sake of the people, demonstrating sacrificial love. Nehemiah could also have used the law allowing collateral for loans (Ex. 21:1-11; Deut. 24:10-13), which might have enabled the few wealthy Jews to misuse God's law (Lev. 25:35-43). However, he fought for justice by asking for lands to be returned to the poor (Neh. 5:6-19). This is a good example of the ideal servant leadership as portrayed in his own behavior. By following the principles of Nehemiah, modern leaders will be able to establish trust and credibility, demonstrate selflessness, share sacrifices in hard times, care about the welfare of the community rather than their own gain, and set an example of the desired behavior.
Esther is a paradigm of bold and wise leadership acting based on limited formal power. She mentions the need to be sensitive to organizational timing and to identify critical action points. The example of her life shows that even seemingly insignificant roles can be influential, and leaders can be effective with godly wisdom. The strategy of Esther highlights the significance of adequate foresight and strategic planning in handling emergent circumstances. She did it because the good of the community (Esther 4:14) was at stake, not self-interest, since she was no longer passive but took action when her people were in danger. She is also an illustration of the need for spiritual training before making decisions with high stakes and the courage to risk her own life (Esther 4:16). The values of Esther can guide contemporary leaders to learn that it is important to take personal risk to defend justice and moral values or the welfare of their people. She did not take advantage of the situation and personal gain; she took it for the higher good.  
Notably, Esther is patient and well-timed, refusing to hurry in revealing Haman's plot. This prudent silence reiterates the patience in leadership and timing by God, as John P. Kotter puts it, that Effective Leaders know when to act and when to wait.  In that regard, the success of leaders is their capacity to make strategic decisions and act accordingly. Robert Hargrove adds, "Leadership is not just about what you can do on your own but about how effectively you can collaborate with others to achieve,"  which underscores the importance of unity. Esther understood King Xerxes's mindset and used her knowledge to her advantage. Besides, she was well-connected with people in the palace, demonstrating the relational intelligence necessary to navigate tricky leadership environments. Consequently, she did not assume her role as a royal figure, remembering Mordecai, her mentor, and the Jews who were fasting and praying that she succeed. Instead, she showed a collective sense of purpose and empathy for their suffering, demonstrating her leadership, compassion, and emotional connection with the people. Daniel Goleman notes, "Leaders with high emotional intelligence are better at motivating others and creating an environment where people feel understood and valued." Leadership, grounded in the principles of biblical community, also demands relational networking and cooperation. Contemporary leaders, when confronted with the same problems as Esther, must find a balance between decision-making and careful thinking. Therefore, the leadership model of Esther supports the primary focus of this study that Christ-like courage, empathy, and discernment are the keys to developing unity and ethical leadership in such ecclesial settings as the TAGC. 
Combining all the Old Testament case studies (Moses to Esther) in this discussion, it is evident that contemporary leaders can apply these ancient and basic theological principles to address contemporary situations. Despite Moses, Solomon, Joseph, Daniel, Nehemiah, and Esther living in diverse contexts, cultures, and times and facing different obstacles, they exhibit leadership qualities that are useful today. The modern world needs leaders who are courageous and willing to act in ways that are more community-oriented than self-focused. Therefore, their leadership philosophies do not apply only to them but contain much more valuable lessons, lessons that can be relevant to contemporary leaders. As an illustration, unity, a shared God-given vision, interpersonal skills, assistance in solving conflicts, and strategic spiritual preparation are necessary. Leaders who apply the principles of Nehemiah not only to reconstruct the walls that have been broken, but also to repair the relationships, not to mention the lives, are not only leaders who contain the immediate problems, but also leaders who look forward, envision, and create identities and dignity. Another dimension of biblical leadership is facilitating group involvement in God's work. Empathy, integrity, and leaders such as David or Esther, who have emotional intelligence, are essential in a contemporary church environment. Accordingly, in a world often marked by a lack of forgiveness, revenge, and behaviors that create problems rather than solve them, there is an urgent need for leaders like Joseph who demonstrate resilience, ethical leadership, courage, forgiveness, and the ability to let go of past grievances for the greater good.
These leadership illustrations conclude the theological reflection on divine calling and charisma. Brueggemann also notes that the Old Testament theology of leadership is both covenantal and charismatic, revealing that God calls human vessels not on the basis of their natural credentials but on His initiative.  Leon Kass contends that Moses's reliance on Aaron and Hur shows the weakness of human beings in charismatic leadership and that covenant leaders must share responsibility. Aaron and Hur hint at the demystification of charisma through this episode. Kass further notes that God was leading Israel to avoid viewing Moses as a divine character. Success in God's mission does not depend on the extraordinary deeds of a charismatic leader. God was also preparing Israel for the post-Mosaic era, for the rule of law (under God) rather than under the rule of the glorious leader. Kass adds that the majestic Moses, God's champion, is like any mortal and not immune to human weakness.  Contemporary leaders, no matter how charismatic they may consider themselves, must acknowledge their human frailties.
On the other hand, Heinz Guenther observes, "The Old Testament does not paint charisma in such broad strokes but uses the term more specifically to describe the pivotal role charismatic leaders played in the early days of Israel's political history."  This insight explains why charismatic empowerment within the OT enhanced community identity rather than establishing a sense of superiority. He further explains that the recognition of the Christian's unlimited share in the Spirit, justified as it were by the particular χάρισμα working in every believer, prevented Paul from endorsing any church hierarchy. For him, no one Christian is to be ranked above another.  Believers' equal sharing in Jesus's authority stemming from Paul's concept of charisma cautions present ecclesial leaders against vainglory and pride that fuel ecclesial division.
C. G. Montefiore emphasises that Jesus was a charismatic Jewish prophet who transformed leadership with his teachings of kindness and service. According to Montefiore, Jesus was a Jewish prophet and ethical reformist, the most outstanding charismatic leader of all time. He asserts that the ideals in the Sermon on the Mount and other Gospel passages cannot be transformed into civil laws; however, they embody the hero's principles and remain robust and spiritually meaningful today. In this context, he notes regarding Jesus's expectations from His disciples (Matt. 8:21f), "Jesus's demands and inspires disciples to follow Him not for study but for service to help the Master in his mission to carry out his instruction, etc."  Thus, the discipleship of Jesus that calls to service should guide modern leadership in shaping a comprehensive approach to service for communities rather than self-promotion. According to this view, modern ecclesial leadership practices grounded in Jesus's teaching must emphasize inclusiveness and humility. Overall, the Old Testament examples and theologizing can demonstrate that covenantal duty in the Old Testament goes hand in hand with charismatic empowerment of effective leadership. This synthesis prefigures the subsequent discussion of patriarchal leadership and its constitutive influence on the construction of familial and communal identity.
In conclusion, the examples from the Old Testament and theological reflections prove that leadership founded on covenantal accountability and the charismatic force are combined. This overview foreshadows the discussion of patriarchal leadership and its positive impact on the development of family and community identity.
Patriarchal Leadership, Familial, and Community Identity
The patriarchal paradigm is the first divinely directed pattern of leadership in the history of Israel and laid the basis for the covenant pattern of leadership in the future. Patriarchs such as Abraham (Gen. 12, 15, 17), Isaac (Gen. 26:2-5), and Jacob, who later became Israel (Gen. 28:13-15), are foundational because they instituted the covenant and established the genealogical foundation of Israel. They lived during the pre-Mosaic period, before the law was given and before Israel became a nation. Bruce K. Waltke (2001) points out that the patriarchs were not merely historical figures; they also held religious, legal, economic, and moral authority, serving as spiritual leaders, decision-makers, and covenant-holders—serving as theological symbols of God's covenantal promises and guidance.  The patriarchal model in the Old Testament is rooted in divine election, where God initiates leadership through sovereign election (Gen. 12:1-3). This text is the thematic focal point of the biblical salvation narrative since it serves as the starting point of the Abrahamic covenant, as he is the model of covenantal obedience. Therefore, the Abrahamic covenant continues to be central to both the identity of Israel and the universal redemptive purpose of God. The model illustrates how leadership in ancient Israel was formed through familial accountability and divine pledge, rather than institutional frameworks.
Patriarchs were also intercessors (Gen. 18:22-33) who served as a good example of ethical leadership grounded in justice and compassion. They also had covenantal promises and were an example of faith. The emphatic Hebrew phrase (לֶךְ־ לְךָ֛), "Lekh-lekha," meaning to go, depart, proceed, move, go away, go for yourself, go forth, to die, live, and a way of life, etc. To expound on these meanings, they show that God required Abram to believe in His initiative and to follow without revealing to him the ultimate destination He wanted Abram to pursue. The other lesson that can be learnt is that this exodus is not only geographical but also theological, as it represents the renunciation of idolatrous foundations and a journey towards a covenantal future. Correspondingly, God requires contemporary leaders to act faithfully and rely entirely on Him (Heb. 11:8) if they wish to foster ecclesial unity and collaborate as effective leaders. In agreement with Waltke,  contemporary leadership and ecclesial unity require obedience to God’s inclusive concept and detachment from past prejudicial in-group preferences to adhere to mutual respect and acceptance.  
To add to this, the patriarchal leadership model reflects what John T. Greene and Andrew Swafford identify as the concept of all humanity in Adam, also known as the "patriarchal priesthood" or "priesthood of progeniture," serving as a pattern through which blessings unfold. The missional theology grounds God's election of Abraham not as exclusive but as instrumental for worldwide blessing. The global redemptive plan unfolds through the nationhood plan (לְג֣וֹי גָּד֔וֹל), meaning a "great nation" (Gen. 15:5; 17:4-5), a divine favor and provision rendered through the Hebrew phrase (בְּרָכָֽה׃), which means "a blessing," making Abram’s name great (Gen. 11:4) or a conduit of blessing (Gen. 11:2) for all families of the earth (Gen. 11:3). These trends lie in the life of Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob, which have given rise to the family-oriented nature of governance, which essentially considers leadership to be covenantal and familial, and not administrative and political. Besides that, the global redemptive plan urges modern leaders to come together in the God-appointed mission to His Church.
These concepts are interrelated and form the theological framework through which Israel perceived its identity, relational responsibility, and divine calling. This order involves unity and accountable roles for spiritual or biological parenthood that would facilitate the fulfillment of God’s purposes. Jason S. DeRouchie and Michael Grisanti discuss covenant typology, emphasizing that the Abrahamic promises (land, seed, and blessing) lay the foundation for both the Davidic and Sinai covenants, which are more fully revealed in the Sinai law and Davidic kingship.  Brueggemann articulates that the patriarchal stories reveal familial and spiritual leadership stemming from divine promise (Gen. 12-50). 
Simultaneously, the judges' period illustrates cyclical patterns of charismatic deliverance and communal instability (Judg. 2:11-19).  He argues that modern secular leadership theories tell us little about Old Testament leadership. Careful attention to the theological anthropology and covenant theology inherent in ancient Israel's society is essential.  The analysis of the idea of patriarchal leadership and theological reflections confront modern leadership paradigms that prioritize personal success over covenant duty and the generational heritage. These dynamics undermine modern versions of leadership, mainly when ethnic, regional, or favoritism-based inclinations are used to undermine ecclesial unity.
Additionally, patriarchal discourses provide a framework for interpreting leadership as covenantal service rather than institutional authority. Based on the Abrahamic leadership model, in line with H. C. Gallagher, Jasmine Fraser, and Danihelova Lea, "to have a family implies love, a sense of belonging and well-being, as mutual reciprocity nurtures the development of its members."  Likewise, having a spiritual family or belonging to the Body of Jesus Christ provides a sense of spiritual well-being and mutual respect and acceptance, while ethnic prejudice forms the opposite. 
Moreover, Frank Moore Cross and Brueggemann explain the nuance of legitimacy by noting that, contrary to the belief that "Ancient Near East leadership models legitimacy typically depended on birth, divine adoption through established cultic systems, or conquest,"  Brueggemann further observes that legitimacy does not arise from institutional appointment, but through divine calling, which he identifies as "promise-based leadership."  This doctrine warns church leaders not to base power on institutional designations or divine destiny. In addition, W. Bennis, along with Lynne Kelly, James A. Keaten, Cynthia Finch, Ilze B. Duarte, Patrizia Hoffman, and Margaret M. Michels, concurs with N. Saphir, M., and Chaffee, S. H, J. Koesten, and Q. Zhang, noting that "Family communication environment is a contributing factor in the development of reticence. Besides, scholars argue that leadership is formed and enhanced during childhood, thus, closely connecting family and leadership."  It is also noteworthy that patriarchal leadership is exemplified by biblical characters such as Abraham whom Cameron A. Batmanghlich identifies as "as the patriarch of the three main religions in the world,"  and Isaac, Jacob, Joseph, Moses, David, and Solomon who functioned as familial and national leaders, covenant bearers, and intercessors.
The second clue to patriarchal leadership lies in the word "father" (אָב, av), where the Old Testament understanding of the word carries immense theological connotations far beyond its biological connotations. The Hebrew word for father (אָ֭ב), which refers to the genitor of an individual  (אָבִ֖יו) "his father" (Gen. 2:24) and is used to interpret that the fatherly leadership was not institutional, but it is relational, based on the divine promise and covenant obedience (Gen.12:1-3; 15:1-6). It describes Abraham as an example of faith and obedience whose leadership led to the creation of Israel as a covenant people. In addition, there is the term (bet av), "father‘s house," which means the basic social unit of Israel. Meanwhile, this structure shows how individual identity depends on belonging to a family, which in turn depends on the divine covenant. The identity of leadership in the New Testament is based on the work of the Holy Spirit, and it is a spiritual family, not a biological one. In that way, the Holy Spirit's spiritual lineage shows that it cannot live or work in a fragmented state.
Abraham's Paradigm of Servant Leadership and Community Formation
Abraham is a central figure in the Bible, representing a crucial period of transformation. He is not only the father of Israel's faith (Gen. 12:1-3) but also a model leader whose life exemplifies servant leadership rooted in covenant obedience, reverence, hospitality, intercession, humility, and the protection of family. His leadership also offers valuable theological and ethical lessons for both biblical and modern leaders, especially those in churches. Abraham's leadership is an exemplar of covenantal relationship and God's leadership system, founded on divine call and obedience. The promotion of unity, however, must be based on these fundamental values of obedience, reverence, hospitality, intercession, humility, and protection, which are biblically grounded and practiced values of the Christian faith and teachings. These values can be combined to develop spiritual, community, and personal wholeness, promote relational wholeness and maturity, and encourage the faithful witness within and without the Church, as depicted by the following points.
To begin with, leadership entails obedience since it is a sign of submission to the authority and will of God as seen in the example of obedience as exhibited by Christ "to death, even death on the cross" (Phil. 2:8). In addition, Paul proposes that leaders should be obedient and teach sound doctrine (Titus 2:1), and states that unity in church is based on obedience to the word of God.
Second, leadership should be marked by a sense of awe for God and a great respect for the community. Although Christians are called to "worship God acceptably with reverence" (Heb. 12:28-29) and "to serve with reverence and awe" (New American Standard Bible), this appeal mainly addresses those who shepherd others. The Greek term (εὐλαβείας) "eulabeias," which means "reverence," denotes a cautious, pious fear, godly fear, piety, or profound respect. The other Greek word (δέους), "deous," translated as "awe," means holy fear, or dread of the majesty of God with no reference to terror, cowardice, shyness, or fear. Instead, Christians need to cultivate a sober consciousness of divine greatness and judgment, and when every leader adopts such a stance, it will create unanimity. Lastly, a Greek word (λατρεύωμεν), "latreuōmen," which means "to serve/worship/serve" God, is used to denote religious service in the temple. Worship is also associated with a way of life and obedience (Rom. 12:1) as a form of appreciation of having been given the indestructible Kingdom via acceptable worship and reverence, since God is a consuming fire (v. 29), a mentioning of Deuteronomy 4:24. Finally, the modern day leader should understand reverence is not Old Testament legalism but the New Testament worshipful fear ethic. Reverence is not purely a creation of covenant theology, but also finds its foundation in Christology, since the mediation of Jesus is the mediator of the New Covenant (Heb. 12:24), without taking away His majesty. The nature and the holiness that cannot be changed make God appear to force us to comprehend that grace must never be a cause of presumption, but of deep humility and obedience to God.
Third, hospitality according to the theological perspective must not be exclusive but inclusive, as the welcoming nature of the Kingdom, particularly what is expected of the elders (1 Tim. 3:2; Titus 1:8). Hospitality is radicality towards Christ and resembles Him that, as a receiver of strangers, He dined with sinners without judging or neglecting them (Luke 19:5-10). Leadership hospitality is an aspect of openness and relational inclusivity, which is a feature of early Christian communities.
Fourth, there is the case of intercession where church leaders intervene on behalf of people as Christ interceded on behalf of the Church (Rom. 8:34; Heb. 7:25), and Paul also constantly interceded on behalf of the churches (Eph. 1:16; Phil. 1:3-4). The Greek (δεξιᾷ), meaning "right hand," is used to convey Christ's ultimate power and dignity, which is equivalent to Psalm 110:1; this reflects both Christ's divine nature and his advocacy. The phrase (ἐντυγχάνει), "egertheis," connotes active mediation and supplication. Leaders cannot be effective in promoting reconciliation while being divided. The importance of spiritual leadership and oneness is emphasized by the role of Jesus and Paul in the intercession.
Fifth, humility is not only an essential quality of Christian leadership, but it also impacts the spiritual lives of leaders, avoids dictatorship and division, and serves as a source of unity in the Church. Humility is a trait of servant-kingship of Christ (Matt. 20:27), opposes pride and overpowering (Phil. 2:3), promotes relational harmony, servant leadership, mutual respect and acceptance. 
Sixth, protection is a core value of the church leadership and is not negotiable; it involves spiritual and communal safety, as shepherds are charged with protecting the flock against false doctrines and threats (Acts 2:28-30), thereby guaranteeing unity in truth and love (Eph. 4:15-16). As such, the protective mandate is biblical and depicts leaders as having been given the mandate of the flock of God, beginning with Jesus, as he declares, "I am the good shepherd. The shepherd of the sheep sacrifices his life to the sheep" (John 10:11). Protective mandate extends beyond physical protection to spiritual protection, doctrine protection, emotional protection and relational protection since the leaders are aware that they have been given the responsibility of what is Gods and the truth that Paul underlines in Acts 20:28. Due to this, contemporary leaders must promote spiritual maturity, collective relational wholeness, personal and shared emotional integrity, and faithful witness all these are the pillars of church leadership. Collectively, these aspects constitute a Christ-like model of church governance that sets the reader up for future theological synthesis. These values collectively embody Christ-like character, relational integrity, and missional faithfulness, fostering an effective church community. 
In addition to Abraham's leadership model, David J. Ridges states, "The Abrahamic covenant is the heritage of all faithful, baptized, covenant-keeping members of the Church. Such faithful members are often called 'Israel' because these covenants were renewed and reaffirmed through Jacob, Abraham's grandson, whose name was changed to 'Israel' (see Gen. 12:28). The blessings of the Abrahamic covenant extend far beyond this mortal life to the eternities."  Paul Williamson also notes, "The church is the spiritual Israel, the true people of God. The Church is now the 'Israel of God' (Gal. 6:16); the spiritual Israel has taken the place of the literal Israel."  Therefore, Abraham's willingness to leave behind security and kingship ties and to embark toward an unknown Promised Land (Heb. 11:8) highlights a radical form of obedient leadership that listens, trusts, and moves according to God’s will. 
Abraham's leadership illustrates obedience that awaits the kenotic posture adopted by Christ (Phil. 2: 5-11), not personal ambition or human reasoning. Rad underscores that patriarchal discourses represent a special type of charismatic leadership when the divine initiative and human response meet in history-making events.  Moreover, Abraham's leadership exemplifies humility, hospitality, intercession, and relational faith in God, as an example of faith-based leadership that builds community, mediates divine blessings, and looks forward to the servant kingship of Christ. The qualities are still core in imparting effective church leadership and unity in the modern world.
To elaborate upon this point, the role of fatherhood in the Old Testament is actually a sophisticated theological metaphor that turns the concept of authority against the concepts of relational responsibility and absolute power. Abraham portrays the aspect of servant-hood by intercession and advocacy. Gen. 18:16-33 demonstrates that Abraham is an intercessor to the Sodom people, where he beseeches mercy (Gen. 18:25) and justice on behalf of other people, who are morally compromised, at the risk of divine anger to save the righteous. This not only represents the subsequent intercession of Moses (Ex. 32:11-14) but also prefigures the mediatorial role of Christ, which encourages men in modern times to be the carriers of the justice and mercy of God as they represent the vulnerable.
The act of Abraham accepting the three visitors (Gen 18) is radical hospitality and covenant faithfulness (Heb 13:2). Early Jewish Christian traditions regarded this as a paradigm of righteousness (Heb. 13:2).  In particular, Jean Jeffress supports the idea that, according to Acts 2, Jews traveled from near and far to gather together. As they gathered, the Holy Spirit descended, breaking down cultural and social barriers, and Acts 2:42-47 illustrates the growth of a community engaged in being the church. 
A complementary point is made by Asher Intrater, who recounts a prophetic message that came to him and Eitan Shishkoff as they awaited Dan Juster’s arrival for a meeting to strengthen their relationships and address misunderstandings that had arisen in one of the ministries they oversee. While they waited, they contemplated whether it was worth continuing to strengthen their relationships as they had been, or going their separate ways, which would yield more money, more people, and more accomplishments for the kingdom of God. Since their friend Dan was somewhat late, Eitan and Intrater decided to pray rather than continue an unproductive conversation, and the Holy Spirit told them, "I am not in the business of building ministries, but rather of building a Body."  For the sake of their unity, contemporary leaders must meet regularly to work on their relationships, mainly when misunderstandings occur.  
Moreover, Intrater refers to God as the covenant God and considers the Bible an effective, time-proven tool for promoting enduring, spiritual, and emotional relationships among all participants. He also promotes a more profound spiritual covenant and then redefines biblical jealousy as a healthy defensive system in relation to the covenant. Intrater believes that covenant is a key component in establishing lasting and robust relationships, mending troubled relationships, and sustaining relationships despite conflict and disagreement. Thus, these principles of covenant can serve as a biblical basis for leadership and ethnic issues in the modern church setting. In conclusion, Intrater’s book offers key leadership principles, including what to look for in a potential servant leader, how to develop deeper relationships, markers of godly character, and characteristics of elders and pastors. Any church that seeks to embody biblical principles as a lived, inside-out transformation must revisit covenant principles with humility and prayer.
Theological Meaning of Fatherhood and Authority
Fatherhood is a bond between individual identity and the theological identity of the chosen people of God. Fatherhood in the Old Testament has a deep religious significance, as it identifies one as a member of the covenant community. It assures universal eschatological reconciliation between God and humanity (Mal. 3:24). The Old Testament fatherhood also suggests that Messianic fatherhood transcends the temporal limitations of human fatherhood, establishing an everlasting paternal bond between the Messiah and His people. It is a term that comes into play later in the book, as Paul articulates his views on leadership and unity, which center on his idea of adoption. According to Trevor J. Burke (2006), God is relational and relates to his people. He claims that the nature of God is relational and is manifested in the family relationship with Father, Son, and Holy Spirit. The purpose God has of His adopted sons obliges the latter, too, to honor the former by seeking to live accordingly.  Burke explores a broader biblical theology of sonship and its implications for Christian identity and conduct, offering valuable insights for contemporary leaders. Burke presents Pauline concepts of adoption, stressing the familial relationship between God and believers and the corresponding ethical responsibilities. 
Thus, the social reality and practice of adoption in antiquity provide valuable insights into what Paul says about the believer's responsibility as an adopted son. It also aids in understanding the trinitarian implications of the term "Father ('Abba, Father', Gal. 4:6; Rom. 8:15) in adopting believers into 'this new family of God.'"  Paul's adoption metaphor depicts a human—divine relationship "(God as 'Father' and Christians as 'adopted sons' as well as the relationship between adoption and the Spirit, Rom. 8:15)."  Additionally, Isaiah 9:5 gives to the Messiah the title of "Eternal Father" (avi ad) and this is to show that there is an eschatological aspect of fatherhood. The very notion still guides modern thought on authority, the family, and the nature of the relation between God and humanity. It brings out relational aspects of power and authority that are usually ignored in purely institutional perspectives. 
These father-child relations, as they are depicted in the Old Testament, therefore provide good theological insights towards understanding and correcting division on ecclesial, ethnic, and leadership bases. Old Testament fatherhood teaches authority as parental service, a paradigm of protection, teaching, and kindness. Therefore, by assuming an entirely hierarchical model of authority, church leaders are outside the biblical idea of spiritual fatherhood, which can provoke conflicts that may result in division. 
The practical implications suggest approaching ecclesial leadership as spiritual fathers who seek unity rather than division. Some biblical accounts indeed reveal rivalry between brothers, such as Cain and Abel, Jacob and Esau, and Joseph and his brothers, which can lead to family division. These patterns can recur in the church when in-groups oppose out-groups or when groups or members compete for the attention or favor of leaders. Therefore, when group identity fosters opposition to others (us versus them) or favoritism, as Jacob’s family demonstrates, favoritism and family justice, as seen in Joseph’s story, show how parental favoritism can lead to lasting divisions. In a church setting, such favoritism can manifest itself through privileged access to preferred leaders, church positions, leadership nominations, or ministries, the application of different standards to various leaders, people, or groups, and the unfair distribution of resources or opportunities. Besides, the ecclesial leadership issue of succession or spiritual heritage may cause disputes.
Conversely, the Old Testament patriarchal leadership is a good structure for reconciling families today and helping the church heal, as exemplified by the story of Joseph and his brothers (Gen. 45-50). The reality lessons underscore the need to understand that one is wrong and the other is right. This godly view transcends human sins, and a slow development of trust builds up. Given that the brothers of Joseph had confessed (Gen. 42:21), Judah made his substitutive plea (Gen. 44:33-34), and Joseph reshaped the crime as a plan of God suffering (Gen. 45:5-8), all of this knowledge leads to the pinnacle of forgiveness. Divine paternity is a model that can be used to reconcile and explain what can be done to reconcile a church, and it is dedicated to the future. These lessons highlight that God corrects but does not entirely reject, that fatherly love persists even when one is rebellious, and that they refer to gradual restoration rather than immediate change. 
Moreover, paternal authority in the Old Testament is biblical, which underscores the need to train, equip, and build up others rather than control them, because authority may cause divisions when it is possessive rather than free. The other impact of the patriarchal leadership is the aspect of maturity and independence development, since the biblical model of fatherhood aims at developing children and not keeping them dependent. As a result, churches that create dependency rather than discipleship can recreate immature spiritual parenting, leading to stagnation and conflict.
Summary and Contemporary Implications
Finally, patriarchs were mediums of divine blessing and mediators of divine election, which formed the new communal identity based on the belief in the promises of God. It is their leadership, both at the family and community levels, that fosters a culture of family over tribal identity. The theological and relational basis of the unity of Israel under a national nation was anchored in this patriarchal leadership and provided a reasonable means of conflict resolution. A spiritual maturity was established that transcended personal loyalties. Also, the Old Testament patriarchal leadership model offers theological lessons, such as the notion that the call of God is to serve rather than to be privileged. All the later covenants, such as the Sinai, Davidic, and the New Covenants, are rooted in the central tenets of the identity of the state of Israel. Moreover, the universality of the Abrahamic blessing is also oriented to the purpose of the Church to all nations (Gal. 3:8).
Thus, the contemporary church can be taught through this reconciliation process to mend existing divisions. This entails being aware of and solving dysfunctional patterns that result from family maladaptive patterns. It involves repentance, such as acknowledging that one has failed to practice spiritual fatherhood; restoration, which involves forming new relational patterns based on biblical patterns; and renewal, which involves helping to create a new vision to cross the boundaries of the past. In such an approach to church leadership, modern theological problems could be addressed by balancing authority and equality. 
The implementation of biblical models of fatherhood entails that the current church authorities consider the nurturing mandate of God and address favoritism or competition in the Body of Christ. Actual spiritual authority should reflect divine fatherhood—benevolent, nurturing, and focused on growth—especially when current leaders recognize that church divisions often mirror family problems rooted in favoritism, rivalry, and authoritarianism. Today's leaders are also called to obey and to act as channels of divine blessing to all people and nations, promoting the development of covenant communities centered on mission and ethical reflection. 
Isaac's Paradigm of Leadership and Community Formation
After Abraham's leadership, Isaac assumes a special transitional role within the patriarchal histories, serving as a stabilising medium between charismatic initiation and covenantal propinquity. Isaac is often confused with the dynamic Abraham and the dramatic Jacob, serving as a passive, weak intermediary when people exclude his unique paradigm of leadership. On the contrary, Isaac embodies stability, continuity, and peaceful community formation, teaching contemporary leaders conflict mediation, ways to navigate family dynamics, community challenges, a non-retaliatory approach without aggression, and a willingness to relocate, as well as peacebuilding, patience, resilience, relational leadership, and trust in divine provision for ultimate vindication. Contemporary leadership can also apply Isaac's willingness principles to forgo contested possessions when relationships and unity are at stake, seeking what benefits all parties, rather than aggressively fighting in hostile relationships for what one believes to be their own. 
Brueggemann compares Isaac's ability to reestablish community infrastructure (the multiple digging of new wells) to reclaiming identity and sustainability in the land, and as a sign of economic stewardship and spatial identity formation.  Additionally, Isaac exemplifies community preservation by avoiding confrontation after being driven away repeatedly (Gen. 25:19-35:29; 26:12-33). Current leadership, by applying Isaac's leadership principles, can live and work in unity, regardless of the adversaries' actions aimed at harming them. 
Moreover, the related passages illustrate God's faithfulness to the Abrahamic covenant, as well as the continuity through Isaac, divine protection and vindication amid human circumstances, and blessing despite opposition. God assures Isaac that leadership legitimacy stems from divine faithfulness, trust in God’s timing, and His ever-present presence rather than from human strategies or manipulations. Contemporary leaders must recognize that human schemes cannot thwart God's purposes, and human manipulations can never replace divine calling. Ethical requirements are always in effect because God never endorses deception; neither can He be forced or manipulated through political maneuvering in ministry, such as in dysfunctional, fractured relationships or family manipulations (as seen in Isaac's treatment of Esau and Rebekah's treatment of Jacob). 
Equally important are the agricultural metaphors, which carry deep theological meanings. These phrases are (מֵאָה שְׁעָרִ֑ים), translated as hundredfold, where the Hebrew word מֵאָה (me’ah) is a cardinal number meaning one hundred, and the term (שְׁעָרִ֑ים).  The meaning of "fold" signifies a hundred measures or a hundredfold return (Gen. 26:12), or (רְחֹבוֹת)  stemming from the Hebrew root (רָחַב) "rachab," which means "to be or grow wide, large, or spacious," or "to be wide" or "spacious." All these terms point to the divine provision of adequate space. Isaac's narrative helps understand the symbolic power of patriarchal blessing (Gen. 27) and how continuity, covenantal identity, and familial stability shape community formation in ancient Israel. 
Other agricultural metaphors in Genesis 26-27, such as well-digging, the naming of Rehoboth, and the imagery of blessings from fertile fields and dew, provide valuable spiritual and leadership lessons for community formation and development. These lessons include:
•	Patience in leadership (sowing and reaping principle, Gen. 26:18-22).
•	Endurance and trust in God's timing; just as in farming, growth attracts pests, and requires hard work; likewise, leadership growth must confront resistance that demands perseverance and patience.
•	Peaceful space and divine provision (Rehoboth "רחבות" symbolizes God's provision of unopposed room to flourish, Gen. 26:22). 
•	Wells, symbolizing life, sustainability, blessing, legacy, or future inheritance and covenant, with Isaac redigging his father's wells, representing honoring spiritual heritage and restoring covenantal legacies (Gen. 26:18). 
•	Isaac naming wells.  
All these lessons invite leaders to discern seasons of battle and rest spiritually. 
Another lesson comes from the agricultural imagery, which highlights divine fertility and fruitfulness, symbolizing divine blessing (Isaac's blessing over Jacob, which portrays the cast in agrarian terms, such as dew and fatness, and implies ongoing fruitfulness under divine favor, Gen. 27:28-29). The spiritual insight here is that leadership is not about control and dominance but about placing oneself under God's provision, resulting in what Cary L. Cooper and Ivan T. Robertson compare to "the flourishing of the community."  This resembles fertile soil. The disputed wells demonstrate the opposition's leadership development. Isaac's quiet retreat is a demonstration of adaptive leadership, power based on restraint rather than confrontation.
To make this worse, Isaac, even though there had been some tensions before, welcomed the Abimelech delegation, which asked to sign a non-aggression treaty. These subsequent dealings with Abimelech and his representatives (Gen. 26:26-31) indicate that the non-confrontational approach enhanced Isaac's regional leadership effectiveness and did not negatively affect it or diminish his position in the area. John C. L. Gibson notes that in this part of the epic, the conflicts are solved by the hero quitting.  Also, the covenantal relational contract that Abimelech and his men demanded of Isaac in the interest of Isaac hosting a feast for these previous hostile and harassing neighbors (Gen. 26:26, 31) is another revelation of how he thought of long-term, sustainable relationships, by making temporary arrangements permanent treaties.
Moreover, Isaac's perspective on leadership and community formation is revealed through the challenges he faced at home, including the Jacob-Esau rivalry and Rebekah's manipulation of the blessing ceremony (Gen. 27). The leadership values that Isaac has demonstrate his weaknesses and strengths. According to Nahum M. Sarna (1989), the effort by Isaac to bless Esau and Jacob at the same time, but was unable to make this decision, is typical of inclusive but undecided leadership that wants to avoid being biased. But he says that the fact that Isaac does not discuss the inherent incompatibility of Jacob and Esau requires more vigorous management of conflicting claims.  Some scholars, such as Gunther Plaut, Nahum M. Sarna, Victor P. Hamilton, and R. H. Charles, do not interpret Rebekah’s actions as complete manipulation or simply as maternal favoritism (Gen. 27),  but rather as a response to God's prophecy (Gen. 25:23), or as a way to compensate for Isaac’s inability to identify Esau’s true nature; thus, fulfilling divine will. This narrative highlights the importance of spiritual discernment in leaders' decision-making to avoid ethical compromise and division. 
The theological importance of Esau, who was practicing deception in the blessing of Isaac to his sons, brings out the strength of a given benediction that, once bestowed, it bears an irreversible burden. The phrase "Yes, and he shall be blessed (גַּם־בָּרוּךְ יִהְיֶה)" influences the future of nations and communities. Rebekah and Jacob's deception warns present leaders, as divine agencies, about the weight and permanence of authoritative pronouncements, and about when irreversible decisions prove problematic. Leaders' words, once spoken, cannot be retracted; they cannot undo the impact of their words. Thus, careful consideration is required before delivering public pronouncements and for personnel decisions, such as promotions, terminations, and public endorsements, as these have lasting consequences. 
The current leaders should also be wary of actions or attempts to fake favors, such as Isaac secretly trying to befriend Esau. This is a lesson: to avoid manipulation and deception, they need to be open so they are not caught in the act. They should also acknowledge that divine sovereignty is usually achieved through human failure, which teaches humility and reliance on God's redemptive purposes. This leads to dilemmas for leaders about when to accept and tolerate irrevocable, undesirable consequences as perhaps for the greater good, and when to reject them. The current leadership should take note that executive appointments, ordinations, and commissions have long-term effects that cannot be undone, because the spiritual authority given or communicated cannot be easily recalled, particularly when it falls into the wrong hands. Recommendations and endorsements open opportunities for protégés; hence, modern leaders must be wise in making decisions of an irreparable nature in accordance with God's work. 
To achieve holistic leadership development, the current leaders need to understand the concepts of adaptive leadership to manage the consequences of leadership errors. In addition, they must understand how important their authority is and how influential their decisions in ecclesial leadership can be in the long term. They must also discipline church members on parental declarations because they are confronted with situations like Isaac's, in which church members make statements about a child's future, character, and potential, which can have long-term psychological and spiritual consequences that are difficult to reverse. 
Isaac's physical blindness is used as a metaphor to symbolize the lack of complete information when making leadership decisions. It denotes the importance of judgment, checking, and modesty in making irreversible decisions. Therefore, they must learn to speak intelligently rather than to seek to reverse all the unimaginable effects. Wise leaders must develop the wisdom to transform when the environment makes them realize that they have done something which cannot be reversed. They should also acquire the ability to evaluate situations in which perceived errors can benefit broader courses and adjust their scenarios accordingly. 
Modern leaders should realize that they cannot always regulate the results of their actions and should find other ways to address inequities by making permanent decisions on behalf of those who have fallen through irreparable ecclesial decisions, as Isaac did when he was unable to withdraw the blessing of Jacob. The leadership of Isaac also illuminates contemporary pastoral and intergenerational operations grounded in preservation and faithfulness rather than domination and conquest. The success in agriculture and pastoral life of Isaac provides a paradigm for viewing prosperity as divine blessing, thus resulting in a view of material prosperity in the context of the covenant relationship rather than as individual accomplishment. The leadership of Isaac is also transitional as it is the inherited power and not the established power. 
According to Brueggemann, Isaac remains central to the continuation of the Abrahamic covenant (Gen. 26:2-5), positioning him as the inheritor of the covenant bearer, unlike Abraham, who received direct covenant promises, and as the transmitter to future generations.  Isaac did not receive new-covenant revelation but rather confirmation (Gen. 26:2-5) that Claus Westermann identifies as receptive leadership.  He had to navigate life challenges to maintain covenant identity while demonstrating his leadership integrity, revealing how inherited authority must be personally appropriated and demonstrated. Besides, his willingness to remain in the land during famine, trusting God’s instruction, exemplifies obedient leadership grounded in trust, and that divine election in leadership is not based on merit but on divine grace and promise.
To conclude, Isaac's example illustrates formative leadership that develops the community's identity through faith, fidelity to the covenant, and the continuity of sacraments. Ecclesial leadership, especially in such a form as the TAGC, should broker the purposes of God and not serve their own interests, as Isaac did in his humility and his faith to the covenant. Isaac embodies leadership stability, securing the covenantal trajectory and demonstrating covenant continuity and strength through quietness that should not be confused with passivity, as well as a faithful response to God's guidance. Rad qualifies this as the charismatic-institutional leadership tension at the heart of the Old Testament paradigm.  Isaac's leadership demonstrates covenantal fidelity, peacemaking, intergenerational faithfulness, quiet endurance of faith, patient leadership grounded in trust, faithfulness of divine promises, communal sustainability and stability, obedience, spiritual discernment, and continuity rather than dominance or charisma alone.
Jacob's Leadership Development and Communal Formation
Based on the stabilizing leadership of Isaac, the model of Jacob illustrates that genuine leadership development tends to happen during times of crisis that challenge character and mold it. Jacob's name changed to Israel (Gen. 32:28) after he wrestled (שָׂרִ֧יתָ) "sah-rah'," meaning to persist, to exert oneself, to persevere, to contend, to strive. The implication is that effective leadership encompasses one's core identity being challenged, reshaped, and transformed through difficult experiences. Contemporary ecclesial leaders, rather than projecting invulnerability, must acknowledge their vulnerability and limitations, accepting ministerial wounds that are physical marks or reminders of spiritual encounters; these insights stem from Jacob's wounded hip. Viewing the theologically and ethically rich paradigm of leadership of Jacob, its path is among the most dynamic and changing courses in the Old Testament. Jacob faced a critical decision when he was alone, without family or money, which shows that the formation of a leader may demand loneliness, self-reflection, and the absence of outside interference.
The narrative shifts from a previously manipulative pattern focused on personal gain to a direct, honest confrontation, arising from Jacob's early plotting and manipulation involving Esau, Isaac, and Laban. Authentic and effective leadership develops not through avoidance but through direct engagement with one's weaknesses, including confronting one's inner self. Then the account relates the transfer of covenant blessing from Esau to Jacob, revealing Esau’s sale of his birthright (Gen. 25:29-34) and Jacob’s theft of the blessing (Gen. 27), to the patriarch Israel, who fathers a covenant community. This reveals profound insights into how flawed leadership can be transformed through divine encounter, leadership development, moral transformation, and community identity formation. Brueggemann summarizes Jacob's leadership as an early deceptive strategy or proto-leadership manipulation, a transactional opportunist, and problematic leadership patterns that emerge through his strategic manipulation of family dynamics and covenant heritage (Gen. 25:29-34; 27), yet are instrumentally effective.  Jacob represents a strategic leader who acts decisively to secure an advantage, often disregarding ethical requirements and relational consequences. At the same time, Esau corresponds to a leadership style characterized by a natural aptitude but lacking in moral formation. 
Ethically, present leaders must transition from cunning tactics to genuine engagement. They must also learn from two dispositions exhibited by Jacob, who, although deceptive, actively sought the covenantal inheritance. In contrast, Esau embodies impulsiveness and worldliness (Heb. 12:16-17), trading this blessing for instant satisfaction, only to later lament without repentance. However, Genesis 25 and 27, which reveal Esau selling his birthright and Jacob sealing his position, point out that leadership in God's plan does not depend on hierarchy or birth order but on God's eternal plan, divine grace, God's heart desire, calling, spiritual vision, covenant fidelity, brief on God's economy (oikonmia, 1 Tim. 1:3-4; John 4:4:24; Eph. 3:21; Rev. 21:10-11).  
To paraphrase Ridges, the Hebrew term for birthright (בְּכֹרָה), which is central to leadership legitimacy in a patriarchal context, signifies the privileges of the firstborn, including family leadership, a double inheritance (Deut. 21:17), and priestly responsibilities before the establishment of the Levitical priesthood.  Bruce K. Waltke observes that the story of selling the birthright highlights the spiritual failures of Esau and Jacob, whose cunning ambitions significantly influence the development of the covenantal story.  Esau’s action is not only thoughtless but also reveals an underlying disdain for covenantal responsibilities; from a divine perspective, such a life disqualifies one as a leader.
In conclusion, Jacob's leadership is transformative, arising from personal struggles and divine encounters as he shifts from self-serving manipulation to covenantal surrender, exemplified in his wrestling with God (Gen. 32:24-30). His fight throughout the night and his refusal to unleash the divine creature until blessed are good examples of leadership developed through perseverance rather than rights. Transformative leadership thus requires patience, modesty, and the readiness to learn through crises rather than avoid them. The development of ecclesial leadership promotes leaders to face intricate issues, crises, misconceptions, and resistance.
Leadership in the Period of Judges and Community Formation
The judges' era showcases a decentralized leadership model grounded in charismatic qualities suited to specific crises. Assis Eliyahu emphasizes the chaotic, anarchic, and spiritually fragmented state during the days of the judges,  as rendered through the phrase "There was no king in Israel, everyone did what was right in their own eyes" (Judg. 17:6; 18:1; 19:1; 21:25). David M. Howard Jr. echoes, "The office of king does not guarantee a godlier nation than the mere office of prophet or priest because the problem is with the type of king, prophet, or priest, not the institution."  Contemporary leadership, such as that of the TAGC, must acknowledge that no leadership office guarantees godly churches; rather, the character of the leader is the core ingredient.
Leadership under judges emphasizes the relationship between divine sovereignty and human responsibility in community formation, as exemplified by the rejection of Saul. The lack of centralized human authority in this instance does not indicate divine abandonment. For contemporary leadership, the recurring cycle of apostasy, oppression, crying out, and subsequent deliverance (Judg. 2:11-19) highlights a crisis-response paradigm, in which the divine intervenes through human agents. Once again, biblical leadership is not about position but service, as the Judges period reveals a leadership model rooted in charismatic authority rather than institutional position.
Furthermore, judicial leadership demonstrates that leaders played covenantal roles, keeping the community united rather than pursuing individual goals. Crisis leadership, as exemplified by Ehud (Judg. 3:12–30), Deborah and Barak (Judg. 4–5), Gideon (Judg. 6–8), and Jephthah (Judg. 11–12), proved successful through divine authorization. Each battle resulted in success that affirmed the divine appointment and effectiveness of leadership, and each judge received either a direct or indirect divine commission. Moreover, these were only the military leaders. However, some judges played roles in law and administration, including dispute-solving, covenant-interpreting, and community-guiding, which led to the formation of communities and showed that their interests were based on the purposes of God, not their own. 
Finally, the era of the Judges introduces an original leadership and community model, which is crisis-driven, charismatic, and god-appointment-based, depicting how communities may maintain cohesion and identity. The main point is that the leader should be able to maintain the covenant community while allowing local autonomy and a response to the crisis that can be characterized by a decentralized leadership paradigm. An organizational dedication to values must extend beyond short-term situations. Churches and communities that follow this type of leadership model lay stress on charismatic endowment.
Other Old Testament Leadership Models and Community Formation
Along with the patriarchal examples of leadership, there are instances in which other leadership figures of the Old Testament are called "patriarch."
