However, the term is applied loosely to other biblical leaders and fathers who have played an essential role in the Old Testament. "Patriarchs," as they were called, were the pillars of the Jewish faith, and their lives formed part of the early history of the Hebrews. Although the Bible uses the expression "Patriarchs" mainly to refer to Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob or Israel, the history of numerous other fathers is also significant. This is not about the argument over terminology, but learning about the special position of Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob in the introduction of the covenant makes one acknowledge that this first paradigm cannot be repeated or imparted to the leaders who came after him. 
Joseph's Model of Leadership and Community Formation
Joseph, the eleventh son of Jacob and Rachel's firstborn, exemplifies a distinct model of leadership (Gen. 37-50) and demonstrates exceptional crisis management during Egypt's seven-year famine. He offers what Brueggemann identifies as a paradigm of "providential leadership." Moreover, echoing Brueggemann and John Goldingay, this leadership paradigm establishes a theological bridge between charismatic family leadership and national institutional leadership, operating within foreign political structures. It also teaches contemporary leaders about resilience, leadership in the face of suffering, adversity, providence, strategic foresight, and redemptive reconciliation.  Joseph's ability to interpret Pharaoh's dreams is an act of divine enabling by the Spirit, and his strategic plan of administration is a demonstration of visionary care and a long-term survival plan. Joel Paul indicates, "Leaders play a crucial role in setting the vision, shaping organizational culture, and mobilizing resources to drive sustainable innovation. Servant leadership prioritizes the well-being of the team and community, emphasizing the importance of serving others before self-interest."  The setting of such a vision, the shaping of organizational structure, and the mobilization of sustainable resources become robust when done in a team rather than by a single person, revealing the importance of ecclesial unity. 
As a complementary point, Joseph, as a Hebrew in a leadership role in Egypt, serves as a cultural bridge, encouraging modern leaders to reduce ethnocentrism, regionalism, and other forms of exclusion. By maintaining his identity in Egypt, Joseph demonstrates the principles of non-assimilation and isolation for leadership growth. Another key leadership lesson comes from Joseph's forgiveness and reconciliation with his brothers (Gen. 50:20); this sets an example for current leaders who need to move past grievances and forgive wrongs, which are crucial for inclusive leadership, while uniting the extended family into a single community. Moreover, today's leaders should learn from Joseph's leadership, which not only helped preserve Egypt but also benefited surrounding nations. Cross-cultural ministry concepts extend the church’s mission beyond its immediate communities. 
Based on these observations, Joseph's leadership model illustrates a process of building trust and a character test that allows relationships to develop naturally in community building. This is the central tenet: Joseph did not immediately identify himself to his brothers when they arrived in Egypt, but waited until later. Having come out, Joseph managed to meet his family and to remain an Egyptian governor. The leaders of the Togolese Assemblies of God Church can also reconcile ethnic loyalties and leadership without losing their identity or harboring past grudges.
In summary, Joseph's leadership demonstrates resilience, forgiveness, strategic insight, elevation through suffering, divine favor, and servant leadership that protected Israel and other nations during times of crisis. Additionally, his dedication to reconciliation and long-term vision promotes relational restoration and economic stability, illustrating how transformative leadership can operate within institutions while preserving cultural identity. Joseph's leadership within the covenant community (Gen. 45:4-8) showcases administrative excellence and cross-cultural skills, which are vital to today's church's mission. 
Moses's Model of Leadership and Community Formation
The leadership qualities of Moses are those of humility (Num. 12:3), divine calling, covenant mediation, intercessory compassion of the priestly (Exod. 32:11-14), delegation, spiritual formation, judicial oversight, prophetic authority, faithfulness to the commands of God, and community identity (Exod. 3:1-12; Num. 12:6-8). These verses demonstrate intercessory leadership, faithfulness to covenants, and God's relenting, which are paradigmatic of biblical models of ecclesial leadership and nation-building. He frees Israel from slavery, mediates the Sinai covenant, and sets up systems of government by delegated authority (Exod. 18:13-27). Rad writes, "Moses is the servant-leader, obedient to God, loyal to His people, and radically devoted to covenant justice."  The leadership paradigm of Moses provides a model for modern church leadership, especially in situations where ethnic and leadership-based divisions are rife. 
To enjoy the benefits of this leadership model and community building, present-day leaders should not take positions for status but for a divine call. This principle is exemplified by Moses' leadership, which begins with an encounter with God (Exod. 3:1-2). Olu Brown notes that after murdering an Egyptian in anger (Exod. 2), Moses underwent significant changes in his life and began to live out the vision God had for him. He observes that Moses invested all his time, energy, and purpose in God's people, even questioning how different his life might have been had he stayed in Pharaoh's palace and played by the rules.  Moses's encounters transformed his life, making him highly sensitive to God's will, as illustrated when the Reubenites and Gadites approached him to request a settlement in the wilderness that would benefit them. Brown notes that from Moses's perspective, acknowledging the moment as defining in his leadership legacy, he viewed these leaders’ demand to settle for land as not being part of God’s promise to them.  To echo Terence E. Fretheim, genuine ecclesial leadership begins with obedience to God's call, will, and agenda rather than human ambition.  Moses stands as one of the historically complex and instructive leaders who guided Israel from slavery to the Promised Land over forty years. Sailhamer H. John notes, "Moses' appointment as mediator in Exodus 20:18-21 represents a fundamental change in his role from prophet (pre-Sinai) to priest (post-Sinai), which is called into question by the frequency with which Moses acts as a mediator in Exodus 1-18."  Moses's leadership paradigm offers profound lessons to contemporary leadership theory and community formation, demonstrating how effective present leaders must navigate the tension between divine calling and human limitations. Additionally, Moses's leadership model strikes a balance between individual authority and collective participation, providing visionary direction and fostering practical governance. Thus, Moses's leadership principles remain applicable today to ecclesial leaders facing tensions between divine calling and human conflict, such as within the TAGC.
Second, present leaders should learn to redirect their followers' attention from themselves to God’s word, as demonstrated by Moses, whose covenantal mediation and law-giving (Exod. 19-24) shape Israel into a covenant community under God's rule, rather than his authority. God's covenant begins with grace and identity rather than law alone, teaching contemporary leaders to emphasize God's initiative in forming community. In Israel, the law was covenantal, not merely legalistic; therefore, covenantal ethics cultivate a holy people who reflect divine justice and love. Moses, by giving the Torah, imparted ethical and spiritual identity and formed a just and holy people (Exod. 19:6).
Third, spiritual leadership requires leaders of today to understand that there is both sacrifice and pastoral care. Moses was a model of his priestly duties as a mediator of mercy, since he was the one who constantly came to the people's rescue (even during a rebellion in Israel) (Exod. 32:11-14; Num. 14:13-19). The two texts portray Moses as a mediator when the Israelites rebelled against God with the golden calf, and how God intended to destroy the people and create a new nation out of Moses (Exod. 32:7-10). Thus, Moses plays a priestly role, desperately pleading with Israel and expressing three important lessons of his pleading that first, intercession is not restricted by any borders (Exod. 32:31-32), which shows Moses is ready to sacrifice, and his passionate desire to mediate, which his intense closeness to God can explain. Also, Moses was descending the mountain as a legislator. Nevertheless, at this moment, when he beheld Israel worshiping the beast rather than God, the lawgiver became an intercessor and laid himself down in place of the punishment of the people and prefigured the substitutionary atonement of Christ. The contemporary leaders can learn from Moses how not to stick to a single role when a situation demands otherwise. In addition, Moses also appeals to the investment of God in Israel in asking, "Why should your anger burn against your people whom you brought out of Egypt with great power?" (Exod. 32:11). Instead of letting God destroy the people who rebelled against him and take the destruction as vengeance, Moses uses the terms of "your people" and "great power", which forms the theological inconsistency when God destroys the people, whom he rescued.
The second lesson is rooted in the perception of Moses's intercessory leadership, and it is a request to God to speak in favor of the sinners (Exod. 32:12-13). Moses is a lesson for modern leaders on how to intercede strategically to catch God's eye through divine justice, mercy, and reputation. Moses pleads with the name of God (Exod. 32:12), referring to the covenant with Abraham, to what the Egyptians would say about how He does this to his people, and to the larger purposes of God. Moses grounds his advocacy thesis on God's devotion to Israel, which is founded on his solid appreciation of the divine character.
The third lesson is found in the way Moses was an intercessor, with his position between the merciful and the just God, because both he and Aaron fell face-first (Num. 16:22, 46-48), and the rebellious people who not only warrant God's punishment but also His protection. By doing so, Moses creates a defense against the verdict; this protective wall blocks devastating powers.
Fourth, today's leaders must depend on God's unwavering covenant commitments to build trust, as Moses recalls the patriarchal promises to Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob, reminding God of His oath to multiply their descendants. Moses' appeal emphasizes divine self-consistency and ongoing theological importance. Childs S. Brevard states, "the appeal to God’s oath functions as a reminder of divine character rather than divine memory."  The patriarchal promises, grounded in the theological foundation of biblical theology, establish principles of divine faithfulness, create a framework for understanding God's character and His relationship with humanity, and provide insights into the nature of divine commitments that transcend human limitations, failures, and instability.
Fifth, contemporary leaders must learn to delegate and share leadership (Exod. 18) by following Jethro's advice to Moses, which involves appointing capable leaders over groups of tens, fifties, hundreds, and thousands (Exod. 18:17-18). This approach signifies a decentralization of authority and empowers others for shared governance. Joyce K. Fletcher and Katrin Käufer note, "New models of leadership recognize that effectiveness in living systems of relationships does not depend on individual, heroic leaders but rather on leadership practices embedded in a system of interdependencies at different levels within the organization."  Christopher J. H. Wright echoes, "Wise leadership embraces team ministry and structural delegation to avoid burnout and promote collective responsibility." Wright emphasizes the central contradiction of authentic shared leadership and demonstrates that sustainable leadership is not the result of centralized control but of deliberate cooperation. He combats the common fallacy that direct oversight is equivalent to effective leadership, reminding leaders that long-term health and impact require the distribution of responsibilities, not absorption. All leaders should therefore embrace the importance of authentic teamwork, in which various individuals share their gifts, views, and skills to a common purpose. This means creating an atmosphere where all team members feel respected, listened to, and free to share their ideas. In practice, this involves frequent dialogue about vision and purpose, and role assignment to areas of strength and expertise, rather than ethnicity, familiarity, or in-group preferences. It is also critical that specific job descriptions and responsibilities be developed based on competency mapping, not on shallow task delegation. These practices not only help in averting burnout but also foster the shared responsibility that Wright has in mind. Team management ensures the team is free to express their feelings in the group towards shared goals; it entails tactical waiting so the team can be left alone to run without the individual constantly leading it. Additionally, team-shared leadership encourages accountability, group ownership of and contribution to team success, collective problem-solving, learning from others, and group improvement. The principle of team ministry is significant for understanding what Old Testament Israel was and what the New Testament Church is.
David's Model of Leadership and Community Formation
The history of David, from shepherd boy to the greatest king of Israel, presents one of the most detailed studies of leadership growth and community building. This shift is a theological landmark in the history of Israel, showing that leadership is involved in the redemptive work of God, not just in earthly success. This model illustrates how authentic leadership is developed through character development and provides a theological perspective of the kingdom of God. He built his forty-year reign that made Israel a regional power and created a single national identity that lasted centuries. 
In contrast to Joseph, whose role was redemptive, administrative, economic, and diplomatic in exile (Egypt), a foreign empire (Gen. 37-50), David's leadership credentials were not developed in royal courts. Instead, he served as a covenantal shepherd in Israel, transitioning from a tribal confederacy to a monarchy (1 Sam. 16-1 Kings 2). The leadership of David developed within the covenant society of Israel rather than within the imperial bureaucracy and combined military skills, religious piety, and oracular responsibility. 
Furthermore, Joseph's path to leadership was marked by suffering, dreams, and divine providence, while David's was shaped by divine anointing and military leadership. Thus, modern leaders should understand that leadership is commonly crafted by crisis and often by negotiating the vagaries of power and creating enduring communities with a servant-hearted governance. Leaders need to know that leadership channels are varied-Joseph rose by means of faithfulness in trials, and David rose by means of divine anointing and communal recognition.
Also, modern leaders should understand that the work of the leadership community cannot be a universal model; the work of Joseph was to supply and maintain the family of Israel in a famine, whereas the work of David was to unite the nations, centralize worship, and renew the covenant. John Goldingay points out that the stories of Joseph and David demonstrate the many-sidedness of God's rule, one of service and wisdom in exile, the other of worship and covenant in the land.  Wright repeats: Joseph is a model of vocational integrity in secular systems and a model of Christian witness in pluralistic situations.  The leadership model provided by Joseph focuses on integrity, providential trust, and adaptive leadership in exile or hostile environments, which is especially relevant in minority situations. Conversely, David's paradigm revolves around covenantal identity, spirit worship, and restorative governance, which best suits the production of national unity.
Modern leaders can also emulate David's leadership model, with its spiritual richness and political prudence, for use in ministry and pastoral care. The defects in his leadership highlight the importance of establishing a biblical community based on a common covenantal identity, righteous rule, and pious worship. According to Brueggemann, the element of David's leadership failure, moral failure, and repentance reveals that redemptive leadership never refutes failure; instead, it faces it through confession and a restored relationship with God.  Therefore, based on David's leadership insights, today's leaders should not view themselves as super-spiritual heroes. Instead, they must pay attention to their weaknesses. Acknowledging also differences in ministerial calling helps avoid competition, envy, jealousy, and conflicts over position-seeking. David's paradigm remains relevant for present-day ecclesial leadership, and contemporary leaders must prioritize holiness, spiritual sensitivity, justice, mercy, relational leadership, humble repentance, God's presence, reverence, and communal unity.
Solomon's Model of Leadership and Community Formation
The son of David, Solomon, is an example of leadership that integrates spiritual gifts, political structure, and covenant worship, which is the peak and the start of the disintegration of the united monarchy of Israel. The period was characterized by peace, prosperity, and temple-based worship, which shows that success is possible when wisdom is not obedient. The ascension of Solomon is supported by Godly election and prophecy, and his forty-year reign shows how intellectual knowledge and governmental power can co-exist with religious corruption and social injustice.
	From Solomon's priorities, contemporary leaders can learn the value of a discerning heart and the divine wisdom that comes solely from God, not from human intellect or education, nor from governing their ministry (1 Kings 3:9) when God offered him anything he desired. The Hebrew word (לֵ֤ב שֹׁמֵ֙עַ֙), "lêḇ šō•mê•a'" literally means "a hearing heart" or "a heart resolute to hear, to obey" (having power to hear), to hear with attention or interest, to consent to agree, to yield, to grant a request, and summoned to hear, etc. which Francis Brown, S. R. Driver, and Charles A. Briggs equate with a heart that perceives divine instruction and understands moral complexity beyond natural human capacity.  God granted Solomon's request because he prioritized moral discernment over personal advantage (1 Kings 3:10-14), revealing that divine pleasure stands as the proper request. The current leaders should also learn that God will be pleased with those who seek wisdom so that others can benefit rather than so that they can gain or attain power. Wisdom is a divine empowerment to govern by the Spirit, necessary in maintaining ecclesial cohesion and justice. In turn, modern rulers need to pray earnestly and humbly to be wiser in the spiritual sense of governing. In agreement with James L. Crenshaw and Tremper Longman III, the theological principles derived from the concept of a discerning heart are that wisdom is a divine gift, a prerogative, and relational rather than merely intellectual.  The theological implications of 1 Kings 3:9, alongside Solomon's request, reveal authentic leadership seeking divine assistance to serve the community's welfare, extending far beyond his historical moment to establish enduring principles about divine wisdom, human leadership, and community formation.
In conclusion, the model described by Solomon shows that God rewards humble prayers, wisdom should speak in the cause of justice, and prayerful leadership should promote the work of heaven. Godly leadership is by justice, worship, prayer, and dependence on the divine, which are the principles of life in a Christian community. Moreover, the fact that Solomon divided Israel into twelve administrative districts (1 Kings 4:7-19) suggests that effective ecclesial leadership establishes systems that decentralize responsibility, lessening over-centralization, enabling unity and joint stewardship, and fostering inclusion, resulting in sustainable resource management. Additionally, authentic ecclesial leadership starts with total dependence on God's wisdom. Wisdom listens to God and others. Solomon's leadership was both a blessing and a caution. His wisdom, justice, and temple-building advanced Israel's covenant mission. His narrative teaches ecclesial leaders not only to rule through giftedness but also in Godliness, and his split heart is a reminder to modern-day leaders to avoid idolatry, pride, lust, and self-preservation.
Prophetic Leadership Paradigm and Community Formation
Prophets such as Moses, Elijah, Isaiah, Jeremiah, and Nathan, who distanced themselves from institutional power, served as advocates for the covenant by calling on and urging kings, leaders, and the people to embrace divine justice (e.g., Amos 5:21-24; Isa. 1:10-17). Abraham J. Heschel (1962) points out that prophetic authority is based on divine appointment rather than institutional rank, in contrast to political or priestly roles.  These prophetic leaders challenged authorities to return to God, speaking truth to kings and priests (e.g., Nathan to David, 2 Sam. 12), while reminding them of mercy and the significance of faithful worship. Nathan confronted David about his adulterous behavior with Bathsheba (2 Sam. 12:1-15), illustrating that patriarchal leadership remains subject to divine ethical norms. Brueggemann notes that, despite Nathan's parable of the poor man's lamb, which successfully challenges and appreciates patriarchal leaders, it still does not question their legitimacy.  Prophetic voices challenged corruption and iniquity, called to repentance and moral reform, and offered critical moral insights. Prophetic leadership in the Old Testament provides an important corrective model that prevented the patriarchal leadership from degenerating into authoritarianism. Abraham J. Heschel, conversely, indicates that the prophets were not merely philosophers or political reformers; instead, they were deeply engaged in the pathos of God (His divine feelings and concerns for justice, righteousness, and human suffering). They did not live insensitively to evil and injustice, but were profoundly affected by moral failure. They felt God's emotions as "their ear perceived the silent sigh," referring to their unique ability to detect the suffering of the oppressed and the corruption of the powerful.  Contemporary leaders who are also deeply involved in the exact pathos of God cannot live divided.
Old Testament prophetic voices identify specific violations of covenant law and proclaim divine judgment for unfaithfulness. Amissah, Patrick Kofi, references Amos as an example of someone who passionately denounced economic and political structures marked by bribery, the denial of justice, extortion, excessive taxation, and market manipulation that oppressed the vulnerable (Amos 2:6-8; 5:12; 8:4-6), or ritual without justice (Amos 5:21-24), leading to God's disgust at empty sacrifices amid social inequity. Kofi adds that Amos advocates for sincere worship, the divine authority over all nations, and a moral consciousness that demands reform, employing visual symbols such as the plumb line (Amos 7:7-8) and rushing waters (Amos 5:24), thereby extending his critique to modern societies.  For Christians to worship sincerely, they must live in unity as one body, guided by moral consciousness and divine authority.
Finally, the paradigm of prophetic leadership emphasizes the restoration of covenant faithfulness, social equity, and ethical rejuvenation within the world of God. Modern ecclesial leaders should be biblical prophets, the moral conscience of the nation. Both current leaders and prophets must challenge injustice, confront corrupt structures, and address favoritism; in-group exclusions are social and spiritual injustices and also corruptions. God requires historical leaders to call on the past, present, and future communities to repentance and renewed identity in the sovereign reign of God. To comply with such expectations, leadership and cohesiveness are necessary. As the development of society is based on Godly holiness and social accountability, prophetic leadership, and ecclesiastical leaders should fearlessly seek to build a holistic community. 
Priestly Leadership Paradigm and Community Formation
The leadership of Priestly is concerned with ritual responsibility and the education of Torah, which spread further to Second Temple Judaism and early Christianity. This, according to Jacob Milgrom, was central in upholding the spiritual identity of Israel. Priestly leadership, in contrast to charismatic or royal leader styles, which emphasize individual vibrancy or political power, involves working through institutionalized mediation, ritual, performance, and transmission of sacred tradition. Thus, studying and examining the model of the priesthood helps show how societies are shaped and maintained through embodied holiness, liturgical engagement, and organized access to the divine being. 
The priesthood serves as a mediatory institution that shapes communal cohesion and theological memory, as Pope Innocent III, Corinne Jordan Vause, and F. C. Gardiner identify, positioned between God and humanity.  The Pope asserts that the most important functions of the priesthood are spiritual leadership and pastoral care. Milgrom, Jacob, and Janzen J. Gerald concur with Pope Innocent III that the priesthood's role is to preserve communal holiness, enabling Israel to maintain a covenant relationship with Yahweh, even if Israel's community identity was ritually forged, rather than merely performing rituals. Contemporary ecclesial leadership’s role does not differ significantly from the Levitical priesthood, as Christopher J. H. Wright indicates, which served to stabilize Israel's community by sustaining sacred memory and ritual order.  Ancient Near East, Aaronic, Levitical, and post-exilic priesthood models offer rich insights into critiques and transformations needed to build current communities.
The leadership of the priesthood does not derive from a human appointment. However, Aaron was first consecrated by divine election, and afterwards the priesthood was restored under the hand of Ezra in the post-exilic period.  The foundational principle is the call of God to Aaron (Exod. 28:1), which is paramount to the leadership of priests. This trend has shown the uniformity of principles in creating and sustaining covenant communities that exemplify divine character by meeting the human desire for structure, significance, and belonging. The combination of charismatic and traditional authority offers important insights into how spiritual groups negotiate between divine experience and institutional permanence. The Aaronic model of the priesthood shows that genuine community construction cannot be based on human merit or social positioning, personal transformation, or the formation of a corporate identity. 
Priestly leadership acts as a bridge between the sacred and the profane. For instance, Aaron and his sons entered the Holy of Holies (Lev. 16:3) on the Day of Atonement to perform rituals, allowing the community to access the divine presence. Thus, if this mediation requires the priesthood to engage in sacrificial rituals that facilitate divine encounters, protect community members, and transform the consecration of human activities into consecrated experiences, the theological meaning of this consecration is not limited to the individual appointment, but to the formation of community. Additionally, the high priest's garments symbolized communal identity, reminding modern ecclesial leaders that their ministry must visibly embody the Church's collective relationship with God. This contemporary role of priests as a mediator, with its focus on a collective relationship with God, cannot be adequately performed when split.
In short, the priestly leadership model suggests that contemporary leaders who understand the concepts of priestliness can be successful in promoting the holistic growth of their community. The present leaders should be full of holiness, faithful, and constantly prophetic to stay in touch with the issues of justice, spirituality, and the institution's competence. They must learn to deal with the current challenges of leadership and always maintain their charismatic and deep experiences with God.
Pneumatologically Leadership Framework and Community Formation
The Old Testament consistently demonstrates how Spirit empowerment leads to divine election and enablement for specific tasks, prophetic insight, and wisdom for leadership vocations (Saul and David, 1 Sam. 10:1-10; 16:13). Brueggemann notes, "David's anointing marks a critical moment when Yahweh’s governance is mediated through charismatic empowerment."  Additionally, Old Testament pneumatology anticipates a fuller realization in the New Testament (Joel 2:28-32; Ezek. 36:26-27). However, unlike the New Testament pneumatology, which presents the Spirit as universally dispensed, the Old Testament presents the Spirit as experiential pneumatology, grounded in divine action and covenantal purposes. It further presents the Spirit of God (וְר֣וּחַ אֱלֹהִ֔ים), "wə•rū•aḥ 'ĕ•lō•hîm,)" or ruach "spirit, wind, breath" as the dynamic agent in the formation, empowerment of chosen individuals, and guidance of leaders within Israel's covenantal history. 
In other words, the Old Testament discloses the Spirit's work as selective, purposeful, and oriented toward accomplishing God's covenant purposes through human leaders. Charismatic empowerment for leadership functions is exemplified through the Spirit granting wisdom, strength, discernment, or skills for tabernacle construction (Exod. 31:2-5). Julie C. Ma, Wonsuk Ma, and Andrew F. Walls observe that, despite the various traditions of the Spirit of God, they can be divided into two broader categories: charismatic (Selected leaders and prophets) and non-charismatic Spirit (The Spirit in the context of creation and wisdom). They add that God's Spirit is given to call, equip, enable, or empower one to perform a divinely commissioned task.  Lee Roy Martin notes that although the Old Testament describes God's activities in detail, creation, covenant, deliverance, and judgment, the identification of the Holy Spirit's role is often complex and implicit. He explains that the word "spirit" (Hebrew וְר֣וּחַ) itself is sometimes ambiguous. Adding to the difficulty of identifying the Spirit in the text, the Hebrew term נשׁמה ("breath"), which is found twenty-four times in the OT, can also refer to the Spirit of God (e.g., Job 32:8, Isa. 30:33)."  The Old Testament also reveals that authentic leadership is grounded not only in human natural ability or ambition but also in divine calling and spiritual enablement, offering a sophisticated understanding of leadership that fundamentally depends on such divine empowerment through the Spirit of God.
Furthermore, the Old Testament presents various typological categories of Spirit-empowered leadership, including charismatic military leaders depicted in the book of Judges, especially in the context of crisis leadership (Judg. 6:34; 14:6), prophetic leadership (Num. 11:25-29; 1 Sam. 10:6-13), royal leadership (1 Sam. 16:13; Isa. 11:1-5), and institutional leadership (Exod. 31:3; Num. 27:18-23). This empowerment is task-specific and often temporary, illustrating God's sovereignty in raising deliverers, providing revelatory insight, and bestowing communicative power that enables prophetic speech with authority (Othniel, Gideon, and Samson are empowered for liberation purposes Judg. 3:10; 6:34; 14:6). In terms of royal leadership, the Spirit provides wisdom for governance, understanding, righteous judgment, knowledge, and counsel, as well as military success, the fear of the Lord, and covenant faithfulness. Thus, the Spirit enables leaders to interpret God’s will and apply covenantal truths (e.g., Moses and the seventy elders who received the Spirit to share the burden of leadership, Num. 11:16-17, 25). John R. Levison notes that the prophetic insight and shared leadership, as illustrated by the Lord taking some of the Spirit on Moses and putting it on the seventy elders (Num. 11:25), illustrate the distributive nature of divine empowerment, delegating the prophetic capacity to share governance.  To concur with Sidney Greidanus, in the Old Testament, the Spirit is neither abstract nor universally distributed, but specifically given to some for tasks aligned with God’s redemptive mission.  Regarding Old Testament pneumatology and community formation, it is noteworthy to observe that the Holy Spirit is not self-serving but serves God's people, enabling leaders to administer justice (Isa. 42:1-4), uphold the law (Deut. 34:9), and unify the community under God's covenant rule. Contemporary leadership can learn about dependency and recognize its complete reliance on divine empowerment and humility, as seen in Moses' reluctance (Exod. 4:10-13) and Gideon's fleece-testing (Judg. 6:36-40), from the implications of Old Testament pneumatology.  
Additionally, Old Testament pneumatology challenges modern leadership models that prioritize methods over dependence, personalities over character, and achievements over faithfulness. Contemporary leaders must prayerfully discern moral integrity, recover the vision of Spirit-empowered service rather than self-serving practices, become Spirit-sensitive in their governance vocation, and avoid institutionalism that lacks the Spirit's empowering presence. Old Testament leadership demonstrates a balance between hierarchical structure and collaborative empowerment (Num. 11), revealing that shared leadership reflects divine order.
In sum, Old Testament pneumatology has focused on the empowerment of the Spirit, moral faith, and a servant-based ministry, thereby enriching our understanding of God's work through human leadership to deliver the will of God. It serves as a theological paradigm rooted in divine calling, ethical integrity, prophetic insight, and communal accountability. It points out the enabling, which is temporary and selective, with the universal availability in the New Testament through Christ. It ushers in a new era of covenant in which the Spirit gifts every believer for ministry. Moreover, it refers to Christ Himself as the thoroughly Spirit-empowered ruler who embodies all the Old Testament typology and idea of permanence of indwelling. It challenges modern leaders to lead not through strength, supremacy, power, and dominion, but through the Spirit of the Lord (Zech. 4:6). The Spirit's work goes further to develop a Spirit-led community that leads the congregation, validates leadership, makes spiritual decisions, and eases corporate worship.
Servant Leadership as Theology of the Cross and Incarnation
Expanding on the Old Testament form of servant leadership, the New Testament offers more profound insights into the theology of the cross and incarnation, thereby offering a clearer perspective on how God experienced the fullness of human constraints and this form of servant leadership. A study of the theology of the cross helps explain the suffering of Christ on the cross: God did not merely want to witness the sufferings of humankind; the cross of the cross refers to the suffering of God himself, including forsakenness and death. Notably, the cross is a symbol of the might of God in his powerlessness, victory in defeat, and insight in his seeming folly. The cross teaches contemporary leaders that suffering can be vocational and that actual authority arises from self-giving service rather than control. In contrast, examining the theology of glory leads to an understanding of God through authority and victory. 
Martin Luther advocates two ways of knowing God: through the theology of glory and the theology of the cross.  Luther argues that God's central way of revealing Himself is through suffering, weakness, and the cross, leading to glory through weakness (Rom. 8). This concept calls for viewing the Kingdom as cruciform rather than triumphalist, challenging the pursuit of leadership for status or fame. He argues that Christ represents the end of the law (Rom. 10:4), the interpretive key to all exegesis that must point to Him as the fulfillment of God's promises. Les Carter and Jim Underwood observe that the absence of moral and ethical principles and practices in leadership leads to egocentric outcomes, inner dysfunctions among leaders, and a profuse relationship between these dysfunctions and behaviors.  The cross represents suffering, self-giving, sacrificial, and redemptive love, demonstrating a radical redefinition of power, while the incarnation grounds leadership in divine identification with human weakness. 
Considering the cross (symbolizing self-emptying, forgiveness, and reconciling love, along with inspiring leaders to yield instead of grasp and to sacrifice rather than pursue self-preservation) and the incarnation (John 1:14; Phil. 2:5-11, encouraging leaders to identify with those they lead), leadership is both incarnational and participatory rather than focused on retrieval. Overall, this means that Christian leadership overturns worldly ideas of domination, power, and self-exaltation (Mark 10:45; 1 Cor. 1:18-25). For the Church today, the incarnation and the cross serve as a foundation for leadership in serving others, prioritizing the collective needs and interests over personal gain, and fostering personal growth and well-being. Therefore, considering leadership through the theological prism of the incarnation and the cross provides a profound understanding of its ethical, moral, and missional requirements.
In summary, the incarnation and the cross together frame leadership as a vocation rooted in vulnerability, service, and redemptive suffering (Phil. 2:6-8). They highlight that challenges, hardship, and suffering are redemptive, fostering resilience in leaders that offers opportunities for growth, transformation, surrender, and dependence on God rather than relying solely on personal abilities. Firstly, the cross is an example of the final service to others (John 15:13), trust and community building, and the incarnation can be seen as the illustration of empathy and bonding. Consequently, the theology of the incarnation forms the patterns of values that the leaders ought to follow, as Christ encourages them to show His love, humility, and justice in practice. 
Foundations of Ethical, Moral, Missional, and Relational Leadership
The Old Testament establishes a comprehensive framework for ethical, moral, missional, and relational leadership grounded in covenant fidelity. According to the Old Testament, the moral foundation of leadership is the relationship between God and his people through the covenant, in which leaders are supposed to represent the holiness and justice of God. Besides, the leadership of the Old Testament extends past inner affairs and serves the missional purposes as a witness to the neighboring states. Relational leadership is essential, reflecting God's covenant commitment and shared responsibility between leaders and the community. As Walter C. Kaiser Jr., Rolf P. Knierim, and Christopher Wright argue, the Old Testament offers proper guidance to ethical foundations that many present approaches overlook, establishing principles that encompass ethical integrity, moral character, missional purpose, and relational authenticity.  Biblical leadership is fundamentally ethical; thus, ethical leadership is rooted in divine justice and righteousness; it demands discernment, impartiality, and moral accountability (Mic. 6:8; Deut. 16:18-20; Ps. 89:14). Moses embodies ethical leadership, exemplifying justice and integrity (Exod. 18:13-27), while the prophets consistently call leaders to justice (Isa. 10:1).
Ethical leadership is grounded in a moral framework that emphasizes personal integrity, humility, reverence for the Lord, inner growth, justice, and righteousness. Banks and Bernice assert that biblical leadership involves God’s calling, humility, obedience, and the embodiment of God's character. They also stress that a central theme of the biblical narrative is redemption, which encompasses restoring relationships with God and humanity.  Thus, ethical leadership requires leaders to ensure that their inner character aligns with their outward behavior, ensuring their lifestyles reflect God's holiness (Lev. 19:2; 1 Pet. 1:15-16; Matt. 5:8). Leviticus reflects on relational covenant character; Peter focuses on behavior, while Jesus grounds His concept in sincerity, integrity, and spiritual vision. All these passages are ethical cornerstones of communal holiness. Peter cites Leviticus 19:2, and Jesus’s in Matthew 5:8 declares that inner purity implies sincerity, moral integrity, and undivided loyalty. In the Old Testament, Joseph demonstrates moral courage by resisting Potiphar's wife (Gen. 39:9); in the New Testament, Jesus, the ultimate leader of all time, calls for congruence between heart and action (Matt. 5-7).
The missional leadership aspect demonstrates that leaders are chosen by God, not for personal ambition, but to fulfill His mission in the world. Just as Abraham is elected within his family and called to bless the nation (Gen. 12:1-3; Isa. 6:8), the church also exists as a missional community, commissioned to the world (Matt. 28:18-20; John 20:21). Wright indicates that election in Genesis 12 is not about privilege; instead, it is for global blessing through covenantal faithfulness.  The missional mandate is grounded in the Old Testament missional covenant call to Abraham (Gen. 12:1-3). The Hebrew terms (לְךָ֛ לֶךְ־) "leḵ-lə•ḵā," translated as "Go forth," mark the foundation of Israel's communal and global redemptive mission. The central theme of this passage is covenant and blessing, revealing that leadership is based on divine election, rather than ethnic belonging or family membership, and serves a global purpose rather than in-group inclusiveness. The identity of community formation is rooted in the mission for those outside the covenant community. Therefore, current leaders must live redemptive-minded, knowing that biblical leadership is about serving God's redemptive purposes.
To concur with Waltke, the missional leadership perspective requires that all ecclesial leaders act as divine representatives, community exemplars, covenant mediators, and mission catalysts.  Essential characteristics of missional leadership include divine calling, accountability, a servant-leader orientation, moral integrity, community, and mission focus. The missional leadership paradigm emphasizes community formation as a means to fulfill the Church’s mission.
From Matthew 28:18-20, the Greek phrase (βαπτίζοντες αὐτοὺς εἰς τὸ ὄνομα τοῦ Πατρὸς, καὶ τοῦ Υἱοῦ, καὶ τοῦ Ἁγίου Πνεύματος), "baptizing them in the name of the Father and of the Son and of the Holy Spirit," grounds the mission in divine relationality. Bosch indicates, "The risen Christ’s authority universalizes the Abrahamic blessing, transforming it into a mandate for global discipleship."  John 20:21 portrays the incarnational sending or mission as the resurrected Christ appears and sends, saying, "Receive the Holy Spirit," indicating that sending is Spirit-empowered and leads to the Church's ongoing mission. This authority of Christ empowers global discipleship, obedience to His teachings, and community. 
Isaac 6:8 discloses the prophet's preparedness, which follows his cleansing (Isa. 6:6-7), and that holiness precedes appointment. It further demonstrates that prophetic leadership results from the vision of the holiness of God and a readiness to accept his mission. The call of Isaac follows theophany in the temple, which emphasizes the sublime holiness of God and the missional urgency, and therefore, modern leaders should understand that God is more interested in their change than in their sending. 
Ecclesial leadership prioritizes relational commitment within a framework of shared authority, illustrated by David and Jonathan (1 Sam. 18:1–4) and Paul’s pastoral relationships (1 Thess. 2:8). Relational leadership fosters community empathy and Christlike fellowship (Exod. 33:11; Phil. 2:1-4; John 13:34-35). 
To sum up, contemporary leaders should recognize that pneumatic leadership supports missions. Ethics, morality, mission, and relationships are essential attributes of biblical leadership. Biblical leadership is rooted in the leaders' calling, empowerment, and character. Missional leadership, rather than ambition, underpins the Church’s mission. Relational leadership fosters effective missional communities. Together, all these leadership paradigms are vital for forming and sustaining just, holy, missional, and unified people of God.
Conclusion
The Old Testament presents diverse leadership paradigms that shaped Israel’s communal identity and covenant fidelity. The key aspect of authentic biblical leadership is that, regardless of the trust and mediatorial role of Abraham, the leadership of Solomon, a combination of the spiritual gifts and the political organization, the servant leadership of Moses, and the God-love of David, genuine biblical leadership is inherently relational, accountable, and oriented towards God. The modern Church, especially the TAGC, can use these models to seek a fresh vision of servant leadership that focuses on the formation of communities, grounded in shared leadership rather than control; service rather than status; service rather than personal interests; mission rather than personal ambitions. Moreover, the Old Testament challenges the present Church to return to the ethos of leadership grounded in the justice of God, care, humility, faithfulness, and the creation of holistic, reciprocal relations that form divine-human and human-human relations. These models show that household structures, communal responsibilities, and covenantal obligations foster unity and shared identity. Also, the modern-day Church needs to focus on the call of God to service rather than on the inherited institutionality. 
Modern leaders who are knowledgeable about Old Testament leadership paradigms can positively impact the development of the community. Nevertheless, accountability is a necessary element of this leadership model, so that vainglory, self-serving behavior, and a lack of connection to a God-given calling and mandate cannot be pursued. The Old Testament worldview can be incorporated into modern leadership to support their communities with a better attitude and to meet the demands of the present generation of human beings, while remaining holy, faithful, and under God's guidance.

What Scholars and Church Leaders Are Saying
“This final volume provides a courageous and penetrating case study of the Togolese Assemblies of God Church, exposing how ethnic, regional, and historical forces shape contemporary ecclesial conflict. Kamassé skillfully integrates theology, history, and sociology to propose biblically grounded pathways toward unity and servant leadership.”
— Dr. Samuel K. Mensah, Professor of Practical Theology

Having explored the biblical foundations of unity and leadership through the life and teachings of Jesus and the witness of the apostles, we now step into the real-world struggles that churches face today. Part III turns to the Togolese Assemblies of God Church as a living case study, tracing its history, leadership structures, and internal dynamics. It brings into focus the deep tensions shaped by regional and ethnic divisions, showing how these challenges affect governance, relationships, and the mission of the Church. By linking Scripture to lived experience, this section reveals not only the roots of division but also the practical pathways toward Spirit-led servant leadership, restored trust, and unity that reflects the heart of Christ.

Togolese Assemblies of God Church Development and Structures
 
Any society that practices tribalism eventually faces inter-regional, inter-ethnic, or inter-tribal conflicts, whether verbal or physical. Examples from certain African countries, such as Rwanda, Côte d'Ivoire (also known as the Ivory Coast), and Liberia, are long-lasting, telling, and revealing. In these societies, leaders often prefer to appoint individuals from their own tribe, ethnic group, or region. Zadi Samuel states, "In fact, the groupings in parties 'politics' which were created are mostly ethnic groupings. The connection between them is not based on shared political beliefs, but rather on their shared ethnic heritage. As a result, the national bond weakens while ethnic ties grow stronger."  Sadly, churches, including the TAGC, that are supposed to set a good example for the non-Christian world to follow, fall into this favoritism. Frank D. Macchia highlights the Holy Spirit as the key factor in uniting Christians into the body of Christ, rather than ethnicity. "We as the church are the people 'baptized [in] one Spirit into one body—whether Jews or Gentiles, slaves or free—and we were all given the one Spirit to drink' (1 Cor. 12:13). According to this text, Spirit baptism not only forms the church as Christ's body but also begins its life. Notice also that Spirit baptism involves a diverse group of participants united as one."  Piper, for his part, believes that the blood of Jesus unites Jews and Greeks. Piper supports the idea that Jesus challenged the ethnocentrism of his hometown and that His mission is to end ethnocentrism. The same blood of Christ creates one new community of humanity.  Numerous theologians advocate for the Holy Spirit or the blood of Jesus as the core unifying factor of all believers. For example, Fee, Allan Anderson, Amos Yong, Asamoah-Gyadu, Simon Chan, Clark H. Pinnock, and Steven J. Land, among others, emphasize either that the Holy Spirit creates fellowship and unity, or stands as the divine agent of unity, inclusion, and reconciliation, or baptizes communities in the actual bond. In the African charismatic spirituality, some theologians argue that the blood of Jesus and the presence of the Holy Spirit represent the protective bonds. Consequently, African Pentecostals consistently invoke the blood and the power of the Spirit as sources of victory, protection, unity, or fellowship, thus constituting the spiritual bonds of Christian unity.  Kalu, the ordinary Pentecostal in Africa, is more focused on a renewed relationship with God, intimacy with the transcendental, empowerment by the Holy Spirit, and protection in the blood of Jesus, which serves as the great equalizer, creating a new covenant family in Christ. This new kingship transcends tribalism and ethnic loyalty.  Macchia or Piper also believes that the Holy Spirit or the blood of Jesus binds all believers together. All these selected theologians, such as Fee, Yong Chan, and Kärkkäinen, consider the Holy Spirit to play a key role in incorporating all believers into the body of Christ, while others, notably Chan and Land, also consider His blood. Therefore, the TAGC leaders should seriously reconsider the importance of the blood and the Spirit over ethnic considerations. Since ethnic ties are based on shared ethnicity among Christians and leaders, rather than on common bonds of blood and the Spirit, or on shared Kingdom beliefs and mission-driven goals, they need to reconsider the power of the blood of Jesus and the Holy Spirit.
Historical Survey of the Togolese Political Leadership
The historical overview encompasses periods preceding, during, and following colonization. Jennifer C. Seely and Samuel Decalo note that Portuguese explorers had a significant influence on Togo‘s history. They traveled along Togo's coast from 1471 to 1473. Before their arrival, various tribes, including the Ewe from Benin, the Mina, and the Guin from Ghana, had already settled in the coastal area. These groups had little interaction with each other, except for two small kingdoms in the north. Due to Portuguese settlement, the coastal region became a key trading hub for Europeans seeking slaves. Seely and Samuel also observe that for about 200 years, Togo and its surroundings were known as a slave market called "The Slave Coast."  Supporting this view, Robin Law explains that West African regions such as Togo and Benin formed a significant part of the eighteenth-century "Slave Coast," where large numbers of enslaved Africans were exported to European markets.  Lamin Sanneh argues that in these countries, history has often connected missionary work with business interests, thereby hindering the growth of Christianity.  Robin Law and C. R. Boxer agree that this entanglement between trade priorities and missionary presence significantly weakened the credibility and witness of early Christianity in the region. Sadly, Law notes that the Protestant Dutch during this period showed little interest in Christian missionary work in Africa—mainly because their focus was primarily on trade, partly due to a shortage of clergy, and also because Calvinistic doctrines of predestination discouraged the idea that "human agency" could be effective in converting non-Christians.  Different mission agencies, such as the Catholic, Portuguese, and Spanish groups, often compete with or challenge one another in their evangelistic efforts. These rivalries have caused divisions, and, unfortunately, their roots still weaken the body of Christ today. Current denominations struggle to cooperate in expanding God's Kingdom in Togo, as each seeks recognition, dominance, and praise. This tragic loss of passion for souls can still occur if contemporary leaders prioritize personal pursuits over God's Kingdom priorities. When leaders chase after leadership titles, advantages, and fame instead of staying mission-oriented and Spirit-led, they often end up clashing with one another.  
When World War I started in 1914, Togo became involved in the conflict. The British and French forces invaded Togo and quickly took control. In 1916, the British and French divided the territory into separate administrative zones. In 1922, Great Britain received the League of Nations mandate to govern the western part of Togo, while France was responsible for the eastern part.  After World War II, these mandates were established as UN Trust Territories. Nicoué Lodjou Gayibor notes that the cocoa boom in British Togo during the interwar period is a key event in the region‘s economic history. The economic growth was accompanied by a steady increase in emigration from French Togo to British Togo, where available land and low costs drew many settlers. The residents of British Togoland voted to join the Gold Coast as part of the newly independent nation of Ghana in 1957.  In this context, Esprit Barack explains that most Western nations have started conflicts among their citizens, communities, or religious groups within their colonies. While these rival factions opposed one another, colonial instigators often secured strategic advantages, such as increased arms sales and access to valuable natural resources. The divide-and-conquer approach continues to benefit France as Togo's former colonizer, offering the same advantages as during colonial times. However, this time it is not against a rival faction but against Togolese citizens fighting among themselves, as long as the protégés safeguard their own interests. 
Gayibor also notes that in the eighteenth century, the Danes arrived in Togo, soon followed by their slave traders. Later, the German Empire entered the region and, in 1884, formally established the protectorate of Togoland, seeking to expand its influence and secure commercial interests along the West African coast. However, as colonizers began exploring the interior of African countries more systematically, they started to interfere with each other because of their relentless thirst for territory control.  Furthermore, Gayibor notes that the effort to resolve this colonial power struggle led to the Berlin Conference of 1885, which divided Africa among European nations. As a result, Germany officially gained control of the Togolese coast. France recognized German authority in the region on December 24, 1885. Consequently, German control gradually expanded inland.  The division of Togolese territory by the great powers did not just cause problems in the past; its effects are still evident today. In Africa, superpowers continue to compete for control over the continent's resources, and France remains a significant influence on the politics of its former colonies, often seeking to manipulate countries or national political parties against one another. 
Historical Development of the Togolese Assemblies of God Church 
Modern Pentecostalism, which emerged in the early twentieth century in the United States, indirectly influenced the founding of the TAGC through the Assemblies of God's global expansion beginning in 1914. Following the establishment of the Assemblies of God (AG) in 1914, it began spreading worldwide, reaching many nations, including West African countries such as Burkina Faso and Togo. Joël Noret identifies Burkina Faso as the entry point into West Africa.  From there, the first AG missionaries arrived in the northern Togolese town of Dapaong in 1936.
Initially, the Togolese Assemblies of God Church was connected to the one in Burkina Faso, as the first missionaries and evangelists came from there. Then, in 1945, missionary A. E. Wilson and his wife established the first missionary station in Natitingou, in what was then Dahomey and is now the Republic of Benin. The year 1951 marked the merging of the two mission fields into Togo and Benin. This union lasted for a decade. Noret notes that the AG of Togo and Dahomey (present-day Benin) gradually gained independence from the American mother church. First, the AG granted autonomy to this joint mission field in 1955 during the convention held in Dapaong (Northern Togo), followed by ratification on December 7, 1956, in Tanguiéta (Benin). The first African leadership team for both Togo and Benin consisted of three members, namely a President (Pasgo Daniel Bila, a Togolese northerner), a Vice-President (Kalifa Sabou, also a Togolese northerner but settled in Benin), and a Secretary-Treasurer (Wagbe Claude Gbandi, another Togolese northerner).  Noret further explains that after its founding, the church gradually moved southward. Unfortunately, at that time, Togo had treaties in place that bound existing churches to the Federation of Protestant Churches, prohibiting any new denomination from pioneering new churches where other denominations already existed. This impeded the AG's progress toward the south. As a result, it remained in the north until after the country's independence in the 1960s. 
Following the separate independence of Togo and Benin, the two missionary fields went their separate ways. The joint convention held at Natitingou in 1965 decided on this separation. As a result, the first executive committee for Togo included a President (Pasgo Daniel Bila), a Vice President (Yeropa Joseph Bemou), and a Secretary-Treasurer (Wagbe Claude Gbandi).  Kodjovi et al. reveal that the first General Superintendent—Pasgo Daniel BILA—held this position until 1987. He then passed the baton to Mitré Djakouti, who continues to serve to the present.  One should note that the national leadership of TAG has remained in the hands of northerners for the past 68 years.  Also, as mentioned earlier, northerners have held the presidency for the past 58 years. This could be seen as either a coincidence or a sign of the church ‘s influence on the conflict of North-South dynamics.
The Northerners have traditionally held church leadership roles mainly for two reasons. First, the north was the birthplace of the church. Additionally, some Togolese Christians—both northerners and southerners—believe that northerners are less materialistic than southerners. Others also think that people from the north generally have a closer relationship with the Lord than those from the south, even if this cannot be proven. Moreover, because the soil in the northern part of Togo is not fertile, it results in hard work over larger areas to harvest a few crops, making northerners more hardworking and resilient than southerners. The latter, due to their first contact with the white man, are accustomed to bureaucracy. These factors and beliefs have influenced elections for many decades, explaining why the outnumbered Southern pastors tend to vote for Northerners over the years. Second, because the churches' agreements with other missionary organizations divided the country among themselves, the AG was confined to the northern region, preventing it from entering southern territories and establishing churches there. Consequently, this restriction from 1936 to 1960 explains why the first Christians were from the north; thus, for twenty-four years, all initial church leaders were from the north. On the other hand, the time it took for the AG to establish itself in the South and for Southerners to become capable of leading the national church of Togo is another reason for the apparent leadership monopoly held by Northerners.
Furthermore, the country has been governed for over fifty years by northerners, both politically and within the Assemblies of God church. As a result, in the 1990s, when Togo experienced political unrest amid the spread of democracy in West Africa, some Assemblies of God pastors openly stated that a change in political leadership should also lead to a change in church leadership. This suggested that a southerner should become the General Superintendent, and all northern pastors serving in the south should return to the north. Another division that hampers unity, along with signs of north-south tension, is evident in the TAG. Many Southern church members and leaders sympathize with their Southern political leaders who oppose Northern political elites. With this sympathy or even empathy, they often resent the ecclesiastical leadership of the northerners in the church. They even accuse this leadership of supporting the Eyadémas. This accusation deepens the divide within the AG’s leadership. 
Therefore, the issue of leadership change remains contentious. Adewale Banjo observes, "With references to the West African sub-region, no country has been spared the tensions and pressures associated with the succession process since the annulment of the June 12, 1993, presidential election in Nigeria."  The fact that the northerners have held power in the church for 65 years does not sit well with the southern members. They feel marginalized and powerless, similar to their political counterparts. This resentment drives them to organize along ethnic lines to change this situation. Unfortunately, biblical principles do not support this approach; instead, it reflects ethnocentrism. Christianity is not founded on nationalism; it is based on the cross, even if Bonhoeffer argues for "a Christ-centered view of  Christian community, an empathy for the exploitation and dehumanization of the proletariat, and a stance of resistance to the complicity of the bourgeois church in this class exploitation."  If contemporary leaders do not carefully manage their ethnic feelings, it can influence their Christology, ecclesiology, theology, and approach to leadership collaboration and unity. 
Ethnic and tribal divides, ethnocentrism, and hostility exist outside the church. Suppose Togolese society has not found a solution to the issue of ethnic division. In that case, the church is expected to lead in addressing social problems and providing solutions for the struggles of the Togolese. Unfortunately, the church and the Assemblies of God give the illusion of unity but are not different from the surrounding society. It appears that disciple development in local churches and the training of future leaders in theological formation centers do not shape or define the lives of Togolese Christians. 
Unspoken ethnic tensions exist among the TAG pastors, similar to those in the political sphere. Anonymous letters have circulated, warning that Togo's future remains bleak unless a sense of unity is fostered and a theological reflection is undertaken. In some contexts, ministry priorities risk becoming shaped more by ethnic loyalties than by shared theological convictions, cultural values, tribalism, and regionalism. In a conversation with the former General Superintendent in April 2012, Rev. Djakouti Mitré notes, "The Togolese Assemblies of God Church looks great from the outside, but there is an ongoing boiling dissatisfaction among pastors." Most importantly, the issue of ethnicity in Togo is connected to the question of competing loyalties.
In many regions of Togo, ethnic loyalties often overshadow other kinds of loyalty. Some church leaders have struggled to move beyond ethnic loyalties. Therefore, the current TAGC leaders need to understand what causes the lack of unity among TAGC churches and their pastors. They should also examine the conscious or unconscious factors that might foster ethnocentrism and hinder unity among pastors. Moreover, these leaders must consider whether the TAGC failed to provide a remedy for the ethnocentrism virus present in secular society. Lastly, they should work to establish and nurture discipleship that cultivates believers who can become a prophetic community within Togolese society.
In conclusion, the historical survey of the TAG shows a north-south rivalry. This rivalry between the North and South in the TAG churches needs careful examination to find practical solutions. Clearly, unity also maintains peace and prosperity within a country; it is very easy to divide people with different views and ideologies. However, when people stay united despite their differences, it becomes impossible for any force to break apart the nation. Current leaders must treat other leaders or Christians outside their ethnic group with the same grace and fairness God showed to them as outsiders of divine grace (James 2:13).
Togolese Current Leadership Structures
The religious and political leadership structures have faced ongoing tensions that reflect both continuity and change. Politically, the 2024 constitutional reforms shifted from a presidential to a parliamentary system, which the opposition has not accepted since May 3, 2025, when Faure E. Gnassingbé launched the Fifth Republic. After succeeding his father in 2005, Faure became the first President of the Council of Ministers, serving as the head of government and the key figure with executive authority. The President of the Republic, Jean-Lucien Savi de Tové, now holds a ceremonial role. 
Ecclesiastically, the Catholic Church, the largest denomination, is organized through the Episcopal Conference of Togo (CET). Catholics make up about twenty-five percent of the population and are organized into seven dioceses. Togo also hosts various Protestant and evangelical denominations, including Lutherans, Methodists, and neo-charismatic movements. Adding to these churches is the Evangelical Presbyterian Church of Togo (EPCT), founded toward the end of the 19th century and the second-largest Christian group. Another prominent church is the Assemblies of God, which has 1527 active pastors and elected its first Southern General Superintendent, Adade M. Ayi, in July 2024. In 1936, the AG had nine local churches and five pastors; today, it has 1,568 pastors, 1,500 local churches, and over 201,000 members. The AG emphasizes holistic ministry; its role extends beyond worship to actively address social needs, including microfinance, education, healthcare, and support for people with disabilities. The Baptist Convention of Togo (CBT), established in 1964, has 537 congregations with a membership of 25,403. Both the AG and the Baptist Convention offer theological training; however, the AG provides higher theological education, including doctoral degrees in philosophy, and its theological institutions serve students from twenty-six countries worldwide. 
The Togolese population consists of 42.3 percent Christians, 36.9 percent traditional animists, 14 percent Muslims, and less than 1 percent followers of other religions, amid a complex relationship between political and religious leadership. Denominations interfere with one another, highlighting the divide the secular nation faces. Muslims make up approximately 18-20% of Togo’s population and follow the Sunni Maliki school of Islam. They operate through pluralistic and decentralized structures under the Union of Muslims in Togo (UMT), led by Inoussa Bouraïma. If church leadership unites, the harvest will be plentiful, but there are still few laborers. Other independent ministries also actively participate in interfaith efforts, although their involvement is often less publicly acknowledged. 
Cultural and Social Factors Influencing Leadership Conflicts
The conflicts among Togolese leaders cannot be fully understood without considering the 40 distinct ethnic groups and their alliances, along with the deep-rooted cultural, ethnic, and social factors that influence group formations, preferences, loyalties, and rivalries. The Ewe in the south account for 32% of the population, the Ouachis, also in the south and closely related to the Ewe, make up 14%, and the Kabyiè in the north comprise 22%. The colonial legacy has created lasting tensions that continue to affect current political and religious struggles, hindering efforts to promote unity. Since independence, the country's political landscape and ethnic ties have shaped conflicts within churches, as civil society perceives authority as primarily controlled by one ethnic group at the expense of others.
Nicoué Lodjou Gayibor states that contact with Europeans led to two primary outcomes: the creation of unequal commercial exchanges, mainly through the Atlantic slave trade, and subsequent social and political upheavals, including the rise of coastal slave systems and kingdoms.  However, strong indigenous resistance arose in response to this German expansion. Even though they signed agreements with some kingdoms, many others revolted. These revolts continued to grow as the German occupiers moved further into Togo. In particular, the Kabyiè revolt (1890) and the Konkomba revolt (1897-1898) presented significant challenges for the Germans.
Essoham Assima-Kpatcha emphasizes that Germany based its colonial policy on the belief in the superiority of the white race. Therefore, the Germans did not see the natives as their black brothers. For example, the colonial administration used armed forces to subjugate the population. All the "imperial commissioners" who served successively in Togo used the same approach.  Birgit Pape-Thoma, Kangni Alem, and Patrice Nganang also reveal that the German government encouraged their settlers to use forced labor to build railroad lines and other infrastructure needed for the exploitation of Togo.  Commenting on forced labor, Ludwig Klüz, cited by Nicoué Lodjou Gayibor, writes, "The African must feel that the white man is strong; otherwise, he will never obey him. If he experiences the stick (lashes) once, he will never feel the need to do it again; he only needs to know that if needed, the stick is there, ready to be used."  Gayibor adds that Klüz agrees that whites believe blacks only obey when threatened with violence; if not, they will never feel compelled to do so again. They must understand that, if necessary, the stick is there, ready to be used. Furthermore, Gayibor indicates that colonial violence, humiliation, and authoritarian methods led neighboring colonies to witness these abuses and atrocities, earning Togo the nickname "Country of 25 lashes." The expression "And one for Kaiser," meaning an extra lash given after the twenty-fifth, became proverbial in Togo. Besides whippings, colonial justice also imposed fines, the requirement to stand still, detention of the entire clan, and even exile to Cameroon.
Gayibor also reveals that the colonizers focused more on developing the southern regions while neglecting the northern ones. This uneven development fostered a deep perception of marginalization among northern populations. He further explains that the colonizers recruited natives to use the police forces assigned to them to gain respect, collect taxes, or enforce orders from the colonial administration. As a result, the Togo colony became self-sufficient, and Germany regarded it as a "model colony."  The inhumane, racist administrative policies, deeply rooted in racism, gave the colonial powers absolute dominance over the black citizens. This sense of superiority still influences some ethnic groups in Togo who consider others as inferior or "uncivilized."
Sociological Historical Survey of Ethnocentric Biases
Before exploring some sociological efforts to reduce ethnocentric biases, it is worthwhile to examine some historical challenges that have affected Africa in general and Togo in particular. David A. Shank, in his book, "L'Afrique en crise, quelles perspectives?" [Africa in crisis, what perspectives?] emphasizes the different aspects of the crises affecting Africa. These include political, economic, social, religious, military, moral, cultural, educational, and public health issues (malaria, AIDS, etc.). Shank adds that sometimes, these crises manifest as uprisings, wars, exclusion among ethnic groups or tribes, and genocides. Such events lead to famines and severe public health crises that undermine the efforts of political and religious organization leaders.  Similarly, on the eve of its independence in 1958, Togo faced political and ethnic crises that resulted in thousands of deaths. This intensified north–south tensions and deepened political rivalries. Since the 1958 crisis, when Sylvanus Olympio gained power, the Togolese people have experienced resentment, anger, distrust, stereotyping, and suspicion. The government suffers, and politics become tense because political relationships are not based on policies but on ethnic preferences. The Church is exploring its identity and role in response to the impact of ethnic factionalism. Thus, if left unchecked in relationships, ethnocentrism can foster prejudice and stereotypes against certain out-group members. In fact, the Jew-Gentile issue in the early church's history is important for Togo's understanding of the nature of the church. However, the Togolese Assemblies of God Church has not yet actively engaged in theological reflection on this issue.
An example of stereotyping comes from Hazel Rose Markus and Alana Conner's report about a white male passenger on a flight who expressed his anger that people of color now hold leadership roles in media, sports, and government. This passenger said, "I am so sick of this. I mean, we have a Black president, for Christ's sake!"  They add that this man continued, "Just in the past ten years, we have seen the election of President Barack Obama and the appointment of Secretary of State Condoleezza Rice, Secretary of State Colin Powell, and Supreme Court Justice Sonia Sotomayor. We have also watched as Oprah Winfrey, Denzel Washington, Jennifer Lopez, and many other entertainers of color have become some of the wealthiest people in America."  They conclude that "Hazel's aggrieved seatmate does not exist solely in his opinion that race no longer matters. Studies show that most Whites believe that discrimination against Blacks is not a major problem. More than two-thirds believe that Blacks have equal opportunities in employment, education, and housing."  Benjamin Isaac further points out, "these concepts existed combined with an almost generally accepted belief in the heredity of characteristics that human beings acquired during their lives."  Isaac also reveals that if the environment shapes human character and qualities, this idea is combined with the belief in inherited traits; these false notions can become "a powerful tool justifying imperial rule." He even concludes, "the conquered must, because of their defeat, remain inferior by nature to their conquerors and then, once they have become subjects and slaves, they quickly acquire and pass on to their descendants the qualities of being born slaves as described by Aristotle."  Isaac also highlights another source of division as an "ethnic prejudice, proto-racism, and imperialism in antiquity."  He further suggests that Aristotle expanded on this theory by adding two elements that made it a valuable tool for imperialists. Aristotle believed that the Greeks held a prime position between Europe and Asia and were therefore uniquely suited to rule others. His second point is that the people of Asia naturally lived as servants or natural slaves, making them fit to be subjects of the Greeks. Roman authors adopted these ideas, properly replacing themselves as the ideal rulers.  Therefore, according to Aristotle's approach, people can pass on either positive or negative traits to their descendants, which relates to some of the current Togolese church leaders who may influence future generations if they do not overcome their existing ethnic biases.
Eugene E. Carpenter and Philip W. Comfort also explain how the ancient Greeks viewed and valued themselves. They called all others 'barbaros,' which translates to 'barbarians' in English, implying 'uncivilized' ones.  One should agree that identifying people as 'barbarians' or 'uncivilized' is a prejudice because it could lead to interpreting their verbal or non-verbal actions as polite or impolite. 
Maria Sifianou and Tzanne Angeliki observe that participants' real-life experiences and expectations regarding social norms and the concept of appropriateness in context reflect the specific subgroup's understanding of impoliteness or politeness.  Mills argues, "In politeness theorizing, the difference between impoliteness at an individual level and that at a societal level is blurred, the latter drawing primarily on ideology and stereotype."  Therefore, the Greeks' belief that they were the only truly cultured people in the world, or superior to others, reflects stereotyping. They considered themselves a pure, authentic people. Carpenter and Philip also mention that the "Romans adopted Greek culture, regarded themselves as equals to the Greeks, and also saw other languages, customs, and people as barbarians,"  a pattern of superiority that mirrors similar attitudes within some Togolese ethnic groups today. The ancient Greeks viewed themselves as the superior race and referred to all others as barbarians. Likewise, some Togolese ethnic groups believe they are better than others. The fact that the South, being near the coast, was the first to interact with white people led some Southerners to feel superior to those from the center or, worse, from the North. Negative stereotypes about others usually cause division rather than unity.
