Therefore, if current leaders want to influence future generations positively, they must avoid promoting damaging ambitions related to ethnicity and leadership dynamics, both among in-group and out-group peers.
Furthermore, the Greeks' self-view, which the Romans also adopted, along with some modern Togolese ethnic attitudes, bears resemblance to the long-standing ethnic tensions between Jews and Samaritans. Ethnic loyalty can lead to division. Jews primarily identified only with other Jews and looked down on the Samaritans. Paul Mbandi correctly observes that the adverse reaction of Jesus's disciples when they saw Him talking to a Samaritan woman, and her "astonishment," show that both Jews and Samaritans did not expect Jesus to associate with a Samaritan.  Conversely, the Samaritans also grouped themselves and believed they did not need religious instruction from the Jews (John 4:27). Carson notes that this mutual distrust reflects the deeply rooted ethnic prejudices of the period. It shows the deep division between Jews and Samaritans. If one does not handle ethnic differences and cultural loyalties carefully, they can lead to wrong motives and ethnocentrism. Wrong motives, such as evil ambitions, the desire to control relationships, and self-interest, also promote negative unity. Regarding the harmful effects of evil ambitions on unity, Francis Frangipane cites the story of Korah in Numbers 16 as a biblical illustration of how ambition-driven rebellion disrupts God's intended community order. He explains how Korah's excessive ambition led him to accuse Moses of overbearing leadership. These wrongful ambitions also led Korah to gather 250 princes in Israel to rebel against Moses 16:1-40).  The deception fueled by Korah's malicious ambitions resulted in a disaster so serious that Jude describes it as a dangerous rebellion (Jude 1:11). 
Contemporary leaders may also mobilize in-group ethnic members against out-group peers, leading to serious factional conflict. For example, in 2022, an anonymous letter was sent to all southern pastors during the TAGC general council meeting, on the eve of elections, urging southerners to unite behind a fellow southerner to vote out any northerner seeking the same leadership role.  The call for all southerners to unite behind their own during the 2022 general council elections highlights Korah's ambitions and underscores how deeply divided the TAGC leadership is. All Togolese churches, including the AG, must engage with Togo as a whole. Togo needs excellence and passion in Pentecostal ministry—spiritually exceptional individuals and ministers who recognize that they are nothing without His anointing as a universal truth. Togo will stay overlooked if church divisions continue, and without active efforts from churches to foster unity, it will become even more neglected.  
All Togolese churches need to develop new strategies that go beyond traditional methods, ethnic divisions, and leadership conflicts to connect with people from diverse ethnicities and cultures. Today's leaders worldwide must answer to spiritual authority; they have a role in God's kingdom. Church leaders are called to serve in God's kingdom rather than build their own personal kingdoms. Consequently, the mission of the Church in Togo does not require ineffective people who talk aimlessly and cause frustration, distrust, and division among others. Leaders must prepare both members and peers for church ministry and the missio Dei, rather than trying to keep them within an ethnic group for personal benefit. Leaders who do this cannot assume they possess the right spirit; instead, they need to focus on the broader vision and collaborate on the Church‘s mission.
Overall, TAGC leaders need to stop fixating on flaws and focus on God's plans for His body. They must ask God to reveal His schedule to them and follow it, rather than seeking leadership positions without understanding if it is God's timing. They should be disciplined in their walk with God. The effectiveness of ministry depends on one’s relationship and intimacy with God, not on ethnic influence. They must remain sensitive to the things of God. Spiritual preparation by the Spirit of God involves not being one's own boss but requiring personal discipline. Christian leaders must reflect God's character in their lives. They should uphold high standards in their ministry by being Godly men and women. Furthermore, all Christian leaders should value their connection to God in His Kingdom, regardless of their role, knowing that serving God through ministry is a rewarding experience. As servant leaders who are kingdom-minded, mission-focused, and committed to discipleship, they will help people love God and be willing to lay down their lives for His mission. Effective God-fearing leaders, aware of God's timing, never demand promotions or use ethnicity to achieve higher positions. When leaders start wishing they were somewhere else, they miss the joy of serving where they are. 
James Kalb warns that, regarding fundamental human rights, people must eliminate all forms of discrimination—social or cultural—based on sex, race, color, social condition, language, or religion, as such discrimination contradicts God's intent.  Contemporary leaders must recognize that the rational soul inherent in every human being and fundamental rights demand respect, acceptance, and value, which naturally promote unity. Furthermore, unity in diversity boosts morale across workplaces, families, churches, communities, and nations. It enhances coordination, fosters healthy human relationships, and encourages teamwork, leading to improved performance, higher-quality work, increased productivity, and an overall better quality of life. It enables effective communication even under challenging conditions, helps prevent social problems, and facilitates easier conflict resolution. Moreover, it upholds the equal worth of all humans, enriches and protects the country's or church's rich heritage, and fosters a sense of national or ecclesial unity among its people. Unity in diversity is essential for the development of a nation or church because a united country or community is more likely to succeed. It faces fewer internal problems than a nation or church that is socially unstable and divided on various issues. Although diversity can sometimes lead to internal conflicts, unity in diversity remains vital for peaceful coexistence among people from different cultures and backgrounds.
In conclusion, whether from a sociological or spiritual perspective, unity remains crucial because it enhances and fosters peaceful living. It also helps combine resources during disasters, leading to greater productivity. In other words, unity provides many advantages and can steer a united nation or Church toward growth, prosperity, or mission achievement. When leaders follow God's way instead of their own desires, they find peace, while the devil tries to distract them with temporary things rather than what endures. To stay effective in leadership, modern leaders must recognize that the devil will fill their schedules, making it challenging to prioritize God's agenda over their own.
Therefore, all church leaders, including TAGC leadership, must focus on building strong relationships with their families, the church, their calling, and their ministry. As pastors or leaders, they are called to be caregivers, faithful believers, shepherds, agents of change, and God's chosen leaders — not manipulative, self-centered individuals. All leaders should regularly spend time with God before taking on responsibilities or meeting with others. Additionally, leaders should not expect those they influence to see things as they do or follow them solely for personal gain; this can lead them to replace God, as such expectations may subtly elevate the leader's authority above God's guidance. Instead, they should hope that people remain sensitive to God's will and timing and are transformed by Christ. They should also be honest about their internal struggles instead of suffering in silence, which can trap them and turn them into manipulative servants.
Sociological Efforts to Promote Unity in Africa and Togo
Undoubtedly, the historical experiences of colonialism, efforts toward liberation, and post-independence issues rooted in the concept of Pan-Africanism have played a significant role in shaping the movement for African unity through organizations such as the Organization of African Unity (OAU) and the African Union (AU). Pan-Africanism aims to unite people and develop strategies to fight racial injustice, economic disparities, and other forms of inequality, with the ultimate goal of ending colonial rule across the continent. Various elements of African history, religious movements, aspirations, and community development efforts have worked to overcome postcolonial-era divisions, ethnic conflicts, and socio-economic gaps—all in pursuit of continental unity, including in Togo. Therefore, the sociological principles of collective action help explain the rationale behind organizations such as the OAU (founded on May 25, 1963), which promotes political and economic integration. The Economic Community of West African States (ECOWAS, established in 1975 by 15 West African countries) aims to foster interdependence, promote a shared West African identity, ensure fair treatment for all member states, encourage cooperative approaches to development, strengthen regional security, peace, and stability, and uphold social and economic justice for all citizens within its member nations.
Historically, ECOWAS has been recognized for its conflict mediation efforts, having intervened in Liberia, Sierra Leone, Mali, and The Gambia, thereby highlighting its collective commitment to stability. Additionally, the African Union Peace and Security Architecture (APSA) emphasizes conflict prevention, mediating civil wars and cross-border tensions, and post-conflict reconstruction. Therefore, whether it is the OAU, AU, ECOWAS, or APSA—all these initiatives for continental unity—demonstrate how unity in Africa has long been a social, political, cultural, and religious goal. However, from a Christian theological perspective, organizational unity cannot rival the strength of spiritual or divine cohesion. As a result, the African Church, including the TAGC, has a vital role in promoting unity both politically and ecclesiastically.  
Nationwide in Togo, efforts have been made through post-independence national cohesion policies to promote unity. For example, to heal the ethnic and political divides that followed the unrest between 2009 and 2012, a commission called "Truth, Justice, and Reconciliation," in French, Commission de Vérité et de Justice pour la Réconciliation (or de Réconciliation, depending on the exact context [Truth and Justice Commission for Reconciliation (or of Reconciliation depending on the exact context), was established for public hearings, confessions, and reconciliation ceremonies. It aimed to investigate past violence and promote national reconciliation after periods of conflict. Thus, the sociological goal was to strengthen dialogue between political rival groups and ethnic communities. Essohanam Batchana notes that Togo has experienced political crises, which have sometimes led to communal clashes.
Togolese acts of violence led political authorities to establish a Commission of Reconciliation in May 1963 to compensate victims of political persecution that occurred between April 27, 1958, and January 13, 1963. Forty-six years later, the Togolese government was again compelled to create another reconciliation body called "la Commission Vérité Justice et Réconciliation (CVJR)," which translates to the "Commission of Truth, Justice, and Reconciliation." The current Commission has examined political violence from 1958 to 2005 and made recommendations aimed at reconciling all Togolese. In its 44th recommendation, the CVJR proposed establishing a National Day of Reconciliation to be observed annually, renewing Togo's commitment to peaceful coexistence. Reaching a consensus on this was not an easy task. Ultimately, the CVJR suggested August 20 as a national reconciliation day, commemorating the signing of the "Accord Politique Global (APG)," or the Global Political Agreement, in 2006, although not all of its findings have been implemented. Given these difficulties in establishing a Memorial Day, does this not reflect a lack of benchmarks among the Togolese? The absence of consensus on national benchmarks remains an obstacle to building the Nation-State. 
As a result, Togo recognized controversial dates and somewhat hesitant celebrations as significant events that shaped the country's history. Agbobli, Kadanga, and Agboyibo agree on the four most important dates in Togo: April 27, 1958; April 27, 1960; January 13, 1963; and January 13, 1967.  Therefore, these dates highlight important moments in Togo‘s political history. 
Batchana further notes that major Togolese political events not only divide people, but also, between 1958 and 1959, a controversy over the date of the independence proclamation pitted political actors against each other, including Anani Santos, Minister of Justice, and his Prime Minister, Sylvanus Olympio.  The CUT-Juvento, victorious in the early legislative elections on April 27, 1958, campaigned for immediate independence. They stressed that independence should be declared without delay, preferably in 1958. Additionally, to address ethnic and linguistic divisions, French was adopted as an official language alongside Ewe, Kabyiè, and other languages.
Each political party insisted on its own date for the country's Independence Day. On April 30, 1959, the "Comité de l’Unité Togolaise (CUT)" [The Committee for Togolese Unity (CTU)], which held a majority in the Chamber of Deputies, selected April 27, 1960, as the anniversary of the 1958 elections and the nationalist victory. Batchana notes that "On May 11, 1959, eleven days after this decision, the leader of Juvento, Anani Santos, disassociated himself from the government and submitted his resignation to the Prime Minister. This action by Anani Santos, the first break between allies whose agreement had secured the victory of April 1958, marked the end of the CUT-Juvento coalition."  As a result, on August 30th, 1969, Gnassingbé Eyadéma in Kpalimé called for the creation of a significant movement, the "Rassemblement du Peuple Togolais (RPT)," [Rally of the Togolese People, (RPT)], which was formed three months later after a Congress held in Lomé from November 29 to 30, 1969. Since then, August 30 has been celebrated in Togo, not to mark the declaration of the autonomous republic of Togo, but to honor the "historical call of Kpalimé that gave birth to RPT."  If the Togolese cannot agree on major political issues, this situation underscores the deep divide that will affect various aspects of community life, including churches. The sociological split originates from the Togolese former colonizers, who exploited this division to maintain control and gain benefits. Moreover, this seed of division has not only sprouted but also grown, causing internal conflict in Togo. So far, Togolese political leaders and parties have struggled to reach agreements, which has strengthened the Gnassingbé family's hold on power and has extended the years in the presidential office.
In addition to these efforts, grassroots development, peacebuilding initiatives, and cultural mechanisms of unity were implemented. Therefore, local NGOs like "Groupe de réflexion et d’action femme, démocratie et développement (GF2D)" [Women, Democracy, and Development Action and Reflection Group], which was founded in 1992, and the "Comité villageois de développement (CVD) de Bago" [Bago Village Development Committee (CVD)], established in 1994,  played a role. All these local NGOs worked to promote solidarity and unity. There were also the chieftaincy and traditional councils that tried to mediate between ethnic groups, local communities, and the state. These councils aimed to promote unity through traditional festivals such as Evala wrestling among the Kabyiè in northern Togo, Epé-Ekpé or Ekpéssosso among the Ewe in the south, and other festivals that serve as platforms for social cohesion.
Speaking of religious circles in Togo, there was also a joint program, through religious and interfaith efforts, such as the Christian and Muslim councils, to promote unity. The Togolese Assemblies of God Church, the Roman Catholic Church, the Presbyterian Church, the Methodist Church, the Baptist Church, and other denominations often gathered to discuss ways to lead reconciliation campaigns and community mediation. Gathering in the Conseil Chrétien du Togo [Christian Council of Togo], these interdenominational councils even attempted, between 1989 and 1990, ecumenical gatherings by sharing pulpits to promote Christian unity beyond denominational lines. In 1991, Desmond Mpilo Tutu, in collaboration with the Christian Council of Togo, met with political leaders to try to reconcile them and unite the country.  Contemporary Christian leaders, including those from the TAGC, must sadly admit that although they face internal or inter-denominational divisions, they are unable to influence national relationships and unite the divided country, as outlined by the ethical and relational principles for Kingdom living in Matthew 7:1-5. Togolese Christian leaders cannot criticize political leaders' attitudes without exercising moral responsibility and acknowledging their own shortcomings. To demonstrate integrity and unity, they must first avoid double standards (James 2:13), confront their own shortcomings, and acknowledge personal leadership weaknesses.
In conclusion, efforts to promote unity in Africa and Togo involve combining institutional, cultural, and grassroots strategies. Although challenges persist, continued collaboration among political groups, traditional institutions, faith-based organizations, and civil society provides a viable yet sensitive path toward a more united and peaceful society. Therefore, Christian leaders must decide whether they can foster unity despite their divisions (Mark 12:41-44; Luke 21:1-4; 2 Cor. 8:12; Acts 3:6). Divided churches, Christians, and leaders need to acknowledge their lack of unity instead of pretending to offer it to a divided nation or world. Since God values the heart and sacrifice, as seen in Mark 12:41-44, modern leaders should reflect on their hearts, ethnic leanings, and contributions to promote unity and effective leadership. Present TAGC leaders must examine their hearts to understand the spiritual legacy they are leaving behind; they must determine if they are the current Pauls to future Timothys (2 Tim. 1:13-14; 2:2; 3:10-12; 4:2-5, 12, 15-16; 6:20-21; 2 Tim. 4:3-4). God expects each Christian and leader to use what He has given them, evaluating their service and stewardship based on what they possess rather than what they lack. God never supports infighting, exclusivism, or disunity; therefore, all leaders must address the root causes of factionalism.    
The Divide and Rule Policy in Africa and Togo
Some Western colonizers depicted Africa as barbaric or even less than barbaric. They drew borders with little regard for existing ethnic, cultural, or linguistic connections, prioritizing colonial administrative convenience over local realities. Faith Barasa notes that between 1881 and 1914, during the period known as the Scramble for Africa, several European nations took control of parts of the continent. Barasa observes, "European colonizers attained control over much of Africa through diplomatic pressure, aggressive enticement, and military invasions. European countries competed with one another to see who could attain the most power and growth. At the height of colonization, only three parts of the continent remained untouched by European settlers: Ethiopia, the Dervish State, and Liberia."  In many African countries, especially in former French colonies, colonizers used a divide-and-conquer strategy that continues to deepen ethnic divisions, helping them maintain their protégés in power. 
Once again, the roots of division planted by colonizers continue to grow today. This pattern symbolically parallels biblical imagery about seeds and their effects (Matt. 13:19–23; Mark 4:14; Luke 8:11; Col. 1:5–6). Therefore, modern leaders should reflect on and learn important lessons from the biblical imagery of seeds used to describe God's Word, which is planted in human hearts (Luke 8:11), bears fruit (1 Pet. 1:23), and spreads and multiplies (Col. 1:6). Since the Word always fulfills God's purpose (Isa. 55:10-11), leaders today should consider what kinds of fruits their words—spoken within ethnic groups—and their actions—like seeds in the hearts of younger Christians and leaders—will produce. They should carefully think about the two outcomes of the Word—"seed yield," representing future sustainability, and "bread," symbolizing present nourishment—that God guarantees will not return empty but will accomplish His purpose or bring joy (v. 11). Actions and words either build God's Kingdom or the devil's purposes. Therefore, global leaders, including those in the TAGC, can learn that their words and actions have the power to foster unity and reconciliation or cause division and ethnic hatred, much like rain nurtures different types of soil. Leaders' words and actions have current and future impacts. Words and actions today influence across cultures, tribes, and generations. TAGC leaders, as agents of change, must also recognize that where division often aligns along ethnic or political lines, the Word of God given to them as both seed and bread can serve as a common foundation that breaks down barriers and encourages unity.  
Regarding Togo specifically, after Germany's defeat in World War I, Togo was divided between France and Britain in 1919, resulting in the splitting of the Ewe homeland. Since then, reunification has remained a complex and challenging process. The traditional divide-and-conquer approach, once used in former French colonies, where leaders and their rivals are pitted against each other, continues to sow divisions in many countries. As a result, France often suppressed reformist groups capable of bringing political change, a strategy that helped France maintain influence over its former colonies. For example, President Sylvanus Olympio was overthrown and killed in 1963, and Paris signed a mutual defense treaty with Lomé the same year. Additionally, in 1986, France sent troops to support Gnassingbé Eyadéma during an attempted attack against his government, revealing France's involvement in internal affairs. African scholars and journalists often describe France’s relationship with Africa as collusive because France consistently favors current leaders who protect its interests, or intervenes in apparent coups to install leaders aligned with those interests. For example, on August 30, 2023, a military coup occurred in Gabon, resulting in the removal of President Ali Bongo Ondimba. However, Brice Oligui Nguema, who was sworn in as President on May 3, 2025, and previously assumed power as the country's interim leader after the 2023 military coup that ended decades of Bongo family rule, is France's protégé. France often tacitly endorses human rights abuses in numerous French African nations by supporting their leaders who serve France's economic interests. Isabelle King indicates, 
At the 2017 G20 summit, he told a reporter that Africa's problem is 'civilizational,' implying that something is wrong with the continent. The attitude reflected in these statements highlights the most significant barrier to achieving true African sovereignty: France's ongoing influence in West and Central Africa. Since there was no war separating colonial rule from independence, many French systems designed to exploit their colonies remained in place. Although this kind of control has decreased since World War II, France continues to exert economic, political, and military influence in West and Central Africa.  
The phrase "civilizational" reveals France's ongoing stereotypes and lack of regard for Africans. Even the financial ties and dependence on the West African franc conceal French influence over the economy, as France historically retained significant control over the CFA franc, including decisions related to monetary policy. However, reforms in recent years have begun to modify this structure.
Gilles Labarthe states that "until the 1950s, Paris mainly relied on the Ewe chiefs of the South to build networks of influence. These chiefs were sent to schools operated by Europeans and missionaries. The most talented among them became ministers of the Republic in the 1950s."  A detailed analysis of the political and economic exploitation mechanisms in Togo reveals that these networks not only supported and facilitated the 1963 coup d'état but also enabled Gnassingbé's dynasty, from Eyadema (father) and his allies, to maintain control over the country's political and economic sectors for over thirty-eight years.  Labarthe's system demonstrates how the regime, shifting from father (Eyadema, 38 years in power) to son (Faure, 20 years in power), rewarded this collusion by issuing prospecting and mining licenses, managing economic and banking transactions, and facilitating the illegal trafficking of arms and minerals. Instead of blaming Africans for the economic decline, Labarthe reveals the key mechanisms and foreign influences that have destabilized Togo's economy and social stability. Elements of this divide-and-rule dynamic appear to persist within certain ethnic relationships in Togo, and similar patterns can sometimes be observed within segments of the TAGC leadership.
 
Obstacles to Building Unity Passed Down from Colonization
Labarthe explains that Togo, a country rich in raw materials such as cotton, cocoa, coffee, phosphates, and strategic reserves of oil and uranium, was nicknamed "the Switzerland of Africa" until the late 1970s. He affirms, "Instead of blaming the Africans themselves for the stagnation, this black file demonstrates the main mechanisms and influence games that have contributed to ruining the economic and social equilibrium of a young nation for more than forty years."  Labarthe adds, "The military coup and the recent diplomatic pressures that immediately followed the death of Gnassingbé Eyadéma, who died on February 5, 2005, after 38 years of undivided rule, have further confirmed that Western leaders constantly interfere, in defiance of the principles of national sovereignty and will; thus, they impose their candidate for the dictator‘s successor and secure their interests in the region."  Besides, he affirms, "Togo has aroused, and still does arouse, the greed and rivalry of the former colonial powers of Germany, France, and England, and even the expansionist aims of America."  This harmful divide-and-conquer mentality, once employed by former colonizers to favor southern leaders over northern ones, still influences north-south relations today, even within Christian communities. Some leaders use their financial influence to sway the consciences of less wealthy pastors, thereby gaining regional or national support during elections. Others consider Southerners to be more civilized than Northerners, which they dismiss through words or actions. Conversely, some Northerners also see Southerners as greedy, boastful, and lacking spirituality. Regional biases expose the deep divide, and for years, these infectious attitudes have been passed from one generation to the next.  
Gene Taylor explains how certain biblical characters used self-interest to create a false sense of trust and unity.  For example, in 1 Kings 21, Ahab's greed caused him to take Naboth's vineyard with his wife's help. Also, in Genesis 19, Lot’s daughters plotted to get their father drunk and, over two nights, had sex with him without his knowledge. Furthermore, in Matthew 20, the mother of Zebedee's sons approached Jesus to request a favored seat or the most prestigious position in His kingdom for her sons James and John. This family's request demonstrates a focus on personal gain rather than helping others. John Maxwell and Tim Elmore describe this attitude as valuing status over service. Therefore, the desire for everything for oneself can lead to competition or infighting.  Modern leaders can also fall into the same trap by focusing on themselves instead of others. The ongoing regional and ethnic divisions sustain a persistent north-south divide, often characterized as Kabyiè-aligned versus Ewe-network opposition, reflecting the legacy of colonial boundaries that still impact Togolese unity and favor the former colonizer or current TAGC leadership. The only difference is that sometimes, France halts aid to pressure reforms, or church leaders openly deny ethnic divisions but secretly exploit them. However, the political opposition often questions whether this suspension is just superficial, allowing them to place their protégés. Members within the church group also question whether pastoral transfers to financially stable churches or better leadership positions conceal ethnic preferences.
Furthermore, focusing specifically on Ewe-Kabyiè or northern-southern issues helps clarify limitations. In Togo, the Ewe are seen as a melting pot where northerners identify all other southern ethnic groups. At the same time, the Kabyiè serve the same role for northern ethnic groups from the southern perspective. Northerners, or the Kabyiè, include anyone born and living from Anié (Atakpamé) to Cinkassé. Southerners, or the Ewe, consist of those born and living from Atakpamé to Aného. As a result, anyone born within these areas is considered either a southerner or a northerner, even if they are not from the Ewe or Kabyiè tribes.  As a result, some ethnic groups living in Togo are not included in the studied units.
It is also important to note that Togo is a multicultural West African country with about forty-five different ethnic groups. Although the French and German colonial periods made their respective languages official in schools, the late leader Eyadéma Gnassingbé later chose Ewe, a dialect spoken in the south, and Kabyiè, spoken in the north, as the languages to teach in schools. As a result, most AG churches also use both languages from the pulpit. Schools, in any society, play a vital role in communication and development. Werner Ustorf observes, "We cannot reach people at all if we do not establish the school."  Martin Schlunk agrees, "The school is in Togo, as everywhere on the western coast of Africa, the essential means to Christianize the country. Mission can do nothing without her."  However, Nicoué Gayibor states, "the necessity of training the colonized under colonial rule was tainted by ideological considerations tied both to colonial interests and the colonizers' perception of their capacities."  Choosing only two languages out of the forty-five ethnic groups raises questions. Unity makes this world a better place. In other words, respecting others' influences empowers, uplifts, supports, connects, and helps create a world that is fairer, more inclusive, and more sustainable for everyone.
Ethnic issues and relationships among TAG pastors hide the complexities of prejudice. The politics and spirit of the world are overshadowing relationships within the Church. Signs of ethnicity have been emerging for years. In July 2013, during the TAG General Council, Douti Flidja, John Walada, and I urged all present pastors to have the courage to admit that ethnic problems exist among them. These complexities can be better understood by examining the north-south history of political and church leadership from 1960 to today. Politically and ecclesiastically, most leaders since 1960 have come from the north, making it easy to compare two main ethnic groups—the Kabyiè from the north and the Ewe from the south.
Seyoum Y. Hameso suggests that if ethnicity "is criticized in daylight by African leaders who embrace it behind the scenes,"  Togolese church leaders behave similarly, with a deep-rooted divide between the Ewe and the Kabyiè. Most Togolese churches, including nearly all regional groups, exhibit ethnocentrism in various forms. This makes it difficult to see how the concept of the Church—as one family transformed by Christ, a body comprising every nation, language, tribe, and ethnicity—relates to the ethnic groups within the TAGC. The Church's failure to demonstrate Christ's love to everyone without exclusivity continues to tarnish Jesus's mission worldwide, especially in Togo. For example, during a conversation with Adama Ouédraogo on November 26, 2015, he shared the story of Oumarou Kanazoe, a well-known Muslim Imam in Burkina Faso who has invested financially in building churches, roads, and mosques. Mr. Kanazoe told him that he admires Christian teachings and the Gospel's truth, but he has no desire to convert to Christianity. His reason is that he has observed Christians claiming to have the truth about God and Jesus (Isa), but they do not love or support one another. To him, the division among Christians discourages him from becoming one of them.
To echo Volf, notes "the adjective 'non European' (in the sense of 'non-Western') highlights the distancing of 'them' from 'us.'"  In Togo, specific ethnic terms, such as Mandafrère (meaning 'you are my brother'), are used among northerners to identify group members. In contrast, the same Northerners use the term "Ahounatowo" to identify people from the south. Additionally, terms like Miatodedekoe ('it is among us') or Egindoua ('the land of the Ewe') are used among Southerners. At the same time, they often refer to Northerners as Edjiatowoe (meaning 'those from down there,' referring to the north). This term is used among Southerners to emphasize the difference between various Southern ethnic groups and Northerners. These in-group terms are derogatory, discriminatory, and based on stereotypes. Although homogeneous groups develop within ethnic communities, ethnocentrism still influences inter-ethnic relations among Togolese Assemblies of God pastors and church members.
Therefore, the history of the TAGC over the past eighty-five years includes notable achievements; however, ethnicity cannot and should not be considered a marginal issue. Within the TAGC, to echo Hameso, "as a form of nationalism, it [ethnicity] appeals to the common bonds and aspirations of a group of people. In their expression of these bonds, ethnic groups evoke unique linguistic and cultural identities vis-à-vis the others."  There is a need to address the core causes of the ethnic divide and develop potential solutions.
In other parts of the world, biblical scholarship has recently started exploring the complex ideas related to race and ethnicity. Peter C. Wagner states, "Ethnicity itself is, of course, amoral—neither good nor bad."  However, ethnicity can become a moral issue because it can unite or divide people. To agree with Wagner, even though "Christianity prides itself on being a religion of all peoples,"  and as Jesus's commanded His disciples to make disciples of all nations, ta ethne, so Christians not only identify with Jesus's but also see one another as brothers and sisters or as members of different ethnic groups (Matt. 28:19). To concur with Marilynn B. Brewer and Donald T. Campbell, who note that "intergroup, attitudes—ethnocentrism, social distance, prejudice, nationalism, tribalism are common negative factors,"  even though the TAGC should be above ethnic loyalties, they still grapple with these negative influences.
Togolese Assemblies of God Experience Church Growth Despite Ethnic Conflicts
The twentieth and twenty-first centuries saw significant growth in the Togolese Assemblies of God Church, known in French as Église des Assemblées de Dieu du Togo (EADT). Although the TAGC had a modest start, it expanded rapidly by establishing churches throughout the country, including both urban and rural areas. This rapid growth resulted in part from a strategic focus on leadership training through theological and biblical schools, with the first one established in the south (IBT-Sada) in 1976 and the second in the north (IBT-Sagbiebou), launched on September 13, 2008. Another key tool for missional growth is the West Advanced School of Theology (WAAST),  established in 1971. Its historical development occurs within a complex religious landscape and the broader narrative of the expansion of the world’s most significant Pentecostal denomination across West Africa. The TAGC is affiliated with the global Assemblies of God fellowship (WAGF), established in the early twentieth century in the United States. John C. Green and Margaret M. Poloma note that the AG was founded in Hot Springs, Arkansas, in 1914 by men and women whose lives had been transformed by the Azusa Street Revival. By the 1990s, membership had levelled off, and the initial Pentecostal excitement had diminished, leading to another revival called the Pensacola Outpouring, which started on Father's Day in 1995 at an Assembly of God church in Pensacola, Florida, offering hope to those seeking renewal in Pentecostalism.  Gary B. McGee points out that the A.G. was founded by a hardworking American class who embraced the vision of evangelizing the world. McGee also notes that there were missionaries called “one-way” missionaries—those who bought one-way air tickets, often packing their belongings in coffins because they believed they would not return to their home cities before Christ's return. Some of these missionaries thought they were bringing the Azusa Street revival to other parts of the world, but they encountered revival upon reaching the welcoming mission fields. Therefore, for McGee, the Holy Spirit was active worldwide, rather than only at Azusa Street, as most people had always believed.  Imagine modern church leaders embodying the radical dedication of early Pentecostal missionaries to the Great Commission. They would have no time for leadership infighting or chasing earthly fame. Instead, they would focus entirely on the missio Dei, eagerly awaiting Christ's return, which they believe is very near.
William Molenaar emphasizes that the WAGF, established on August 15, 1989, by delegates from various Pentecostal churches stemming from the mission efforts of the AG USA, is the worldwide cooperative organization of more than 170 AG national churches. In 2009, the WAGF reported having 63 million followers across 346,108 congregations and churches.  Building on the Pentecostal movement, which has shifted from its early emphasis on missions, the TAGC highlights several key aspects of African Christianity, including spiritual gifts, divine healing, prosperity theology, and shared leadership within the church. Additionally, Togo's political history has shaped relationships between northerners and southerners, affecting the church's overall and the TAGC in particular. Ethnic tensions and issues are visible in both secular and religious communities. A brief overview of Togo's history and the Assemblies of God churches helps clarify the country's ethnic divisions. This overview focuses mainly on the desire for leadership changes in politics and within the TAGC. The aim is to explore how this history might reveal instances of leadership conflicts and ethnocentrism between Togolese northerners and southerners, especially within the TAGC.
Potential Similar Divisions in the Bible
Some biblical passages mention similar geographic or tribal divisions, showing parallels between biblical tribal structures (tribes, clans, and families, Num. 1:2-4) and traditional Togolese organization (including ethnic groups such as Ewe, Kabyiè, Mina, and extended families). This emphasizes that both biblical Israel and traditional Togo are built around extended family units. From the early apostolic era to the present, church divisions have been standard, and various theological explanations have been offered to justify or criticize this factionalism. Major theological frameworks, from biblical interpretations to systematic theology, help clarify the causes of church divisions. For example, in 1 Kings 12, leadership arrogance, a lack of consultation, and ethnic pride led the kingdom to split into two: Judah in the south and Israel in the north. Present-day Togolese Assemblies of God leaders should learn from this story that leadership disputes, neglect of the collective body, and ethnic and regional favoritism can lead to factionalism.
Furthermore, when TAGC leaders focus on hierarchical titles, honor, positions, and prestige, they resemble Jesus's disciples who argued over who would be the greatest in His Kingdom (Mark 10:35-45; Luke 22:24). Therefore, they should reflect on Jesus's example of true greatness through servant leadership, suffering, rejection, and even death, rather than pursuing power, glory, and hierarchical ambition. They must also seek genuine spiritual insight (Mark 10:46-52) if they do not want to remain spiritually blind to humility and still crave higher positions of honor (Sit at Jesus's right or left hand (vv. 37-38)). Jesus's correction addressed to His disciples reminds present leaders that God‘s Kingdom's values flip the priorities of greatness and humility, positions and servanthood, and are characterized by service, self-giving, love, and sacrifice.
In God's Kingdom, all disciples are encouraged to avoid disputes and personality cults, recognizing that unity depends on humility and collective wisdom rather than hoarding power, and to focus on Christ rather than human status. Kingdom values and ethics transcend cultural and ethnic norms, helping to reject ethnocentrism, favoritism, and in-group biases. Therefore, current leadership should focus on developing disciples who prioritize unity over factions, a transformed life over self-exaltation, and who recognize that serving others in God's kingdom is essential. Furthermore, Kingdom ethics dismantle cultural and traditional paradigms of power that often lead to leadership-driven divisions in churches by providing a radical model centered on Christ-humility, love, and service. 
Conclusion
In conclusion, both Togo as a nation and the AG suffer from ethnic divide, especially between Northerners and Southerners. Politically, the former colonizer still influences the TAGC, which originated in early missionary efforts in the 1930s and has since made significant progress in becoming a nationwide movement, now wielding considerable spiritual and social influence. Building on its missional legacy, the TAGC has grown into a prominent Pentecostal force in Togo, leading in spiritual impact and evangelism. Its leaders have developed, and the AG, in general, is self-governing, funded, and expanding despite ongoing ethnic tensions that continually threaten its growth and unity. It exemplifies a strong spiritual focus and a successful, comprehensive mission centered on theological education and evangelism. Despite internal challenges, the TAGC continues to address the country's social, cultural, and economic issues. Current church leaders are challenged to navigate the inherited divide-and-rule mentality of former colonizers to promote unity and have a positive impact on society as a whole. 
The rapid growth requires its leaders to remain committed to sound doctrine and biblical ethics, integrating cultural and ethnic values without compromising biblical principles. Otherwise, structural complexities and ongoing leadership tensions will continually challenge the balance of authority, unity, and cultural and ethnic diversity. To address these challenges, modern leaders must prioritize service, humility, and collaborative governance, aligning their goals with the organization‘s core mission: to spread the gospel and foster unity, integrity, and effective leadership. 
Comparative Analysis of Jesus, Old Testament, and Pauline Models
 
In the history of the Church, the ecclesiology of pursuing unselfishness on Christian models of power and leadership that facilitate unity has been a priority. It is an important theological and practical problem today. The concept of a community of God's people, who declare the radical equality of all people before God, the universal necessity of salvation, and the presence of the baptism and the Spirit of the exact origin, but living divided by non-essential differences, puzzles many theologians. The Old Testament, the life and earthly ministry of Jesus, and the writings of Paul provide the Bible with very valuable principles of leadership, Christian service, power, accountability, and community building. The three sources provide crucial biblical passages that exemplify different paradigms of religious governance and communal organization, which, in other contexts, appear contradictory, making the work of scholars more difficult, particularly when ethnic tensions, regionalism, favoritism, and power brokering undermine the unity of churches. The comparison of the three main Old Testament governance paradigms, such as patriarchal, Mosaic, priestly, royal, and prophetic models, with Jesus' servant leadership, Kingdom ethic, and Pauline ecclesiology based on the cruciform, shared governance, and transformed community, assists in shaping and guiding the Church life. 
Christian ecclesiology and power offer philosophical and theological guidance for defining authority, vocation, power, and holiness, as well as for practical decision-making, accountability, conflict resolution, and church community functioning. Thus, a critical analysis of these paradigms is essential to create a biblically, theologically, and practically sound structure that can assist modern churches and leaders in addressing the ethnic and leadership-based factionalism problem. The synthesis of these three models into a practical and applicable paradigm in the present churches and leaders creates cohesiveness in the face of diversity and divisions, and uncovers the redemptive and Kingdom principles and purposes of God. It also points out continuities, convergences, discontinuities, and tensions in the creation of a covenantal, Kingdom-oriented, Spirit-transformed, and mission-oriented community. Moreover, it also provides principles that enhance present day church governance and this shows that proper and effective leadership, whether during the Old Testament, during the time of Jesus, during the early church and Pauline ministries, are based on serving other people, reconciling the conflicting members and to build the people of God based on Christian ethics, sacrificial love, mutual acceptance, peaceful coexistence, sound doctrine, and execution of the missio Dei. 
Continuities Across the Three Biblical Concepts
The Bible presents a diverse and multifaceted image of leadership and cohesion, reflected in the Old Testament, the teachings of Jesus, and the works of Paul. Although these three traditions have histories, cultures, and theologies that differ, they all share central values focused on God-centered authority, community togetherness, and servant leadership. An in-depth analysis of these three biblical concepts reveals a shared basis that underscores God as the supreme source of authority. Kings and judges in the Old Testament are described as having been selected and anointed by the authority of God (1 Sam. 16:1; Judg. 2:16). Another view on leadership and unity is also in the New Testament. The Old Testament idea of kingship is not replaced by that of Jesus but is transformed into the concept of servanthood (Mark 10:45). He also clarifies that God is the source of all authority including his own (John 19:11). This is more specifically evident in the narration of the trial of Jesus in the hands of Pilate (John 18:28-19:16) where Pilate boasts of the authority to set free or crucify Jesus his (John 19:10). However, Jesus's corrects Pilate's assumption of autonomous authority, reminding him that God grants any power he possesses. 
In doing so, Jesus' words demonstrate and redefine these chosen biblical concepts in Himself as the true King of David, reminding all humans that earthly authority derives from or is dependent on God (Rom. 13:1). Consequently, Jesus' interaction with Pilate teaches that all human authority operates under divine providence rather than independent autonomy. Misusing God-given authority constitutes a moral failure that contradicts divine intent. Carson observes that behind Pilate's 'power' (ἐξουσία, authority, right, and jurisdiction), Jesus discerns the hand of God. He adds that God's sovereignty never renders Him contingent (e.g., Gen. 50:19-20; Isa. 5:10ff; Acts 4:27-28). Köstenberger echoes by adding and citing Carson, "Jesus's response is 'typical of biblical compatibilism,' even the worst evil cannot escape the outer boundaries of God's sovereignty—yet God's sovereignty never mitigates the responsibility and guilt of moral agents who operate under divine sovereignty, while their voluntary decisions and their evil rebellion never render God utterly contingent."  This acknowledgment of God's sovereignty is reflected in Jesus's earthly ministry, His Kingship, and His obedience, which demonstrate sovereign self-possession. It underscores that His passion is not caused by human coercion; instead, it shows His willingness to submit to His Father's will, establishing Him as the trustworthy source of all authority.
Similarly, Paul's Christology of servanthood is grounded in Jesus's diakonia model and His redefinition of true greatness (Mark 10:42-45; Luke 22:24-27) as the standard for church leadership and Christian identity. Similarly, he carries this over into pneumatological community life (Phil. 2:5-11), in which the Spirit gives gifts, with the hymn of Jesus as an example, emphasizing His equality with God and kenosis (self-emptying by becoming a slave or a servant). Thus, Paul integrates the servant model into a community led by the Spirit (1 Cor. 12:4-27), motivating this community to exercise their gifts humbly as part of one body, serving each other in love (Gal. 5:13) and the common good. According to Keener, the fruit of the Spirit is the character of the Spirit of God in the person of His Son who lives in us (Gal. 4:6; see 1 Thess. 4:8), the image of God in His children.  Similarly, according to Fee, in Galatians 5:22-23, Christian ethical life is identified as the fruit of the Spirit, as opposed to the works of the flesh. Pauline ethics has a notion of walking in the Spirit, which the Spirit guides and constantly guides, yielding fruit in believers. Fee adds that the Spirit leads the believing community into proper relationships and conduct.  The Spirit builds a healthy pneumatological community by reproducing Christ's self-giving love and sustains a fellowship of mutual service and common good rather than exalting individuals (1 Cor. 12:4-11). In practice, Paul's servant-leadership paradigm urges contemporary leaders to model their leadership on Christ. Furthermore, Paul's servant leadership, coupled with his view of Spirit-empowered service, affirms Christ's model and directly challenges church divisions (1 Cor. 1:10-17).
Whether through Jesus or in accordance with the Old Testament and Pauline understanding, all that humans do, achieve, and exercise ultimately depends on God as the source of authority and empowerment. Conclusively, across all three, leadership is divinely initiated rather than self-proclaimed; covenant-oriented. It is not the in-group loyalties or ethnicity that motivates God's people. Servant leadership is not just a historical tradition but a fundamental leadership principle that must be effective in providing fellowship and community life at any age. Such is also the case of the position of the Holy Spirit in man, whether concerning salvation or any valuable and unselfish service to others. Jesus, therefore, does not abolish Israel's kingship tradition but fulfills and reconfigures it in himself as the true Davidic King, whose rule is exercised through sacrificial service rather than political power. Likewise, Paul accepts Jesus's servant model but extends it into the life of the Spirit-empowered community, where Christ's self-giving love shapes relationships, authority, and mission.
In addition, the persistence of the three concepts indicates that Old Testament leadership was founded on covenant principles (Deut. 17:14-20; 1 Sam. 10) that mediated covenant blessings (Num. 27:15-23). This implies that kingship in the Old Testament is a divinely appointed leadership, but it also shows how this leadership may at times be corrupted and become a monarchy. This was a shepherd-like leader (Ezek. 34:1-6). The leaders, such as David and Solomon, earned power by creating a perfect king and covenant faithfulness (Ps. 72), whereas the leaders, such as Rehoboam, caused the division of the nation (1 Kgs. 12). The character and form of authority are also telling. To start with, the Old Testament form of authority is based on institutionalism, hierarchy, and theocracy (Samuel and David). Second, the authority that Jesus exhibits is personal, charismatic, and messianic, which reaches its climax on the cross as the supreme act of mediating unity. Pauline power is apostolic, functional, and delegated, and follows the model of Jesus. 
Equally, the Old Testament often reflects hierarchical and centralized forms of leadership, though with prophetic checks and communal elements. Servant leadership, on the other hand, is concerned with serving God and people with authority. The prophet also has the responsibility to speak against injustice and to remind people of their promises to the covenant. Similarly, Jesus's leadership style is based on serving, being an example, and convincing others. Consequently, His leadership model is another, rejecting the pagan approach that relied on coercive authority, status, and dominance, as well as moral authority, in favor of the right place. Paul is more pastoral in his leadership style, focusing on teaching and collaboration with elders and deacons, while remaining humble and ready to suffer. The interconnection of the three concepts implies that leadership must be mission-oriented, aimed at propagating the mission of God to the world rather than being self-serving. 
Accountability to God is another aspect of continuity related to these three concepts. In each of the three models, leaders are subject to divine judgment, as Old Testament leaders were (such as Samuel to Saul or Nathan to David). Jesus fully demonstrated obedience (John 8: 28) and Paul refers to leaders as stewards (1 Cor. 4:1-5). These three models have been demonstrated to be legitimate by the Mosaic paradigm (Exod. 3-4; Num. 27:12-23), which emphasizes spiritual discernment and institutional process. The other element that justifies the Old Testament models is the significance of anointing, heredity, and military achievement. The Jesus model, on the other hand, is founded on messianic signs, teachings, and resurrection. In contrast, the Pauline model is founded on the apostolic calling, the establishment of the church, and the significance of revelations. Consequently, in the case of the TAGC, power cannot be self-proclaimed or even ethically delegated but must be granted by rightful rulers who are divinely appointed (as seen in the Davidic paradigm in 1 Sam. 15; 2 Sam. 17) and validated by society. Although the Davidic covenant confers continuous legitimacy, effective authority in the modern world must be aware of current structures while leaving the timing of transition to God. The succession of prophets Elijah to Elisha (1 Kings 19:19-21; 2 Kings 2:1-18) illustrates how leaders today ought to mentor their successors intentionally. Good mentorship brings unity and not division. 
It is also worth noting that, given the unity among the three paradigms, one can identify key mechanisms that bind them together. These are a sense of shared values and mission, and a sense of Christ that goes beyond personal or ethnic loyalties. The apostolic continuity is that the standards of doctrine and practice are maintained, and the interdependent operation takes account of and appreciates the varied contributions.
Thus, Unity in the Old Testament is rooted in shared ancestry, covenant identity, land, and communal practices. The approach of Jesus is, however, on the relationship with Christ, and that of Paul on the presence of the Holy Spirit in the Church. Another way the Old Testament expresses unity is through the chosen people, united by the Torah and ritual practices. This concept of unity is not specific to the Old Testament, the era of Jesus, or the era of Paul, but it applies to the Church in the present day. The unity is rooted in the membership requirements of the covenant with YHWH; Jesus promotes unity in the Kingdom to all people, and Paul declares that no boundaries limit unity. Consequently, the limits to such unity are primarily ethnic and geographical (the 12 tribes of Israel), and the temple was the centre of religious unity. Spiritual unity bypasses ethnic and social divisions in the case of Jesus, and the unity of the Spirit in the bond of peace in the case of Paul. To Jesus, unity is founded on mutual love (John 13:13), as He prayed for it in John 17. To Paul, the universal Church, which transcends local communities, has to be a unity in diversity.
Similarities Across the Three Biblical Concepts
What unites these biblical examples is an overarching biblical ideal of leadership that rejects domination and self-interest, even though not all leaders consistently live up to this standard. Throughout history, godly leaders have directed their subjects back to Yahweh as the real eternal King. Consequently, all leaders, including those of TAGC, must reflect the reign of God in service, selfless love, and the building of a reconciling community. They should also bring their followers back to Yahweh as the real and permanent King, and not to cultic ethnic tastes. The overlap of the three frameworks demonstrates a similar pattern in the Bible, whereby leadership is there to maintain unity, lead the people of God in faithfulness, and demonstrate God's values in human society. Other similarities relate to divine authority, which holds that leadership originates from God, not from human ambition, even though it requires responsible human participation, and to the duty that compels leaders to defend and strengthen the community. In addition, the three concepts not only promote unity but also reflect the will of God, or divine will. In the end, every epoch in these three concepts must struggle to break the barriers of that period.
Overall, all three views highlight the value of moral integrity in leadership legitimacy. Righteousness and justice among leaders are stressed in the Old Testament (Deut. 17:14-20), and Jesus emphasizes the need for a pure heart, mercy, and the promotion of peace (Matt. 5:3-12). Paul, however, provides the qualifications of character of elders (1 Tim. 3:1-7). The Old Testament promotes unity in a community through a common covenant, law, and worship; the same is true of Jesus, who also promotes unity through common participation in his mission (John 17:20-23). Paul, on the contrary, develops it using the body metaphor, which emphasizes the significance of interdependence. Although similarities can be observed, significant differences are evident as well.
Key Differences Across the Three Biblical Concepts
Despite the similarity in the theological background of the Old Testament, Jesus, and Paul on leadership and unity of the people of God, they differ significantly in their approaches. These variations are based on the historical backgrounds, covenantal concepts, and theological emphases that informed each approach. The Old Testament has seen leadership being centered on kings, priests, and prophets whose leadership was based on divine appointment and covenant promises. The primary purpose of leadership was to preserve Israel's national identity and religious piety within the Mosaic covenant.
Conversely, the revolution Jesus brought to the idea of leadership was his rejection of old forms of power and his promotion of servanthood, humility, and selfless love. His Kingdom operated on values not derived from earthly political systems, even though it still had real and transformative implications in the present world, and His leadership philosophy was to unite people, promote inclusivity, and use the cross as a symbol of power. This oneness under Jesus was not only restricted to ethnic Israel but was also to all who came under the reign of God by faith, and the area of leadership and community became far more extensive.
Paul, who wrote in the conditions of the early Christian communities, redefined the model of Jesus through the prism of a spiritual and communal structure. In contrast to the Old Testament formalities or the role Jesus played as the Messiah, Paul offered a vision of collective leadership through spiritual gifts and interdependence within the community. Unity was not realized through political influence or mere imitation of Christ, but through acknowledging Christ as the head of the body and the Spirit as the generator of different yet complementary ministries.
Consequently, the three frameworks vary in their centers of authority, approaches to unity, and theology. The Old Testament focuses on the covenantal kingship and national cohesion. The example of Jesus is a servant leadership and reconciling fellowship. Besides, Paul promotes gift-based leadership that the Spirit leads in the global church. Though complementary, these differences show how God's plan for leadership and unity was developed progressively over the course of salvation history.
Jesus's Distinctive Approach Emphasis
Unlike many Old Testament expressions of leadership, which often involved formal or hierarchical roles, Jesus's leadership philosophy emphasizes authority through intentional downward movement, as the incarnation illustrates this descent from divine to human (John 1:1-3, 14-18; Gal 4:4; Phil. 2:5-8) or from teacher to foot-washer (John 13). Echoing Cooper and Ayers, Stephen R. Covey differentiates leaders from managers, observing, "Leadership focuses on doing the right things; management focuses on doing things right."  Jesus, through His suffering, contrasts His leadership approach with the worldly one, where leaders demand service, to show that His leadership is not hierarchical but relational. As the Servant-King,  He did not elevate Himself above others; instead, He led from below, lifting others (Matt. 11:28-30; Rom. 8:15-16). His model shows that He did not lead the group in an egocentric fashion, but instead He shared meals, walked dusty roads with His disciples, and even felt tired along with His disciples (Matt. 6:31-32; 8:23-24; Luke 22:45; 24:13-15; John 4:6-8), not above. He united them (Matt. 20:28).
Contemporary leadership may draw insights from Jesus‘ approach to leadership, which emphasizes transformation over mere direction or imitation. By sharing His heart and mission with people, He demonstrates that authority is grounded in a relationship with God, not in authoritarian control (Matt. 20:25-28). An excellent example of this type of leadership is shown by Jesus, who washed the feet of His disciples (John 13), rebuked self-serving leadership (Matt. 23), mentored and empowered His disciples (Luke 10; John 17). The main message of Jesus is not to provide his followers with only leadership tools, but to change their character. He cultivated their personality by instructing them, not by mentioning their weaknesses, such as impulsiveness (as in the case of Peter, who was prone to speaking or acting without thinking, Matt.). 14:28-31; he even reproved Jesus, Matt. 16:21-23, and proposed to make three memorial shelters, Matt. 17:4, or cut off the ear of the servant of the high priest, John 18:10). After claiming loyalty, Peter denied knowing Jesus three times (Matt. 26:69-75). Likewise, Thomas' doubt (John 20:24-29), to whom Jesus responded by inviting him to touch His hands and side and telling him not to doubt, teaches a valuable leadership lesson about meeting people’s doubts with compassionate clarity. James and John's selfish ambitions, such as calling down fire (Luke 9:51-56) when a Samaritan village rejected Jesus, or seeking the highest honors (Matt. 20:20-28; Mark 10:35-45), also teach important lessons about setting aside self-ambition to serve others, which reflects true greatness in God's Kingdom.
Other than the leadership style of Jesus, His perception of unity emphasizes radical inclusion, not sameness, but a common purpose and love for one another, not in-group preferences or conventional barriers. He also demonstrates how to resist social prejudices, as Jesus chose Matthew, a tax collector, and Simon the Zealot as His disciples, thus showing radical inclusion in creating a new social order. The presence of two very different characters on the same team is a lesson for the current leadership to overcome ethnic, social, or cultural diversity and unite around shared values and beliefs. It also underscores that unity of heart with Christ is more significant than worldly struggles (Matt. 10:3; Luke 6:15).
Not merely that, but Jesus extends His concept of unity by including women such as Mary and Martha (Luke 10:38-42) and open-minded women who accompanied Jesus, served Him, and financed His disciples. This shows the active position of women in ministry and their importance in the kingdom of God. The role of female evangelists is highlighted by the fact that women were the first to see Jesus in his resurrection and preach about it. In the meantime, the male disciples had been somewhat hesitant to share the news (Luke 24:1-11). He also challenges social boundaries by inviting individuals of other, lowly economic status, including Peter and Andrew, fishermen of the working classes, and such individuals as Joanna, wife of Chuza, a manager in the household of Herod, and Susanna, demonstrating how the economic classes can come together in the kingdom work of God.
Jesus also discusses the negative side of competitiveness, factionalism, and self-interest, which are likely to separate people rather than bring them together. Sharing meals, as in the case when He attended a banquet at the house of Levi with tax collectors and sinners (Matthew 9:9-13; Luke 5:29-32), and sharing a meal with Zacchaeus, a chief tax collector who was rich (Luke 19:1-10), He used mealtime experiences to encourage Peter to eliminate the barriers between Jews and Gentiles (Acts 10:15-28). This shows that He supports the establishment of informal environments that help build authentic relationships. Continuing with the significance of the communal meals to the early Christian community, the contemporary leaders should acknowledge that God does not discriminate between Jews and the Gentiles in bringing salvation that cuts across human boundaries. This is later explained by Paul in the Lord's Supper as the bond in Christ (1 Corinthians 11:17-34) and by Luke as profound fellowship and communion in Christ (Acts 2:42-46). The concept of unity as taught by Jesus is special because it entails inclusion (Jews, Samaritans, women, and Gentiles) and the demolition of separating walls (Ephesians 2:14) through His blood, which unites people irrespective of their races, statuses, and backgrounds.
Finally, the leaders of the TAGC today must show sincere reconciliation because of their common identity in Christ, promote unity, make efforts to overcome ethnic and geographical differences, and focus on inclusive relations rather than group prejudices. They must also reflect the unconditional acceptance of Jesus, which promotes transformation by regularly examining the relational, cultural, or structural barriers that hinder people from fully participating in leadership roles, serving, or supportive positions. This way, they will be humble in serving people without titles, invest in people, live authentic lives, be of integrity, welcome people into a Christ-centered world, and encourage harmony. Modern leaders can still incorporate insights from all three biblical models: the Old Testament, Jesus's servant leadership, and Pauline Spirit-led leadership, appreciating that biblical leadership has changed from the structured covenant of the Old Testament to the relational servant leadership of Jesus to the Spirit-empowered, shared leadership of Paul. Nevertheless, the objective is the same at any age, including the current age: to glorify God through a unified, converted, holy, and mission-oriented community of the redeemed. 
Contextual Suitability of Each Model
Each of the leadership models and strategies of church unity was created to address specific issues, which is why they can be applied to the specific context of churches. Their topicality may be complex or even problematic at times. However, their knowledge will enable one to avoid the over-spiritualization of leadership actions and decisions, as well as to guard against neglecting biblical wisdom. The leaders should be called spiritually and not self-appointed or put in office by politics or ethnic preferences. The establishment of formal oversight boards offers transparency, boundaries, and accountability frameworks, which restore trust. The contemporary relevance is based on the reverence for covenant identity, the holiness of God, order, responsibility, and the call to leadership. A comparative summary and practical description of the presentation of church leadership and unity in Scripture across the three biblical periods is helpful for the church's approach today. 
First, to prevent the syncretistic approach, the present-day leaders should keep in mind that ancient Israel was a theocratic state. According to Longkat Daniel Dajwan, "Theocracy is a government in which God has supreme authority over the affairs of human beings through human agents whom God chooses. This can be explained in the biblical history of ancient Israel. Under the theocracy, the human leaders' work is supposed to apply the laws and directions that God has given."  According to Hermann Cohen, under such a theocratic regime, "The unity of God serves as the prototype of the peoples to the extent that they establish their unity in humanity as the purpose of their historical existence."  Benjamin Pollock indicates, "Cohen points to the Israelite demand for a kingship as signaling the downfall of the Israelite theocracy."  The centralized, covenant-based leadership model was effective for an ancient tribal society that required a strong sense of identity, unity, and protection in a hostile environment. Leaders had to maintain the nation ‘s identity, uphold the covenant, and defend against external threats. This led to centralized authority, which helped unite the people (similar to David's unification of the tribes). At the same time, priests and prophets worked to keep the people spiritually connected to God's purposes. As the people transitioned from nomadic tribes to a unified kingdom, unity was mainly based on shared ethnicity and nationality. However, when leaders failed morally or spiritually, this unity also crumbled (as seen in the divisions following Solomon).
Dajwan asks if the principles of theocratic governance, as depicted in the Bible's account of God’s relationship with the ancient Israelites, can be applied to non-Biblical contexts, such as Nigeria.  Today, leadership is usually democratic, elected, and merit-based, rather than inherited through divine right. This means the Old Testament model of centralized leadership is less relevant in our modern world, which requires collaborative, accountable, and servant-led leadership. Similarly, authority is now accountable to the people, laws, and institutions, rather than to God and His Word. Nevertheless, like the Old Testament prophets, leaders of today need to be answerable to God and truth, not to popularity and fame. Pluralism and multiculturalism have been used to define political unity and national identity, transcending national, ethnic, and religious boundaries. In churches and locally, unity is based more on a common mission, values, and fellowship in the world, rather than on tribal or national identity. The unity of the modern church should not be based on tribal or national affiliation but on a Spirit-directed Christ-oriented orientation, which would be more consistent with the teachings of Jesus and Paul. 
Second, the existing leadership model should not overlook the significance of leaders, as depicted in the Old Testament. Leaders were there to protect covenant faithfulness, ritual purity, and national security and political justice. Nowadays, leaders, whether secular or spiritual, are supposed to be visionary, serve others, inspire change, and enable participation. In general, God invites all leaders to create a culture of reverence, faith, and honor towards God. They are to focus their work in the church on pastoral care, discipleship, conflict resolution, and global missions rather than on protecting national borders. 
Third, the extent of unity is essential. The Old Testament unity was marked by exclusiveness, whereby the covenant identity of Israel and its isolation from other countries were emphasized (Deut. 7:6). The country came together under a common covenant (Ex. 19:5-6), although the tribes were divided. They united in national adoration, in war, or in renewing, like when David was king. The root causes of disunity were idolatry, injustice, and disobedience. Today, unity is inclusive, particularly in the church. This spiritual family accommodates individuals of diverse cultures, languages, and ethnicities (Gal. 3:28). Unity is mission-oriented, with a common cause, and places a bias on teaching through example rather than teaching. In addition, united meetings around prayer, Word, and sacraments are indicative of OT national worship festivals. The world Christian community must be united across continents, cultures, and denominations.
Fourth, the principle of the mission of God should be implemented within the Church, where pastors are to be more people-centered than program-centered and service-oriented rather than status-oriented. They are also supposed to change their audience-based ministry into a conscious discipleship and leadership training. In the Pauline model, leaders in the present day must empower elders and ministry teams and encourage collective decision-making rather than pastor-driven leadership. Leaders must train others to be ministers (Eph. 4:11-13), including church planting (Titus, Timothy), leadership training (Acts 20:28), and plurality of elders (Phil. 1:1).
Conclusively, from the OT leadership model to the teachings of Jesus, and up to Paul's views, the story is the same: God wants His people to reflect His personality through holiness, mission-driven leadership, and unity, powered by the Spirit. The OT provides a hierarchical, covenantal framework of leadership, founded on obedience and reverence, which is God-ordained. Even though unity was weak and hampered when leaders failed to maintain God's standards, He continued to raise leaders, prophets, priests, and kings called to restore His people to Him. Jesus did not abolish Old Testament principles of authority but redeemed and redefined leadership through servanthood and sacrificial love, grounding it in servant leadership, favoring lower positions over thrones, and emphasizing the cross rather than control and domination. He points out relational unity by redefining leadership as an act of love, inclusion, and sacrificial service. He formed a new society, His Church as a spiritual family, not an institutional hierarchy.
Paul also extends the model of Jesus to provide a framework for common, Spirit-led leadership, as observed in the early Church as the Body of Christ. Thus, learning the applicability of each model within its corresponding context helps develop denominational governance and contributes to unity. The unity of Christians and the Spirit is not on an ethnic, social, in-group, or traditional basis, but a unity of faith in Christ and Spirit baptism- including a continuing divine presence. This view provides stability to the TAGC and all the churches across the world at any time. It promotes leadership grounded in the character of God, the humility of Christ, and the indwelling of the Spirit. In addition, it brings about pragmatic unity. This unity is not of organizations and conformity but of relational connectedness founded on truth, love, and a common God-given purpose. 
Consequently, TAGC must embrace leadership that serves rather than controls or manipulates. They need to pursue unity that honors diversity while upholding biblical truth and avoiding uniformity imposed by ethnic or political pressures. They are also supposed to re-align their Christian lives to the heart of the Father towards oneness, the example of Christ, and the guidance of the Spirit. However, contemporary leaders must be cautious of the traps of leadership: emphasis on structure may result in inflexibility rather than service, as in the case of Moses; emphasis on limitation of innovation and responsiveness on loving as did Jesus; and the emphasis on in-group or regional favoritism can create a us-versus-them attitude rather than an emphasis on the Kingdom of God, as was the case with Paul. 
Bureaucracy may also serve as an end, not a means, to achieve the mission of God, which is to establish the Church as one body, one hope, one Lord, and one faith. The fundamentalist error that modern leaders should avoid is the belief that there is a single biblical model that applies to every situation, which leads to syncretism. They must also avoid pragmatic approaches that allow cultural pressures to override biblical principles and spiritual discernment. For practical application, TAGC leaders and all church leaders should adopt a contextual approach, striving for a balanced understanding as they apply these timeless biblical principles to address current challenges.
Potential Challenges in Implementation
Leadership models from the Bible, such as the theocratic authority of the Old Testament, Jesus' servant leadership, and Paul's teachings on church organization and the Holy Spirit, have influenced the Church for centuries and continue to inspire and challenge Christian leaders today. As Laaria Mingaine said, neglecting these challenges would make it harder for modern leaders to incorporate these three ideas into their training and development. This would only widen the gap in leadership knowledge and deepen existing inequalities between different groups.  However, moving from these biblical texts and ancient times to the modern complex realities of life raises important questions that require careful consideration. Typically, when applying these models, today's leaders often encounter biblical and theological challenges. For example, the Old Testament's theology highlights the roles of kingship, prophecy, and priesthood. Meanwhile, Jesus’s theology emphasizes servant-kingship, and Paul grounds his ecclesiology in the metaphor of the body and his leadership on a collaborative approach. As a result, if today's leaders ignore cultural, social, and political differences, the contextual gap could make their implementation outdated.
