Alternatively, leaders might choose a selective approach to authority and kingship for personal gain, resulting in a lopsided hierarchical structure rather than a unified leadership model. Contemporary leaders might find it challenging to decide whether the approach is the norm, a lesson from various theological traditions, or a manifestation of the conflict between ideals and reality. In the Old Testament, there is a covenant-based leadership model (Deut. 17). Meanwhile, the highlights of Jesus are mission-oriented (Mark 10:45), and the highlights of Paul are bodybuilding (1 Cor. 12); thus, it is challenging to combine mission and unity in a practical leadership model.
Conversely, the TGAC can encounter specific problems related to national problems. An example is that ethnic and regional allegiances (north vs. south), the colonial tradition of divide-and-conquer (the source of factionalism in leadership), and the hierarchical structure of institutions (including a bureaucracy that monopolizes services and holds the group together) may be obstacles to implementation. Moreover, change resistance (some leaders favor existing systems), discrepancies between verbal and nonverbal messages, generational differences (between the preferred ways of older and younger leaders, collaborative and hierarchical), and the global influence of Pentecostalism (imported ideas that are not adjusted to local conditions) can become obstacles.
The Old Testament is the source of leadership and unity, as God is depicted as the supreme leader who led Israel through Moses. This is a covenantal leadership ordained by God and characterized by a strong relationship orientation, because God's people live in obedience. Jesus also portrays himself as the perfect servant-leader who made the Kingdom of God inclusive and emphasized leadership as a relational and spiritual practice (John 17). This self-sacrifice, humility, sacrificial love and inclusivity is reflected in the Pauline model, which centers on the Church as the body of Christ (1 Cor. 12). According to this model, leadership is functional and charismatic, and the spiritual gifts and leadership positions are directed at mutual development (Eph. 4). Furthermore, contemporary leaders are struggling to interpret, balance and apply these biblical models at both the global and local levels. To strike a balance, structural hierarchies, in-group biases, resistance to change, and the interpretation of Scripture need to be addressed. Leaders must take the lead and foster unity in their own environment.
In essence, true Christian unity is the elimination of tribal divisions —the way the Old Testament unified the 12 tribes, the cultures of Jesus united Jewish and pagan cultures, and Paul united the Greek and non-Greek ones. Modern Christians must overcome the prejudices of political (such as the north-south divide in Togo or left-right divides in other countries), socio-economic, generational, and denominational gaps. Leaders will never achieve absolute unity without accepting diversity as a source of strength rather than a danger, and focusing their attention on personal relationships rather than secondary doctrinal arguments. They should be able to make room to discuss complex matters in light of these principles. 
Conventionally, the local churches must establish diverse leadership teams comprising individuals of various ages, genders, backgrounds, and skills. They ought to also invest in training leaders at all levels, not just the top, and form relationships with neighboring Christian communities. The strategy will empower Christians and leaders at all tiers to become reconciliation agents in times of community conflict, raise their voices against injustices, and develop solutions that will disrupt systems of domination and division. In this way, contemporary leaders will be able to emulate authentic Christian leadership by integrating the Old Testament as a source of structural wisdom, the relational approach of Jesus, and the missionary vision of Paul. Such a combination can establish societies where real power leads to the emotional and spiritual development of all, and where togetherness is an explicit declaration of the Gospel in a world where people are divided by differences.
The church of today should adopt a biblical and holistic concept of authentic leadership and oneness. This methodology must incorporate various models, such as the authority of the covenant in the Old Testament, the example of Jesus, and Paul's teaching on church and spiritual leadership. Both models have their own peculiarities and are grounded in their historical and theological backgrounds. Consequently, the church leaders must develop a balanced approach to exercising authority that prioritizes serving others over self-interest. This practice must be based on flexibility, authentic servant leadership, and the desire to serve the community and take informed decisions that not only benefit their reputation. Leaders are also expected to promote, tolerate, appreciate, and empower others of all kinds rather than abuse power to benefit themselves.
Above all, contemporary church leaders must understand their weaknesses and vulnerabilities, admit that they are not perfect, and instead of trying to excuse their errors, attribute them to serving the purposes of the Kingdom. They should also make efforts to ensure that their personal and professional personalities align with their teachings. Practically speaking, in training future leaders, senior leaders ought to model Christ's acceptance of failure, as He did with Peter's denials (Matthew 26:69-75, Mark 14:66-72, Luke 22:55-62, John 18:16-18, 25-27), but not to presume that they have forever disqualified themselves. Instead, they ought to view errors as an opportunity to grow and be coached just as Christ did (Luke 9-10).
Jesus's leadership model takes a radically different approach to hierarchical authority, dominance, and power. Instead of basing them on traditional notions, it roots them in the ethics of the Kingdom, including servant-kingship (Matt. 23:11), humility, sacrificial love, and service (Matt. 20:25-28; Mark 10:45; John 13:1-17). It also emphasizes inclusivity (John 17:20-23) and rejects hierarchical dominance. As Steven Crowther points out, "servant leadership is grounded in spiritual principles, and focusing on biblical concepts could help inform other leadership styles as well."  Jesus’s also emphasizes leadership as cross-bearing, kenotic (self-emptying, Mark 10:45; Phil. 2:6-8), mission-driven for the redemption of others (Matt. 28:18-20; John 20:21) or a Spirit-anointed mission (Luke 4:18-19), and reconciliation aimed at restoring broken relationships between humanity and God as well as among people (Matt. 18:15-17; 5:23-24), or resolving conflicts within the community (Luke 19:10; John 17:21-23). 
Paul, living in a world prone to conflict, uses the metaphor of the body (1 Cor. 12:12-27) to emphasize diversity and interdependence, centered around shared oversight (Acts 20:28), mutual submission (Eph. 5:21), and the common good (1 Cor. 12:7). He also advocates for a church leadership approach focused on building up the community, emphasizing flexibility over dominance, and a leadership style that is both charismatic and functional, aimed at equipping believers (Eph. 4:11-13) rather than asserting positional authority. Furthermore, Paul states that his apostleship is a direct gift from God, supported by the idea that Christ grants all charisma and offices for the benefit of the Church (Eph. 4:11-12). Additionally, Paul stresses that leadership should prioritize unity and relational harmony (2 Cor. 5:18-19), drawing on Jesus’s teachings and death as the foundation for leadership and reconciliation.
Three principles would show that practical, authentic leadership is not self-promotion, boasting, or ego, but instead serving others, assisting them in overcoming conflicts, and collaborating to establish the people of God in love, truth, and accordance with divine principles. God is the ultimate source of leadership because the Hebrew Bible describes a theocratic model of monarchy, which was adopted by the early Church, constructed on it, and transformed. The Old Testament views leadership as a covenant between God and his people. The table below brings out the differences and similarities between these principles.
Conclusion
The comparison of the Old Testament foundations, the embodiment of Jesus, and the models led by the Spirit in Paul's teachings shows that leadership and unity are not only internal matters of the church, as TAGC discusses, but also essential to the continued work of God in the world. The Bible encourages unity as a universal requirement, stating that Israel is one nation under God (Deut. 6; Ps. 133), and that it is called to live in holiness and obedience, which encourages unity. Jesus, in turn, asks all believers, regardless of their backgrounds, to be united (John 17) and to be spiritual, inclusive, and mission-driven. Another fact to mention is that Paul reminds us of unity in diversity as Spirit-empowered and Christ-centered (Eph. 4:1-6), evident in the unity of Jews and Gentiles and in the application of spiritual gifts and roles. On the same note, every concept in the Bible centers on a missionary concept. An example of this is the Old Testament, which talks of Israel as the light of the nations (Isa. 49:6), and unity and leadership are expressions of the glory of God to the world. Similarly, Jesus sends His disciples to go and make disciples of all the nations (Matt. 28:19-20), equips them to be missionaries, and does so in love and oneness. In the case of Paul, the purpose of apostolic leadership is to build churches and the cohesion of the witness to the work of reconciliation and redemption of Christ. This discussion also highlights what God expects of all Christians and leaders in the past to become Spirit-dependent, servant-hearted, Spirit-empowered, and Spirit-led followers. Further, God invites all believers in the Old Testament and in eternity to cohesive living through humility, forgiveness, respect, sacrificial love, and mutual submission to achieve peace. In history, every church was called to focus on its mission rather than self-interest. Thus, the present leaders, such as those of TAGC, must include these models. They ought to take into account the origin of their leadership; whether they are appointed by God, based on divine Sonship, or given spiritual powers. Also, they should assess the type of leadership they have, whether it is obedience to God, seeking justice, servant leadership by self-sacrifice, or empowered shepherding. Thus, all biblical leadership ultimately aims to reflect God’s holiness, foster unity, and advance His mission in the world.



Final Reflections and the Way Forward
 
This book suggests that a comparative analysis shows the biblical view of leadership and unity provides a clear but progressive insight. It is manifested differently in the Old Testament, in the teachings of Jesus, and in the theology of the church of Paul. The Old Testament mainly focuses on leadership founded on covenant and national identity, vested in kings, priests, and prophets who safeguarded Israel's identity and devotion to Yahweh. The Old Testament model worked well in uniting people under the authority of God, but it was likely to be abused by self-centered leaders who did not regard their duties as covenants. By contrast, Jesus's perception of church leadership and unity builds on these traditions and alters them, adding fundamental teachings that Paul subsequently codified for practice. The authority that Jesus did not abolish was transformed: its origin and manifestation changed, and it was no longer positional power but character influence. Theological concepts, therefore, shifted in the Old Testament from external compliance and institutional order to the internal change of Jesus through incarnational servant leadership, which then led to a broader vision of organizational coordination through common spiritual authority in Paul.
Jesus embodied servant-kingship, redefining greatness through humility, sacrificial love, and reconciliation. His vision of unity is beyond ethnic and social boundaries, but grounded in the fact of God's Kingdom. By emphasizing the cross, He reveals His perception of leadership and unity, and these two can be achieved and sustained through service and unselfish love.
Paul expanded Jesus's vision to help the early church develop. His leadership style, founded on the Spirit and community, divided power within the hierarchy and emphasized the diverse gifts of the believers. He showed that the only way to be united is through diversity, where Christ is the Head and the Spirit is the glue that binds believers, irrespective of social status and origins. This ecclesiology presented an adaptable paradigm of multicultural societies across the Greco-Roman world.
Understanding these models helps modern churches evaluate their own leadership dynamics. In the Togolese Assemblies of God Church, these comparative lessons point to the pitfalls of ethnicity-based, in-group preference, domineering, politicized, and tribal leadership styles. The leaders of the modern generation should know that internal divisions can reflect the failures of kingship, as seen in the Bible or in contemporary politics, rather than the Christ-centered, Spirit-empowered vision of the New Testament. Following the servant leadership model of Jesus and the community inspired by Paul's pneumatology can be seen as a corrective solution that would allow the church to demonstrate unity in diversity and to witness to the Kingdom of God in Togo.
Jesus's Distinctive Insight into Unity and Leadership
The redefinitions of leadership and unity through the notions of incarnation and the cross are evident since Jesus focuses on inner change rather than on outer rules or hierarchical order. He promotes genuine spiritual associations that transcend traditional, cultural, and ethnic boundaries. Leadership is no longer about exercising power, but about serving, and proper authority is grounded in Spirit empowerment, character change, self-emptiness, and sacrificial love, and not status, power, and coercion.
The Johannine prayer (John 17:20-23) expresses Jesus' vision of unity, the bond between believers, and the inner unity of the Trinity. This is not just an organizational unity. Likewise, it focuses on relational harmony grounded in an everyday divine life. This idea is also known as "incarnational unity" and is used to describe believers who share the same Spirit, are involved in the work of Christ, and show divine love in their relations.
Jesus shows that absolute authority is expressed through self-restraint and sacrificial service, not domination, providing a practical model for resolving leadership conflicts. Finally, Jesus points out that an effective Christian influence lies in serving others' needs rather than pursuing self-interest. Such a form of leadership is called a cruciform leadership, which provides a theological basis for overcoming the divisions associated with leadership, cultural, and ethnic clashes.
Addressing the Togolese Context
Applying Jesus's and Paul's models requires evaluating how local realities influence church life. In the Togolese Assemblies of God, leadership, cultural, and ethnic divisions lead to a massive loss of touch with the Jesus vision of unity and leadership, which is not in line with the essence of the Church and the Christian faith. When leaders prioritize personal profit, institutional authority, cultural or ethnic superiority, rather than servant leadership and incarnational unity, they embody inefficient patterns Jesus sought to change.
The Old Testament paradigm of centralized authority and cultural monotony may be suitable in certain situations. Nevertheless, modern leaders need to create a synthesized paradigm that includes Old Testament values, the lessons of Jesus, and Pauline paradigms. Otherwise, it will be problematic without the servant character and the vision of inclusion portrayed by Jesus. Similarly, Paul's system of shared authority would not be as effective without the incarnational basis Jesus established.
Integrating Theology to Develop Practical Solutions
The discussion indicates that Togolese churches, like all other churches worldwide, integrate the advantages of all three paradigms into the formulation of effective strategies to combat ecclesial divisions, while emphasizing the basic teachings of Jesus. The Old Testament represents leadership as stewardship to the people of God, with the Chief Shepherd in charge. All the leaders in any country are also advised to embrace the attitude of a servant of Christ, cultural sensitivity, and self-sacrifice as he did. This style focuses on leadership as a state of being and not as a state of doing, with the main traits of character, integrity, humility, and service being highlighted as defining how one acts and relates. It is the opposite of the conventional approaches that emphasize authority, self-interest, performance, program emphasis, and recognition.
Jesus emphasizes that authentic leadership lies in character, not outward behavior, which means a genuine leader serves and appreciates others to be truly great. He did that with surprise when washing the feet of his disciples (John 13:12-17), not in the role of honor and respect (based on the words like "Teacher" and "Lord"), but in the role of the lowest or the most disrespected in society. This is what this act encourages all disciples to do. It also observes important leadership values that reflect the act of Jesus, which forces His disciples to occupy lowly places in life, even in situations that humiliate them, such as love, humility, and patience (Matt. 23:11-12). The followers of Jesus, who taught leadership that was a symbol of integrity, where followers were encouraged to be the salt and light of the world (Matt. 5:13-16), where true power is a product of internal moral and spiritual rebirth. He presents a leadership paradigm (Matt. 5:3-12) of helping, being meek, seeking righteousness, being humble, being merciful, having a pure heart, and being a peacemaker, rather than being arrogant. By making every global ruler lead by such qualities, this creates a positive attitude of leadership, and their minds are directed to (take care v. 1), and they should be cautious of hypocrisy (Matt. 6:1-8). His approach encourages leading by example and living up to core values, a transformational style that extends beyond actions. These Pauline principles ensure a healthy church structure that promotes unity, practical ministry, and spiritual maturity.
Summary of Findings
The leadership that was redefined by Jesus through emphasis on unity, humility, and servanthood in His teaching on how to rule the church. Authentic church leadership must reflect His pattern of selfless service, spiritual integrity, repentance, love, and common cause. It should not be power struggles or divisions rooted in leadership or ethnicity, which many churches are experiencing today, including the Togolese Assemblies of God. This includes rejecting leadership motivated by ethnic identity, political loyalty, or hierarchical control. The points below describe the main theological aspects of Jesus' perception of unity and leadership within the church, in comparison with the Old Testament and Pauline models of church conflict resolution.
Jesus's Paradigm Versus the Old Testament Leadership Models
The Old Testament tends to emphasize leadership grounded in hierarchical power, from God to Moses, David, Solomon, and other kings. This depicts the powers and rigid hierarchy, which at times created divisions. The leaders usually exercised authority by their positions, and unity was based on obedience and respect. Conversely, Jesus stresses the real power of humble service and redefining the meaning of authority as service (Mark 10:43-44), service among and below followers. He urges people to love one another and have a common purpose to foster unity (John 17:20-23).
In line with this, some traditional elements are absent from Jesus's preaching about the Kingdom of God, including political-nationalist aspects, the reinstatement of the royal throne of David in its glory, and the driving out of the people's enemies from the land. However, these aspects constituted the flaming enthusiasm of these individuals (Ps. 17). Similarly, He does not supersede the Torah, but reinterprets it. He condemns the shape assumed by the principles of purity and Sabbath law, the objects of Jewish piousness. His conversion appeal is a matter of individual choice. By so doing, he divides people. Nevertheless, his death must be of benefit to "the multitude." His preaching is open to the entirety; by prophetic thought (Isa. 25:6), the coming of the peoples at the feast of the Kingdom with the Patriarchs is awaited (Matt. 8:11; Luke 13:28).
Jesus's Model Compared to Pauline Perspective
Building upon Jesus's foundation, Paul extends this vision to the early church. Christian unity as a foundation is also evident in the priestly prayer that Jesus gave (John 17), which forms the theological framework for the unity of the church based on Trinitarian fellowship: "that they may be one as we are one" (John 17:22-23). This harmony results from the ideal relationship between the Father and the Son, which provides a divine template for believers. Paul goes further to explain the Christ-centred vision saying that Christ is "the head of the body which is the church" (Col. 1:18) and that we who are many are one body in Christ (Rom. 12:5). The apostle makes it clear that this unity goes beyond ethnic, social, and cultural boundaries: "there is no longer any distinction between Jews and non-Jews […], all united with the Christ of Jesus, you are all one" (Gal. 3:28-29).
Although he acknowledges the privilege of worship in the people of Israel (Rom. 9:4), Paul does not refer much to the Temple because he refers to Christians as the sanctuary of God (1 Cor. 3:16-17; 2 Cor. 6:16). Jerusalem is predominantly the mother church, which proves the faithfulness of God who made a covenant. Comparative analysis of the teachings of Jesus and Paul on the unity of the Church and leadership reveals striking similarities in theological consistency and distinct focus that complement each other. This discussion examines their scriptural basis, ecclesiological implications, and applications. Paul stresses the significance of the apostolate (Gal. 1:1; 1 Cor. 9:1), which is based on the principles of universality and unity and transcends a particular local church. There is no denying that Paul is a connecting factor between different churches. Having a common Gospel brings believers together despite their congregations (1 Cor. 15:11). Fellowship is also enhanced among Christians through baptism, the Eucharist, and shared meals. Based on 1 Cor. 11:23ff, it is apparent that the form of the Eucharist in Corinth, Antioch, and Jerusalem is the same. 
Paul highlights that his attitude toward the apostolic authority, despite adopting the principles of servant leadership, forms a theology of apostolic authority grounded in a personal, direct divine call. According to Paul, his apostleship is "not from men nor through man, but through Jesus Christ and God the Father" (Gal. 1:1). Next, he bases his doctrinal authority on his teachings that contain the mark of the authority of Christ himself and must be considered as "Scripture" (2 Pet. 3:15-16). In addition to the service in authority, Paul confirms, "We do not preach ourselves, but Jesus Christ as Lord, nor ourselves as your servants in the name of Jesus" (2 Corinthians 4:5).
Additionally, according to Paul, the structure of the church must contribute to unity rather than obstruct it through ensuring that there is an appropriate government, organizing spiritual gifts, and conflict management in a well-organized apostolic rule in a communion (koinônia) among apostles (Gal. 2:9). It also fosters unity between various churches. This koinônia is founded on the attitude of a servant, which is demonstrated by creating positions such as elders and deacons (1 Tim. 3). He emphasizes the importance of being in power but serving unselfishly and healing divisions through teaching and healing relationships. Nevertheless, there are other areas of unity in Israel that are not critical. Although he acknowledges the holiness, justice, and goodness of the Torah (Rom. 7:12), Paul, in other writings, observes its exclusive and national orientation (cf. Gal. 2:14). He rejects circumcision and dietary laws as a means of enhancing unity among the Jewish people. Equally, the diversity of charisms serves as a mediator of unity, as demonstrated by the body metaphor (1 Cor. 12), where Paul encourages the people to be humble, serve, reconcile, and grow in the spirit. The body would not be able to function without different gifts. 
Paul observes that the factional conflicts that caused divisions in the Church of Corinth, as discussed in 1 Corinthians 1-3, were unlawful. On the contrary, in 1 Corinthians 12 and Romans 12, he notes the importance of diversity using the metaphor of a body and its parts. The Spirit gives gifts to people in accordance with His will (1 Cor. 12:4-6, 15-22; Rom. 14:2-3), and this divine diversity is supposed to eliminate chaos. Although unity is supported by the concept of God, Jesus Christ, and the Spirit, it is also supported by the concept of baptism (Gal. 3:27-28; Rom. 6:3-4), Eucharist (1 Cor. 10:16-17), faith (Rom. 1:16; 3:22), and agape (1 Cor. 13; Rom. 5:3-8).
Jesus emphasizes relational unity as the heart of church life, complementing Paul's structural and doctrinal approach (John 17:11). He introduces unity as the main aim of His mission, internal character as the basis of authentic leadership with such actions as foot-washing (John 13:12-17), unity of purpose, and love towards each other (John 13:35). These attributes overcome the division due to ambition or differences in doctrines. 
The lessons on leadership views indicate that the servant leadership model, as demonstrated by Jesus, redefines leadership by emphasizing a servant-first style. This change of values is clearly illustrated by the foot-washing episode (John 13:1-17). The first feature of this approach is its humility, which shows that the leader is a servant. Second, as stated in the Bible in greater love has no one than this that one gave his life to his friends (John 15:13). Third, being an example, according to the words, "For I have given you an example, that you should do as I have done to you" (John 13:15). This opinion can be summarized as follows: the Son of Man did not come to serve but to serve, and to give his life as a ransom to many (Mark 10:45).
The other important lesson is the unifying mission, which does not give the unity of the Church as a goal but as a tool to accomplish a missionary goal. The prayers of Jesus include, "that the world be convinced that you have sent me" (John 17:21), and the unity of the Jews and the Gentiles is the revealed "mystery" of the Kingdom of God being brought to every nation (Ephesians 3:4-6). This balanced model avoids authoritarian leadership that suppresses service, and it also prevents relativism that weakens doctrinal unity. The synthesis of Jesus's and Paul's views offers a model of ecclesial authority that avoids two main traps: authoritarianism, which neglects the servant dimension of leadership, and relativism, which weakens the unity provided by doctrinal authority. It also contributes to doctrinal coherence even in the context of cultural diversity, since Paul shows how to maintain the doctrinal fundamentals ("one Lord, one faith, one baptism," Eph. 4:5) and still permit the cultural and liturgical diversity, which is a key value in the modern Church in its intercultural dimension. Together, Jesus and Paul provide a unified blueprint for leadership that is Christ-centered, Spirit-empowered, and capable of sustaining unity amid diversity.
Hermeneutical Challenges and Practical Applications
The initial difficulty is the institutionalization one, caused by the expansion of church organizations and raising issues of fidelity to New Testament patterns. The pastoral epistles show a premature institutionalization, such as "the laying on of hands by the college of elders" (1 Timothy 4:14), a sign of alteration of forms without changing the fundamental principle of servant leadership. The second obstacle is the endeavor of modern ecumenism. The prayer of Jesus for unity (John 17) and Paul's emphasis on the unity of the body of Christ insist on the contemporary denominational division and demand a search for unity that would both hold to the truth of the doctrine and allow for diversity.
Together, Jesus provides the foundation, while Paul develops its organizational expression. The theological basis and paradigm of servant leadership, as laid down by Jesus, are elaborated by Paul, who constructs the ecclesiological and organizational consequences of this vision. 
Their synthesis gives the modern Church a solid theological basis, and the unity of the Church is grounded in Trinitarian truth rather than in human ideas alone. It balances both authority and service in leadership, protects key doctrines, and embraces cultural diversity. Also, it shifts the focus of unity to mission rather than self-preservation. This holistic outlook challenges the 21st-century Church to find its way back to authentic Christological leadership that places a vital emphasis on unity, through which it can testify to the world of the risen Christ.
Both perspectives underline the importance of the Holy Spirit in creating unity. Jesus also promises the Spirit of truth, who "will lead you into all truth" (John 16: 13). In the meantime, Paul educates that the same spirit baptized all believers into a single body (1 Cor. 12:13) and urges all believers to be zealous to uphold the oneness of the spirit in the unity of peace (Eph. 4:3). Diversity provides the ecclesiological unity since Jesus illustrates using his vine metaphor: "I am the vine, you are the branches" (John 15:5) and the importance of the connection that exists between the members. Paul develops this analogy of the body (1 Cor. 12:12). This practical diversity in a common ontological unity enables the Church to show the abundance of Godly grace.
Contributions to Theological Discourse
This book establishes servant leadership as a biblical criterion and demonstrates that Jesus's leadership is ontological. In an effort to shift toward sacrificial love, humility, inclusiveness, and service, church leaders must abandon authority-based models of leadership. The change essentially reinvents the definition of a leader, beyond their behaviors. It questions the conventional discourse, which centers primarily on leadership positions and forms by offering a being-oriented ecclesiology. Also, it redefines authority and power to emphasize that true church unity is based on character change rather than structural change. 
Paul shows that the factionalism within the Church of Corinth (1 Cor. 1-3) resulted from destructive disagreements that he considers illegitimate. In 1 Corinthians 12 and Romans 12, on the other hand, he emphasizes the importance of diversity, using the example of the body and its parts. The Spirit gives gifts to people based on his will (1 Cor. 12:4-6, 15-22; Rom. 14:2-3), and this diversity of God is intended to bring order. Although unity is supported by God, who is the Christ of Jesus and the Spirit, it is also supported by baptism (Gal. 3:27-28; Rom. 6:3-4) and the Eucharist (1 Cor. 10:16-17), faith (Rom. 1:16; 3:22), and agape (1 Cor. 13; Rom. 5:3-8). This piece of writing emphasizes the significance of leadership theory and provokes classical theological discourse that considers leadership positions and institutions the main issue. 
The book also forms a basis for unity in diversity grounded in the Old Testament, which demonstrates that God established the principle of unity in diversity from the outset. This unity was maintained by various unifying institutions, including elections (Abraham), covenants (berit, Moses), monarchy (based on loyalty and intended to bring justice), and priestly institutions (with the duty to maintain unity through Torah authority). Another concept of the Old Testament that addresses the assembly of the Lord (יְהוָֹה קָהָל, qahal YHWH) is found in Deuteronomy 23:1, which unites all tribes into a single community centered on divine presence. Moreover, the monarchies encouraged justice and fairness, and the priestly institutions promoted loyalty and unity through the authority of the Torah. Thus, the god of the Bible is shown as the one who created a multifaceted universe of creatures, families, nations, and peoples, which He deliberately created and regarded as 'good.' This book would therefore recommend servant leadership, grounded in humility, love, and inclusivity, as a biblical model for leadership of the global church and for organizational models.
The restoration of Israel is a testimony to universal receptivity, in which Jesus symbolizes His wish to bring together and renew His people through prophetic performances by the Twelve Apostles, who symbolized the Twelve tribes. This sight aligns with Jewish messianic expectations for the future age. The teachings of Jesus about the Kingdom of God are more inclusive and universal than traditional political and nationalist perspectives. His message is for everyone; just as Isaiah (Isa. 25:6) prophesied, Jesus looks forward to uniting all nations at the feast of the Kingdom with the patriarchs.
Typologically, the representation is the fulfillment of types and promises of the Old Testament. Theologically, as the apostles read the Old Testament, they could see allusions to Christ and his Kingdom. The Old Testament demonstrates Christ in many ways, including promises (e.g., Genesis 3:15), prophecies (e.g., Isaiah 7:14), and types or parallels (e.g., Noah, Jacob‘s ladder, the bronze serpent).
Pauline's perspective on the diversity of charisms highlights the elaborate theology of unity presented by Paul, particularly the metaphor of the Church as the body of Christ (1 Cor. 12:4-6). He discusses a cosmic Christology in the Deutero-Pauline letters (Colossians and Ephesians). It is worth noting that the notion of defining "Christ as the head of the Church, which is His body" (Eph. 4:3-6) is at the heart of the unity concept. Therefore, Paul's teachings are grounded in unity, centered on the uniqueness of the Gospel, the sacraments of baptism and the Eucharist, and faith, love, and apostleship. It is critical to mention that the Pauline idea of unity is not the same as uniformity since he points out that unity does not mean that all people are identical (1 Cor. 9:19-22). This view enables him to free his ethnically Christian believers of the traditions of the Jewish religion and still retain the much-needed unity in Christ.
This study indicates that the model Jesus proposed can be used to overcome existing bias in leadership and offer an alternative way to resolve leadership divisions based on region, ethnicity, and leadership. It shows that the leadership principles of Jesus can be directly applied to church practices, with a focus on Spirit-empowered leadership grounded in the servant-heartedness of Jesus and Pauline leadership. This approach encourages true harmony among various church traditions. In addition, it provides a biblical basis for conflict resolution and promotes harmony within Christian communities from a theological perspective. These three principles provide a viable framework to guide the training of churches in Africa, especially the TAGC, to create sustainable unity and spiritual vibrancy among leaders and congregations.
Nevertheless, this book identifies some of these gaps as the tendency of most studies to modernize leadership concepts by directly applying them to biblical works without taking into account their sociohistorical, cultural, and political background. Practically, theological arguments tend to follow idealized or abstract paradigms without sufficiently taking into account the relational dimension and the specific historical and cultural settings, such as the patriarchalism of the Old Testament or the socio-political environment of the first century. This makes them less important in the real-world practical situation, particularly in Africa or Togo. 
A pyramidal leadership structure is also the subject of many studies, often to the detriment of values such as service, humility, and equality championed by Jesus and Paul, or the misinterpretation of passages such as John 17 or 1 Corinthians 12 without the context of the passage, and may misrepresent their messages on unity and servant leadership. In many cases, such views are too abstract, as they do not relate leadership models to the principles of justice, compassion, and love on which the community service is based, particularly in the contemporary setting. Furthermore, numerous studies focus on leadership and unity models that apply to specific texts or eras and do not investigate how they relate to or remain consistent over the years, restricting a holistic perspective on biblical thinking. In some studies, hierarchical or individualistic models are preferred, ignoring the communal, relational, and participatory nature of Jesus's and Paul's teachings.
A pyramid or patriarchal leadership style is also highlighted in most studies, and the values of service, humility, and equality advocated by Jesus and Paul are usually overlooked or hidden. Some understand passages such as John 17 or 1 Corinthians 12 literally or without their context, and hence misinterpret their meanings of unity and servant leadership. The discussion is more likely to be theoretical, without sufficiently connecting these leadership models to the principles of justice, compassion, and love in the service to the community, especially in modern contexts.
Moreover, the superficial distinction between the Old Testament, the teachings of Jesus, and the Pauline writings suggests that these paradigms are distinct theological systems, which can lead to the presentation of Old Testament leadership as exclusively hierarchical and authoritarian, an issue that persists. From this limited perspective, we end up ignoring the delegation of authority (Exodus 18:13-27) in Moses's leadership as a participatory model, and consulting with the elders (1 Chronicles 13:1-4) in David's leadership as a consultative model. We can also overlook the female leadership of a judge (Deborah) working together (Judges 4-5) or the setting up of a decentralized judicial system by Samuel (1 Samuel 8:1-3). This same parochial approach creates a one-sided vision that ignores the variety of leadership models in the Old Testament.
To conclude, the three views come down to a vision of unity in diversity grounded in historical continuity, which believes that the New Testament Church has its foundations in the promises and institutions of the Old Testament. In addition, as per the Christological fulfillment, Jesus not only fulfills but also exceeds the Old Testament anticipations of unity. Lastly, according to the pneumatological realization, it is the Holy Spirit that makes this unity alive in the diversity of Christian communities. This ecclesial unity is therefore provided by God (in the ontological dimension), achieved in Christ (in the Christological dimension) and lived through the Spirit (in the pneumatological dimension), without prejudice to the primal unity of the people of God.
Practical Recommendations for Church Leaders
Based on Matthew 5:3-12; Galatians 5:22-23; 1 Timothy 3:1-7, this book proposes an individual leadership assessment or self-evaluation model that entails frequent reflection and auditing to determine whether his or her leadership is uniting or disintegrating. Every leader who wants to be effective in the call that God has given him will have to form an accountability partnership with mature leaders who are not part of their organization or the scope of their ministry. The TAGC should also introduce compulsory leadership development courses to emphasize servanthood, cultural humility, and incarnational ministry to pass on a healthy church to the next generation. In addition, they need to review their decision-making procedures in line with the collaborative approach based on these three paradigms and adopt the Matthean conflict resolution system (Matt. 18:15-17). Specific practical aids, such as a daily leadership journal of decisions and actions, can enable anonymous feedback from the board, congregation, or co-leaders. They are also able to offer spiritual consultation with wise leaders who focus on character development or evaluate existing guidance patterns, secular and ecclesial, on the model of Jesus, to offer a healthy structure. In practice, they have to begin every local, regional, and national leadership gathering with a prayer of unity, make space for vulnerability or errors, and labor harmoniously to address the practical needs of the church. Beyond structural reforms, cultural reconciliation must also be addressed.
The TAGC can hold seminars and pastoral training on cultural reconciliation, using the incarnational approach adopted by Jesus. Such an approach prompts leaders to display genuine cultural awareness and reverence for diversity. They can establish a cross-cultural ministry that aligns with the Pentecostal principle of breaking down ethnic barriers and incorporates comparative biblical leadership studies into the curriculum to enhance incarnational sensitivity. Leaders ought to mentor others (2 Tim. 2:2) and develop biblically based norms which emphasize character over charisma and service over ambitions. With deliberate teaching on Christian unity, its members must understand how to distribute spiritual gifts and not rely too heavily on leaders.
Emphasize raising Togolese leaders grounded in cultural knowledge and biblical values, while avoiding foreign models of leadership. This approach will enable leaders to be closer to local social contexts and to create ways to honor the values of Togolese culture without violating biblical integrity. Moreover, existing TAGC leaders ought to encourage church-based economic empowerment programs that address the socioeconomic challenges that contribute to leadership competition, just as Jesus was interested in the marginalized. This is intended to reduce the role of economic advantage in choosing leaders, so that those with material possessions do not manipulate those with spiritual maturity.  
The leaders of the world must change their autocratic leadership styles to collaborative ones, thereby providing a platform where all heads of various ministries are given equal voice, and through open discussions, consensus is achieved rather than orders being issued. They must make decisions based on the church and the biblical teachings. Moreover, they should develop a conflict resolution strategy (Matt. 18:15) that would help them move toward unity, which would involve privately discussing problems with the parties involved, may require the assistance of neutral senior leaders, and would involve stepping through the steps to hold each other accountable. This is the mediation procedure (Matt. 18:16-17) that would include mature leaders outside the conflict situation and would be used to reestablish the relationship, not just to resolve the conflict.
Local, regional, and national leaders are supposed to be the first to set an example by following the way of Jesus (John 13:14-15), putting the needs of others (both spiritual and physical) before their own interests. Leaders should promote open communication, forgiveness, and teamwork (Eph. 4:3), delegate decision-making and responsibilities to foster the spirit of unity by building relationships and reconciling. To the church in the present day, character and integrity development is a matter of investing in personal spiritual development, as genuine leadership is a product of inner change (Gal. 5:22-23). In addition, the appreciation and acknowledgment of various gifts and roles in the body (1 Cor. 12) and appreciation of every member would foster unity in the face of diversity.
Trust in the Holy Spirit to provide wisdom, vision, gifts, discernment, guidance, and empowerment (Acts 1:8) would promote leadership by the Holy Spirit and the community's growth. Additionally, the current leaders are expected to mentor, train, and delegate roles to support the future development of leaders. In general, the emphasis on the biblical model of mutual edification and collaborative governance will solve the conflict biblically and create a culture of transparency.
To sum up, the biblical servant leadership of church leaders, the development of genuine relationships, the maintenance of biblical foundations of unity, and the reliance on the Holy Spirit should help create a healthy, unified, and spiritually alive community. In essence, all these are aimed at fostering a biblically rooted, practically theological paradigm that upraises servant leadership and inner spirituality. The trick lies in the practice throughout the years, God-given vocation rather than manipulation, character building rather than quick solutions, and seeking harmony rather than self-seeking. The final aim is not only to resolve conflicts or avoid them, but also to narrow ethnic differences, to focus on spiritual change within the existing churches, or to create new ones with the character of Christ and the promotion of his Kingdom, with the help of visible unity and sacrificial and servant love.
Conclusion
At the heart of this book stands a simple but powerful truth: the unity of the Church cannot be separated from the way the Church is led. Where leadership becomes a struggle for authority, unity begins to fracture. Where unity is treated as optional, leadership becomes vulnerable to pride, division, and ethnic tensions. Yet when leadership is shaped by Christ, the Church becomes what it was always meant to be: a spiritual family marked by humility, love, and shared mission.
This study has shown that Jesus’ teachings on church unity and leadership are not built on hierarchy or human dominance. Instead, they are grounded in two defining foundations: theocentricity and servanthood. Everything Jesus teaches about leadership begins with God, and everything Jesus models in leadership ends in service. Greatness is no longer measured by position, recognition, or control. In the Kingdom of God, greatness is revealed through humility, sacrifice, and love. Leadership is not about climbing upward. It is about bending downward.
When placed beside Old Testament authority structures and the leadership guidance found in Pauline writings, Jesus’ leadership vision stands out with unmistakable clarity. The Old Testament provides important patterns of legitimate authority, order, and responsibility. Leaders often functioned as representatives of divine governance, ensuring that covenant faithfulness and communal stability were protected. In these structures, authority was central, and leadership carried a strong public and institutional character.
Yet Christ introduces a decisive shift. Jesus does not abolish leadership. He redefines it. Rather than leading through dominance, He leads through self-giving. Rather than securing obedience through force, He transforms hearts through incarnational love. Jesus presents leadership centered on inward transformation, marked by self-emptying humility, mutual identity, shared purpose, sacrificial love, and relational harmony.
In this way, Jesus becomes the bridge between earlier biblical paradigms and the later apostolic development of servant leadership. The Pauline writings, while providing structure for order and governance, consistently preserve the spirit of Christ’s model: humility, spiritual empowerment, unity, and mutual acceptance. Paul emphasizes that true leadership is empowered by the Spirit, shaped by charisms, and anchored in inner transformation rather than external control. He calls believers to honor one another, submit to one another, and pursue unity through love. His goal is not organizational efficiency alone, but spiritual maturity and relational strength.
Together, these perspectives reveal the movement of biblical leadership from outward conformity and authority toward its highest expression revealed in Christ. Servant leadership becomes the measure of spiritual greatness. Paul does not replace Jesus’ model. He provides apostolic frameworks for implementing it within real communities. The New Testament vision is not disorder, but neither is it authoritarianism. It is order shaped by love. Authority shaped by humility. Leadership shaped by service.
This conclusion matters because churches today, including the Assemblies of God churches in Togo, often experience divisions similar to those Paul addressed in Corinth: power struggles, factionalism, personality-driven influence, and ethnic misunderstandings. This study has shown that even the application of conflict management strategies does not guarantee long-term cohesiveness, genuine spiritual transformation, or sustained unity. Conflict tools can reduce tension, but they cannot replace the inner work of the Holy Spirit. Methods may manage disagreement, but they cannot create Christlike hearts.
The Church does not need authoritarian leadership to grow. It does not need domination to remain stable. It does not need human control to stay aligned with its mission. What the Church needs is Christ-centered leadership that reflects moral integrity, holiness, humility, selflessness, spiritual unity, and service. This kind of leadership builds sustainable unity.
The vision offered throughout this book is therefore not merely theoretical. These biblical paradigms are practical and applicable. They provide a pathway for healing the wounds created by leadership tension and ethnic separation. They also offer concrete direction for building healthier church governance, including stronger leadership training, renewed emphasis on service, and intentional unity in diversity. Where leadership becomes character-based rather than position-based, unity becomes stronger. Where spiritual maturity replaces competition, relationships are restored. Where culture is acknowledged but transformed by biblical truth, theological integrity is preserved and spiritual renewal becomes possible.
Ultimately, this book has argued that ecclesial unity is not simply a desirable goal. It is a theological necessity. Unity is grounded in God Himself and revealed through the incarnation of Christ. The Church fails in its witness when it reflects societal division more than it reflects the love of Christ. The credibility of the Church’s mission depends upon the reality of its unity.
For the Assemblies of God in Togo, and for churches everywhere, sustainability and missionary effectiveness depend greatly on one central question: will the Church return fully to the model of Christ? Unity in diversity is not achieved by policies, councils, or elections alone. It is achieved when leaders and believers embrace Christ’s way, serving the Kingdom instead of personal interests and submitting to God rather than to the spirit of control.
Jesus’ definition of leadership remains one of the most challenging and liberating truths in Scripture. Real power in God’s Kingdom is not measured by domination, but by life given. It is not measured by titles acquired, but by feet washed. This is the path of reconciliation, the doorway to healing, and the foundation of genuine unity.
While this work offers theological and practical insight, it also opens doors for future study. There is need for deeper exploration of pneumatological leadership, especially the role of the Holy Spirit in shaping servant leaders and sustaining unity. Further book may examine Trinitarian ecclesiology and what the mutual indwelling within the Trinity reveals about collaborative authority and relational governance in the Church. Studies may also focus more intentionally on cultural and regional dynamics in Togo, analyzing how traditions shape perceptions of leadership and how biblical models can be adapted responsibly to promote reconciliation and unity.
Empirical research can also strengthen understanding of how authoritative and servant leadership styles affect ecclesial unity in Togolese contexts, including patterns visible in elections and governance. Such work would help translate biblical principles into culturally meaningful and sustainable approaches.
Yet above all, the conclusion of this book is not merely informational. It is spiritual and urgent.
Leadership divisions emerge most visibly where adherence to the Christ model is weakened. Therefore, the Church is called to renewal where servanthood has been abandoned and repentance where pride has shaped leadership. The message of Jesus confronts every leader and every believer with one invitation: return to Christ, return to love, and return to unity. Where Christ is honored, unity becomes possible. Where love becomes the standard, healing becomes inevitable. Where servanthood becomes the culture, the Church becomes spiritually alive.
This is the enduring call of Christ to His Church in Togo and beyond: to lead by serving, to unite by loving, and to grow through surrender to God.

Appendix D 2: Togo Map After Being Divided
      	
The Berlin conference and its division of Togo
1) Le Togo occidental (ou Togoland britannique ou British Togo) fut adjugé au Royaume-Uni. [Western Togo (or British Togoland or British Togo) was awarded to the United Kingdom.]
2) Le Togo oriental (ou Togoland français) fut alloué à la France, qui devint par la suite le Togo. [Eastern Togo (or French Togoland) was allocated to France, which later became Togo.]
 
Appendix E: Togo Map Before Colonizers Divided It
   
It was not until 1890 that the "Ewe country" was divided between the Germans and the British. From then on, the western Ewe were attached to the English colony of the Gold Coast (Today’s Ghana), while the eastern Ewe became citizens of German Togoland. The first missionary posts, founded at Peki and Keta, were integrated into the Gold Coast, while the others were integrated into Togoland (present-day Togo).  After the conflict in 1919, the League of Nations assigned two separate mandates: 1) Western Togo (also known as British Togoland or British Togo) was given to the United Kingdom, and 2) Eastern Togo (also called French Togoland) was allocated to France, which later became the country of Togo. French Togo was granted an area of 56,600 km², while British Togo covered an area of 33,800 km². It can be said that Togo is the only country in Africa that has experienced German colonization, British rule, and finally, a French mandate. From that point on, the western Togolese were part of the British colony, while the eastern Togolese became French subjects. 
 
Appendix F: Togo Map Showing Two Major Regions
 
Zone 1 (Sud): première langue, l’éwé; seconde langue, le tem; [Zone 1 (South): first language, Ewe; second language, Tem.]
Zone 2 (Nord): première langue, le tem; seconde langue, l’éwé. [Zone 2 (North): first language, Tem; second language, Ewe.]

Appendix H: First Head of State of Togo (1956-1957)
 
Nicolas Grunitzky




Appendix I: Second Head of State (1958-1967)
 
https://africanlisbontour.com/2020/04/27/independence-day-togo/ 
	Then emerged Sylvanus Olympio, a Lome citizen whose primary concern was to unite the Ewe People. The resident of the French part of the country was the leader of the Committee of Togolese Unity after World War II and was elected president of the first territorial assembly in 1946. A year later, Olympio, who had a Togolese and British education, began to have issues with the Togoland French colonial administration. With this and his desire to unite Ewes, Olympio joined the Comité de l’Unité (CUT), an organization dedicated to Ewe reunification. The organization also opposed closer ties between Togo and the French Empire. As president of the Togo Assembly after 1946 and later a deputy to the French National Assembly, he appeared several times before the United Nations, emphasizing the need for Ewe unification. However, his goal to unite the Ewes did not materialize when, in 1956, the British Togoland voted by plebiscite to join the Gold Coast, which later became independent Ghana.
In 1956, French Togoland gained limited autonomy. It then held elections, which the ‘Parti Togolais du Progrès’ won, and Olympio’s rival, Nicholas Grunitzky, became prime minister. However, Olympio and the CUT protested the election to the United Nations until another election, supervised by the UN, was held in 1958. Olympio and his CUT won the election, and he became Prime Minister. Togo gained independence on April 27, 1960. A year later, it became a republic with Olympio as its president. Due to dissatisfaction with some of his policies and his pro-French stance, Olympio was assassinated during a coup on January 13, 1963.

LA VOIX DE LA SENTINELLE  DANS LE DESERT

Je bâtirai mon Église et les portes du séjour des morts ne prévaudront point contre elle. Mathieu 16 :18.
AUX SERVITEURS DE DIEU(PASTEURS) DU SUD
OBJECTIF : L’HEURE A SONNE POUR LE REGNE DES PASTEURS DU SUD
Cela fait presque un siècle que l’Église des Assemblées de Dieu a vu le jour au Togo. Durant tout ce temps, elle n’a que deux présidents : le Rév PASGO Billa et le Rév DJAKOUTI Mitré qui ont monopolisé le pouvoir au Nord.
Depuis 1984, le pasteur Mitré seul totalise (08) huit mandats de quatre ans successifs, empêchant ainsi les autres de prendre la relève. Ainsi en 2013 à la fin du 6ème mandat, il lui fallait toilaiter les textes en remontant la retraite de 65 ans  à 70 ans. Il fallait que l’homme meure avant d’aller en retraite ?  Maintenant qu’il a 75 ans jour pour jour selon une source proche du BA ; qu’il dépasse 5 ans les 70 ans, va-t-il encore toilaiter les textes pour ramener l’âge de la retraite à 80 ans ? Nous allons le voir.  Vous les gens du nord, pourquoi vous aimez tant le pouvoir ainsi ? C’est la cupidité à haut débit.  Alors qu’il signe la retraite de ses sujets (pasteurs) à moins de l’échéance ou à la date fixée. C’est la pure injustice, alors que Dieu n’aime pas l’injustice.
NB : Nous savons que dans les assemblées de Dieu, c’est l’adultère seul qui mène dans l’enfer, alors que l’Église est remplie de pasteurs qui sont des brigands, des voleurs, des sorciers, assassins, des cupides, des méchants et voire des injustes. Alors que votre président pratiquait publiquement de la cupidité et de l’injustice à grande échelle. 1 Corinthiens 6 :9.
Au Bénin il y a assez d‘anciens présidents des A/D qui jouissent bien de leur retraite. Alors qu’au Togo, c’est mourir au pouvoir.  
NB : Comment voulons-nous que le monde païen change ? Nous sommes dominés et assujetts  par les Kabiyès pendant bientôt 70 ans par une seule famille et dans l’Église aussi bientôt 100 ans par deux familles, pourquoi ? Alors qu’il y a à côté de nous des Églises sœurs Pentecôtistes, Évangéliques et Baptistes qui changent leurs dirigeants suivant deux mandats. Et ça donne de la crédibilité  et de l’honneur à leurs  Églises.
 Tous les pasteurs kabiyès et certains sudistes mal intentionnés encouragent le pasteur Mitré à aller dans son injustice.
C’est pourquoi, pasteurs du sud du Togo, réveillez-vous et laissez les petites rancunes entre vous, pardonnez-vous réciproquement quel qu’en soit le degré  de la faute.
Saisissez cette opportunité que le seigneur vous donne en vous livrant le pouvoir de régner sur un plateau d’or.
Si un candidat du Nord (soit Moba, Kabiyè, Lamba, Kotokoli ou Bassar) s’est positionné à un poste électif avec un pasteur du sud, unissez vos voix derrière votre frère du sud pour qu’il sorte vainqueur. Ne laissez aucune chance aux Nordistes, car ils ont déjà eu leur part pendant 86 ans de l’existence de cette Église.
NB : Malgré la vaste campagne qu’a effectuée le pasteur SARAM et ses frères avec son fameuse ONG ABEP sur toute l’étendue du territoire à plusieurs reprises, se montrant aux pasteurs qu’il est le meilleur candidat à choisir pour diriger l’Église après le pasteur Mitré.
Non, il ne réussira pas son projet.  
NB : Regardez vos frères des A/D du Bénin et vos consœurs de l’Église pentecôtiste du Togo, ils ont pris leur courage à deux mains et changé l’histoire de leur Église.
C’est pourquoi du Bureau Exécutif jusqu’aux responsables des départements, ayez votre courage pour changer l’histoire de notre Église pour que le Togo change. 
NB : Vous savez que les frères du Nord ne vont jamais voter pour un candidat du sud au détriment d’un candidat du nord, jamais .C’est pourquoi unissez vos voix derrière votre frère du sud, même s’il y a quelques choses parmi vous, pardonnez-vous, nous vous le répétons.
NB : Prenez soin de respecter la constitution des A/D et de respecter l’âge d’aller à la retraite de 65 ans. Nous vous remercions.
« Que celui qui a des oreilles entende ce que l’Esprit dit aux Eglises » APOC 2 :29
NB : N’oubliez pas notre message que nous vous avons transmis de la part du seigneur Février 2012, relisez-le car elle s’accomplira totalement à court sûr.
Fait le 02 Juillet 2022
LA SENTINELLE
 Anonymous (Letter English Version)
THE VOICE OF THE SENTINEL IN THE DESERT
EZECHIEL 33: 1-20; LUC 3: 1-17
MESSAGE FROM THE LORD TO HIS CHURCH OF THE ASSEMBLIES OF GOD IN TOGO
I will build my Church, and the gates of Hades shall not prevail against it. Matthew 16:18.
TO THE SERVANTS OF GOD(PASTORS) IN THE SOUTH
OBJECTIVE: THE TIME HAS COME FOR SOUTHERN PASTORS TO REIGN
It has been nearly a century since the Assemblies of God Church was founded in Togo. Since its founding, it has been led by only two presidents: Rev PASGO Billa and Rev DJAKOUTI Mitré, both of whom are from the North.
Since 1984, Pastor Mitré has served eight consecutive four-year terms, preventing others from taking over. This long leadership has not only hindered the growth of the Church but also raised concerns about potential abuse of power. It is time we address this issue and change leadership. In 2013, at the end of his sixth term, he had to modify the rules by raising the retirement age from 65 to 70. Did he have to wait to die before retiring? Now that he is 75, according to a source close to the Board of Administration, and five years past the age of 70, will he again change the rules to extend the retirement age to 80? Let us wait and see. Why do people from the north love power so much? It is driven by greed. While he signs the retirement papers of his pastors before the deadline or on the set date, this is pure injustice, and God does not love injustice. 
Note: We know that within the Assemblies of God, only adultery leads to hell, but the Church is full of pastors who are bandits, thieves, witches, murderers, greedy, wicked, and even unjust. While your president was openly practicing greed and injustice on a large scale. 1 Corinthians 6:9.
In Benin, many former A/D presidents are enjoying their retirement. Nevertheless, in Togo, it is all about dying in power.  
Note: How do we envision the pagan world changing? We have been dominated and subjugated by the Kabiyiè, a tribe from northern Togo, for almost 70 years by a single family and in the Church for nearly 100 years by two families. Why? Meanwhile, next door, we have the sister Pentecostal, Evangelical, and Baptist churches, which change their leaders every two terms; moreover, this gives credibility and honor to their churches.
 All the Kabyiè pastors and some ill-intentioned Southerners are encouraging Pastor Mitré to continue his injustice.
That is why all pastors in southern Togo need to wake up and put their petty grudges behind them. Forgive each other, regardless of the severity of the fault or offense.
Seize the opportunity the Lord provides by presenting the power to rule on a golden platter.
If a candidate from the North (either Moba, Kabiyiè, Lamba, Kotokoli, or Bassar) has positioned himself for an elective post with a pastor from the South, unite your votes behind your brother from the South so that he emerges victorious. Do not give the northerners a chance because they have already had their share in the 86 years of this church‘s existence.
Note: Despite the extensive campaign that Pastor SARAM [Understand that Pastor SARAM is from the North and serves as the Vice-General Secretary of the Church] and his brothers have conducted with his well-known NGO ABEP across the country on multiple occasions, demonstrating to pastors that he is the best candidate to lead the Church after Pastor Mitré.
No, he will not succeed in his project.  
Note: Look at your brothers from the A/G in Benin and your sisters from the Pentecostal Church in Togo; they have taken their courage in both hands and changed the history of their Church.
That is why all Southern pastors must have the courage to change the history of our Church so that Togo can change by voting for their brothers for the Executive Board and all department heads. 
Note: You know that the brothers from the North will never vote for a candidate from the South over a candidate from the North, never. That is why you should unite your votes behind your brother from the South; even if there are some disagreements among you, you must forgive one another, we repeat.
Note: Please follow the A/G constitution and retire at age 65. Thank you very much.
"He who has an ear, let him hear what the Spirit is saying to the churches" Rev. 2:29.
Note: Remember the message we sent you from the Lord in February 2012. Reread it, as it will be fully fulfilled in due time.
July 02, 2022

THE
		

Appendix M: Statistics Survey of the TAGC 
NAISSANCE DE L’EGLISE DES ASSEMBLEES DE DIEU DU TOGO
L’Église des Assemblées de Dieu du Togo a vu le jour en 1936 à Dapaong.
PREMIERS PIONNIERS
•	Le premier missionnaire à arriver au Togo : Missionnaire Arnold C. Weston. 
•	Nizemba Dentoumba est le premier pionnier. Il sera suivi de :
•	TENGA Sapaga : pionnier
•	SOUKOUBA (ou SOBABA) Kéré : pionnier
NOMBRE DES PASTEURS
Pasteurs en fonction	Pasteurs en repos médical	Pasteurs retraités	Total
1527	06	35	1568


INSTITUT BIBLIQUE ET THEOLOGIQUE DE SADA
L’Institut Biblique et Théologique de Sada (IBT Sada) anciennement appelé Institut Biblique des Assemblées de Dieu (IBAD) a vu le en 1976 
INSTITUT BIBLIQUE ET THEOLOGIQUE DE SAGBIEBOU
Le deuxième Institut Biblique, celui situé au Nord et précisément à Sagbiébou (IBT Sagbiébou), a ouvert ses portes le 13 septembre 2008.
NOMBRE DE MISIONNAIRES ENVOYES A L’ETRANGER
Les missionnaires partis du Togo pour les pays étrangers s’élèvent à 14 y compris les pasteurs KAMASSE, KOUGLENOU. 7 sont revenus au Togo.
NOMBRE DE PASTEURS ORDONNES AVANT 2015
Le nombre de pasteurs ayant reçu leur ordination avant 2014, donc l’ordination est vieille d’au moins 10 ans, s’élève à 282. (Si vous avez besoin de la liste, je vous l’envoie.)
ŒUVRES SOCIALES
Toutes les œuvres sociales se regroupent au sein de l’ONG ODOS (Office de Développement et des Œuvres Sociales) et comprend :
1 – Écoles.
Jardins d’enfants	Ecoles primaires	 Collèges	Lycées
25	37	16	02

2- Centre de santé
Le nombre de Centre de Santé s’élève à 04 : Centre de santé de Kamé, centre médico-social Jérusalem d’Agbalépédogan, centre médico-social Eben-Ezer de Djidjolé et le centre médico-social Shalom du Temple du Calvaire situé à Agoè Anokui.
NB : Le Centre de Santé de Nadoba n’est plus fonctionnel. Un Centre de Santé est en construction par la JAD Togo à Tsévié mais n’est encore achevé. 
 
3 – Les ONG.  
•	ODIH (Organisation pour le développement Intégral de l’homme)
•	PMU InterLife
•	ABEP
•	SERFAH (Service de Formation, de réhabilitation des aveugles et autres handicapés).
•	COOPEC-A/D 
Elle a vu le jour le 11 juin 1994 et compte aujourd’hui 33 agences. 
BIRTH OF THE ASSEMBLIES OF GOD CHURCH IN TOGO
The Assemblies of God Church in Togo was founded in 1936 in Dapaong.
FIRST PIONEERS
•	The first missionary to arrive in Togo: Missionary Arnold C. Weston. 
•	Nizemba Dentoumba was the first pioneer. He was followed by:
•	TENGA Sapaga: pioneer
•	SOUKOUBA (or SOBABA) Kéré: pioneer
NUMBER OF PASTORS
Pastors in office	Pastors on medical leave	Retired pastors	Total
1527	06	35	1568

SADA BIBLICAL AND THEOLOGICAL INSTITUTE
The Sada Biblical and Theological Institute (IBT Sada), formerly known as the Assemblies of God Biblical Institute (IBAD), was founded in 1976.
SAGBIEBOU BIBLICAL AND THEOLOGICAL INSTITUTE
The second Bible Institute, located in the north, specifically in Sagbiébou (IBT Sagbiébou), opened its doors on September 13, 2008.
NUMBER OF MISSIONARIES SENT ABROAD
Fourteen missionaries have left Togo for foreign countries, including Pastors Sidibe N. KAMASSE and Kokou KOUGLENOU in the USA. Seven have returned to Togo.
NUMBER OF PASTORS ORDAINED BEFORE 2015
The number of pastors ordained before 2014, whose ordination is at least 10 years old, is 282. (If you need the list, I can send it to you.)
SOCIAL WORKS
All social works are grouped within the NGO ODOS (Office of Development and Social Works) and include:
1 – Schools.
Kindergartens	Elementary schools	 Middle schools	High schools
25	37	16	02
2- Health centers
There are four health centers: the Kamé Health Center, the Jérusalem Medical-Social Center in Agbalépédogan, the Eben-Ezer Medical-Social Center in Djidjolé, and the Shalom Medical-Social Center at the Temple du Calvaire in Agoè Anokui.
NB: The Nadoba Health Center is no longer operational. A health center is being built by JAD Togo [Youth Department of Togo] in Tsévié, but it is not yet complete. 
3 – NGOs.
- ODIH (Organization for Integral Human Development)
-    PMU InterLife
-    ABEP
-    SERFAH (Training and Rehabilitation Service for the Blind and Other Disabled People).
-    COOPEC-A/D [Assemblies of God Financial Institution] 
It was founded on June 11, 1994, by Pastors Koffi Dossou, Sidibe-Newende Kamasse, and brothers Komlan Aziawa and Koami Deku, and now has 33 branches nationwide.
